Climène was eyeing him with expectant mockery, savouring in advance his humiliation; Leandre regarded him in consternation, whilst behind the scenes, M. Binet was dancing in fury.
“Name of a name,” he groaned to the rather scared members of the company assembled there, “what will happen when they discover that he isn’t acting?”
But they never did discover it. Scaramouche’s bewildered paralysis lasted but a few seconds. He realized that he was being laughed at, and remembered that his Scaramouche was a creature to be laughed with, and not at. He must save the situation; twist it to his own advantage as best he could. And now his real bewilderment and terror was succeeded by acted bewilderment and terror far more marked, but not quite so funny. He contrived to make it clearly appear that his terror was of someone off the stage. He took cover behind a painted shrub, and thence, the laughter at last beginning to subside, he addressed himself to Climène and Leandre.
“Forgive me, beautiful lady, if the abrupt manner of my entrance startled you. The truth is that I have never been the same since that last affair of mine with Almaviva. My heart is not what it used to be. Down there at the end of the lane I came face to face with an elderly gentleman carrying a heavy cudgel, and the horrible thought entered my mind that it might be your father, and that our little stratagem to get you safely married might already have been betrayed to him. I think it was the cudgel put such notion in my head. Not that I am afraid. I am not really afraid of anything. But I could not help reflecting that, if it should really have been your father, and he had broken my head with his cudgel, your hopes would have perished with me. For without me, what should you have done, my poor children?”
A ripple of laughter from the audience had been steadily enheartening him, and helping him to recover his natural impudence. It was clear they found him comical. They were to find him far more comical than ever he had intended, and this was largely due to a fortuitous circumstance upon which he had insufficiently reckoned. The fear of recognition by someone from Gavrillac or Rennes had been strong upon him. His face was sufficiently made up to baffle recognition; but there remained his voice. To dissemble this he had availed himself of the fact that Figaro was a Spaniard.

===Chinese===

克莱门用等着看笑话的眼神望着他，预先砸摸着他的出丑；莱安德罗惊惶地打量他；侧幕后，比内先生气得直蹦。
「名堂啊名堂，」他对聚在那儿、有点吓坏了的团员们呻吟道，「等他们发现他不是在演戏，可怎么好？」
可他们始终没发现。斯卡拉穆乔那阵发懵的僵立只持续了几秒钟。他意识到人家在笑他，记起他的斯卡拉穆乔是个该让人同他一起笑、而不是笑他的角色。他必须救场，把它扭成对自己最有利的局面。于是他真真切切的懵和怕，立刻让位给演出来的懵和怕——夸张得多，却没那么可笑。他设法叫人一眼看明白：他怕的是台下的什么人。他躲到一丛画出来的灌木后头，等笑声终于开始平息，才从那里向克莱门和莱安德罗开口。
「美丽的夫人，恕我冒失闯入，惊了您。实情是，自打上回同阿尔马维瓦那桩事之后，我就没复原过。我这颗心不比从前了。刚才在小巷尽头，我迎面撞见一位老先生，提着一根沉重的木棍，一个可怕的念头钻进我心里：那莫非是令尊？咱们骗您安安稳稳出嫁的小计策，莫非已经败露到他耳朵里？我想正是那根木棍把念头塞进我脑袋的。倒不是我怕。我真的什么都不怕。可我不由得想：那倘若真是令尊，一棍打破了我的头，您的指望岂不随我一道完蛋？因为没有我，你们可怎么办呢，我可怜的孩子们？」
观众的笑声一阵一阵给他壮胆，帮他找回他天生的厚脸皮。他们显然觉得他滑稽。他们还会觉得他远比他自己打算的更滑稽——这多半得归功于一件他没充分算到的巧事。他一直深怕被加夫里雅克或雷恩的什么人认出来。他的脸化装得足以叫人认不出；可还有嗓音。为了掩饰嗓音，他利用了费加罗是西班牙人这一点。

===Original===

He had known a Spaniard at Louis le Grand who spoke a fluent but most extraordinary French, with a grotesque excess of sibilant sounds. It was an accent that he had often imitated, as youths will imitate characteristics that excite their mirth. Opportunely he had bethought him of that Spanish student, and it was upon his speech that tonight he modelled his own. The audience of Guichen found it as laughable on his lips as he and his fellows had found it formerly on the lips of that derided Spaniard.
Meanwhile, behind the scenes, Binet﻿—listening to that glib impromptu of which the scenario gave no indication﻿—had recovered from his fears.
“*Dieu de Dieu!*” he whispered, grinning. “Did he do it, then, on purpose?”
It seemed to him impossible that a man who had been so terror-stricken as he had fancied André-Louis, could have recovered his wits so quickly and completely. Yet the doubt remained.
To resolve it after the curtain had fallen upon a first act that had gone with a verve unrivalled until this hour in the annals of the company, borne almost entirely upon the slim shoulders of the new Scaramouche, M. Binet bluntly questioned him.
They were standing in the space that did duty as greenroom, the company all assembled there, showering congratulations upon their new recruit. Scaramouche, a little exalted at the moment by his success, however trivial he might consider it tomorrow, took then a full revenge upon Climène for the malicious satisfaction with which she had regarded his momentary blank terror.
“I do not wonder that you ask,” said he. “Faith, I should have warned you that I intended to do my best from the start to put the audience in a good humour with me. Mademoiselle very nearly ruined everything by refusing to reflect any of my terror. She was not even startled. Another time, mademoiselle, I shall give you full warning of my every intention.”
She crimsoned under her greasepaint. But before she could find an answer of sufficient venom, her father was rating her soundly for her stupidity﻿—the more soundly because himself he had been deceived by Scaramouche’s supreme acting.
Scaramouche’s success in the first act was more than confirmed as the performance proceeded. Completely master of himself by now, and stimulated as only success can stimulate, he warmed to his work.

===Chinese===

他当年在路易大帝中学认识一个西班牙人，那人说一口流利却无比古怪的法语，嘶嘶的噝音多得滑稽。那口腔他常常模仿——年轻人见了逗乐的特点总要学。凑巧他想起了那个西班牙同学，今晚他就照着那人的腔调说话。吉尚的观众听他说来，同他和同伴们当年听那位被取笑的西班牙人说来一样好笑。
这时侧幕后，比内先生听着那套脚本里毫无预示的流利即兴——已从惊惶里缓过来了。
「我的天哪！」他咧着嘴低语，「莫非他是故意的？」
在他想来，一个像他以为安德烈-路易那样吓得魂不附体的人，绝不可能这么快、这么彻底地回过神来。可疑心仍在。
为了解开它，第一幕落幕后——这一幕那份劲头，是剧团史册上至今未有的，几乎全压在新斯卡拉穆乔瘦削的肩上——比内先生直截了当问了他。
他们站在权当后台休息室的那块地方，全团人聚在那儿，正纷纷向新伙伴道贺。斯卡拉穆乔此刻被成功微微托着——不论他明天会觉得这成功多么微不足道——当场对克莱门报了一箭之仇，为她方才眼看他一时僵住出丑时那股恶意的快意。
「我不怪您问，」他说，「真的，我本该先打招呼：我打头就存心尽力叫观众对我有好感。小姐差一点坏了事——她一点也不接我的怕。她连吃惊都没有。下回，小姐，我的每一个打算都提前向您请示。」
她油彩底下红了脸。可她还没想出够毒的话来回敬，她父亲已经为她的愚蠢痛骂起她来——骂得格外痛，因为他自己也被斯卡拉穆乔高超的演技骗过了。
随着演出进行，斯卡拉穆乔第一幕的成功不但保持，而且愈演愈盛。此刻他完全成了自己的主人，又受了唯有成功才能给人的那种刺激，越演越热。

===Original===

Impudent, alert, sly, graceful, he incarnated the very ideal of Scaramouche, and he helped out his own native wit by many a remembered line from Beaumarchais, thereby persuading the better informed among the audience that here indeed was something of the real Figaro, and bringing them, as it were, into touch with the great world of the capital.
When at last the curtain fell for the last time, it was Scaramouche who shared with Climène the honours of the evening, his name that was coupled with hers in the calls that summoned them before the curtains.
As they stepped back, and the curtains screened them again from the departing audience, M. Binet approached them, rubbing his fat hands softly together. This runagate young lawyer, whom chance had blown into his company, had evidently been sent by Fate to make his fortune for him. The sudden success at Guichen, hitherto unrivalled, should be repeated and augmented elsewhere. There would be no more sleeping under hedges and tightening of belts. Adversity was behind him. He placed a hand upon Scaramouche’s shoulder, and surveyed him with a smile whose oiliness not even his red paint and colossal false nose could dissemble.
“And what have you to say to me now?” he asked him. “Was I wrong when I assured you that you would succeed? Do you think I have followed my fortunes in the theatre for a lifetime without knowing a born actor when I see one? You are my discovery, Scaramouche. I have discovered you to yourself. I have set your feet upon the road to fame and fortune. I await your thanks.”
Scaramouche laughed at him, and his laugh was not altogether pleasant.
“Always Pantaloon!” said he.
The great countenance became overcast. “I see that you do not yet forgive me the little stratagem by which I forced you to do justice to yourself. Ungrateful dog! As if I could have had any purpose but to make you; and I have done so. Continue as you have begun, and you will end in Paris. You may yet tread the stage of the Comédie Française, the rival of Talma, Fleury, and Dugazon. When that happens to you perhaps you will feel the gratitude that is due to old Binet, for you will owe it all to this softhearted old fool.”
“If you were as good an actor on the stage as you are in private,” said Scaramouche, “you would yourself have won to the Comédie Française long since. But I bear no rancour, M. Binet.”

放肆、机警、刁滑、优雅，他把斯卡拉穆乔这个理想典型演活了，还借博马舍的许多背得出的台词来补自己天生的机智，叫观众里那些有见识的人当真觉得：这里有几分真正的费加罗——仿佛叫他们同京城的大世界沾上了边。
末一次落幕时，同克莱门平分今夜荣光的正是斯卡拉穆乔，被叫到幕前同她并立受彩的，正是他的名字同她的名字联在一起。
他们退回来，幕幔把他们同散场的观众重新隔开，比内先生走过来，肥手轻轻搓着。这个被机缘吹进他班里的流浪年轻律师，显然是命运派来替他发财的。吉尚这场突如其来的、空前的成功，该到别处去再现、去翻番了。再不用睡树篱、勒裤带了。厄运落在身后了。他一只手搭上斯卡拉穆乔的肩，含笑打量他——那笑容的油滑，纵有红颜料和那只巨大的假鼻子也遮不住。
「这会儿您还有什么说的？」他问，「我担保您会成功，说错了吗？您以为我在戏班子里闯荡了一辈子，还认不出天生的演员？您是我发掘的，斯卡拉穆乔。我把您发掘给您自己。我把您的脚放上了名利之路。我等您的谢呢。」
斯卡拉穆乔冲他一笑，那笑不完全是好气的。
「潘塔洛内到底改不了！」
那张大脸阴下来。「我看您还没原谅我逼您显出真本事的那条小计。忘恩负义的东西！我除了成全您还能有什么别的用心？我成全了。照这样开头干下去，您终归要到巴黎。说不定有朝一日您踏上法兰西喜剧院的台板，同塔尔马、弗勒里、杜加宗一争高下。到那一天，您也许会记起该谢一谢老比内，因为这一切都亏了这个心软的老傻瓜。」
「您要在台上也像在台下这样会演，」斯卡拉穆乔说，「您自己早进法兰西喜剧院了。不过我不记仇，比内先生。」

===Original===

He laughed, and put out his hand.
Binet fell upon it and wrung it heartily.
“That, at least, is something,” he declared. “My boy, I have great plans for you﻿—for us. Tomorrow we go to Maure; there is a fair there to the end of this week. Then on Monday we take our chances at Pipriac, and after that we must consider. It may be that I am about to realize the dream of my life. There must have been upwards of fifteen louis taken tonight. Where the devil is that rascal Cordemais?”
Cordemais was the name of the original Scaramouche, who had so unfortunately twisted his ankle. That Binet should refer to him by his secular designation was a sign that in the Binet company at least he had fallen forever from the lofty eminence of Scaramouche.
“Let us go and find him, and then we’ll away to the inn and crack a bottle of the best Burgundy, perhaps two bottles.”
But Cordemais was not readily to be found. None of the company had seen him since the close of the performance. M. Binet went round to the entrance. Cordemais was not there. At first he was annoyed; then as he continued in vain to bawl the fellow’s name, he began to grow uneasy; lastly, when Polichinelle, who was with them, discovered Cordemais’ crutch standing discarded behind the door, M. Binet became alarmed. A dreadful suspicion entered his mind. He grew visibly pale under his paint.
“But this evening he couldn’t walk without the crutch!” he exclaimed. “How then does he come to leave it there and take himself off?”
“Perhaps he has gone on to the inn,” suggested someone.
“But he couldn’t walk without his crutch,” M. Binet insisted.
Nevertheless, since clearly he was not anywhere about the market-hall, to the inn they all trooped, and deafened the landlady with their inquiries.
“Oh, yes, M. Cordemais came in some time ago.”
“Where is he now?”
“He went away again at once. He just came for his bag.”
“For his bag!” Binet was on the point of an apoplexy. “How long ago was that?”
She glanced at the timepiece on the overmantel. “It would be about half an hour ago. It was a few minutes before the Rennes diligence passed through.”
“The Rennes diligence!” M. Binet was almost inarticulate. “Could he﻿ ﻿… could he walk?” he asked, on a note of terrible anxiety.
“Walk? He ran like a hare when he left the inn. I thought, myself, that his agility was suspicious, seeing how lame he had been since he fell downstairs yesterday.

===Chinese===

他笑着伸出手。
比内一把攥住，热烈地摇。
「这总算像句话。孩子，我为你们——为咱们——谋划着大事呢。明天去莫尔，那儿集市开到本周末。星期一到皮普里亚克去碰运气，之后再作商量。说不定我这就要圆我一生的梦了。今晚进项少说十五路易。那流氓科尔德迈死到哪儿去了？」
科尔德迈就是原来那个斯卡拉穆乔——那么倒霉崴了脚踝的那个。比内竟叫起他的俗名来，这是个标志：至少同比内剧团里，他从斯卡拉穆乔的高位上永远跌下来了。
「走，找他去，然后回客栈，开一瓶顶好的勃艮第，兴许两瓶。」
可科尔德迈不好找。散戏之后全团谁也没见过他。比内先生绕到门口。科尔德迈不在。起初他恼；随后喊了半天没人应，开始不安；末了，同行的波利希奈勒发现科尔德迈的拐杖被丢在门后，比内先生慌了。一个可怕的疑心钻进他脑子。油彩底下，他肉眼可见地白了。
「可他今晚没了拐杖走不了路啊！」他喊，「怎么拐杖撞在这儿，人走了？」
「兴许他先回客栈了。」有人猜。
「可他没了拐杖走不了路！」比内先生坚持。
不过市场大厅里既然分明找不着，一行人便都涌回客栈，把老板娘吵聋了。
「哦，见着了，科尔德迈先生有一阵子进来过。」
「他这会儿在哪儿？」
「转身又走了。就回来取了他的提包。」
「取提包！」比内眼看要中风。「那是多久以前？」
她瞟了一眼壁炉架上的座钟。「总半个钟头前吧。雷恩的驿车穿城过去没几分钟。」
「雷恩的驿车！」比内先生话都说不囫囵了。「他……他能走？」他问，声调里透着大祸临头的焦灼。
「走？他出客栈那阵子，跑得跟兔子似的。我自家还在心里寲咕，他那腿脚怎么突然这么利索——昨天从楼梯上摔下来之后，瘸得那样。」

===Original===

Is anything wrong?”
M. Binet had collapsed into a chair. He took his head in his hands, and groaned.
“The scoundrel was shamming all the time!” exclaimed Climène. “His fall downstairs was a trick. He was playing for this. He has swindled us.”
“Fifteen louis at least﻿—perhaps sixteen!” said M. Binet. “Oh, the heartless blackguard! To swindle me who have been as a father to him﻿—and to swindle me in such a moment.”
From the ranks of the silent, awestricken company, each member of which was wondering by how much of the loss his own meagre pay would be mulcted, there came a splutter of laughter.
M. Binet glared with blood-injected eyes.
“Who laughs?” he roared. “What heartless wretch has the audacity to laugh at my misfortune?”
André-Louis, still in the sable glories of Scaramouche, stood forward. He was laughing still.
“It is you, is it? You may laugh on another note, my friend, if I choose a way to recoup myself that I know of.”
“Dullard!” Scaramouche scorned him. “Rabbit-brained elephant! What if Cordemais has gone with fifteen louis? Hasn’t he left you something worth twenty times as much?”
M. Binet gaped uncomprehending.
“You are between two wines, I think. You’ve been drinking,” he concluded.
“So I have﻿—at the fountain of Thalia. Oh, don’t you see? Don’t you see the treasure that Cordemais has left behind him?”
“What has he left?”
“A unique idea for the groundwork of a scenario. It unfolds itself all before me. I’ll borrow part of the title from Molière. We’ll call it *Les Fourberies de Scaramouche*, and if we don’t leave the audiences of Maure and Pipriac with sides aching from laughter I’ll play the dullard Pantaloon in future.”
Polichinelle smacked fist into palm. “Superb!” he said, fiercely. “To cull fortune from misfortune, to turn loss into profit, that is to have genius.”
Scaramouche made a leg. “Polichinelle, you are a fellow after my own heart. I love a man who can discern my merit. If Pantaloon had half your wit, we should have Burgundy tonight in spite of the flight of Cordemais.”
“Burgundy?” roared M. Binet, and before he could get farther Harlequin had clapped his hands together.
“That is the spirit, M. Binet. You heard him, landlady. He called for Burgundy.”
“I called for nothing of the kind.”
“But you heard him, dear madame. We all heard him.”
The others made chorus, whilst Scaramouche smiled at him, and patted his shoulder.

出什么事了？」
比内先生瘫进椅子里，双手抱头，呻吟起来。

「少说十五路易——兴许十六！」比内先生说，「哦，这没心肝的黑良心的！坑我——我一直当他亲儿子待——偏挑这个节骨眼坑我！」
噤声震住的团员中间——人人都在盘算这笔亏空会从自己微薄的薪水里扣掉几成——迸出一声嗤笑。
比内先生血红的眼睛瞪过去。
「谁笑？」他吼，「哪个没心肝的畜生，敢笑我的倒霉？」
安德烈-路易还一身斯卡拉穆乔的黑色华彩，站了出来。他还笑着呢。
「是您，是吧？我的朋友，我要是挑一条我知道的路子把损失找补回来，您笑的调门可就变了。」
「蠢货！」斯卡拉穆乔鄙夷他，「兔子脑子的大象！科尔德迈卷走十五路易又怎样？他留给您的东西，不值那二十倍么？」
比内先生目瞪口呆。
「我看您是两杯酒中间——喝大了。」
「喝了——在塔利亚的泉水边。哦，您还不明白？还不明白科尔德迈给您留下了什么宝？」
「留下了什么？」
「一个现成戏本的绝妙底子。它整个儿在我眼前铺开了。标题我借莫里哀一半。就叫《斯卡拉穆乔的花招》。到时候若不能叫莫尔和皮普里亚克的观众笑得肋骨疼，往后我就改演潘塔洛内那蠢货。」
波利希奈勒一拳砸进掌心。「绝了！」他狠狠地说，「从倒霉里摘财运，把亏空翻成本利，这才叫天才。」
斯卡拉穆乔请了个腿。「波利希奈勒，您真是我的心腹之交。我就爱能识得我价值的人。潘塔洛内要有您一半的脑子，科尔德迈跑了咱们今晚照样有勃艮第喝。」
「勃艮第！」比内先生吼起来，他话音未落，阿勒金已经拍起了巴掌。
「这才是气派，比内先生！您听见了，老板娘。他叫勃艮第呢。」
「我叫什么来着。」
「可您听见了呀，亲爱的夫人。我们都听见了。」
众人齐声起哄，斯卡拉穆乔笑着拍他的肩。

===Original===

“Up, man, a little courage. Did you not say that fortune awaits us? And have we not now the wherewithal to constrain fortune? Burgundy, then, to﻿ ﻿… to toast *Les Fourberies de Scaramouche*.”
And M. Binet, who was not blind to the force of the idea, yielded, took courage, and got drunk with the rest.

===Chinese===

「起来，汉子，拿出点勇气来。不是您说的么，财运等着咱们？咱们如今不是有了拴住财运的家伙什？勃艮第，就来……来祝《斯卡拉穆乔的花招》一帆风顺。」
而比内先生呢，毕竟看得出这主意的分量，让了步，鼓起勇气，同大伙儿一块喝醉了。

## IV Exit Monsieur Parvissimus / 四 帕维西穆斯先生退场
===Original===

At four o’clock on Monday afternoon the curtain rose on *Figaro-Scaramouche* to an audience that filled three quarters of the market-hall. M. Binet attributed this good attendance to the influx of people to Guichen for the fair, and to the magnificent parade of his company through the streets of the township at the busiest time of the day. André-Louis attributed it entirely to the title. It was the *Figaro* touch that had fetched in the better-class bourgeoisie, which filled more than half of the twenty-sous places and three quarters of the twelve-sous seats. The lure had drawn them. Whether it was to continue to do so would depend upon the manner in which the *canevas* over which he had laboured to the glory of Binet was interpreted by the company. Of the merits of the *canevas* itself he had no doubt. The authors upon whom he had drawn for the elements of it were sound, and he had taken of their best, which he claimed to be no more than the justice due to them.
The company excelled itself. The audience followed with relish the sly intriguings of Scaramouche, delighted in the beauty and freshness of Climène, was moved almost to tears by the hard fate which through four long acts kept her from the hungering arms of the so beautiful Leandre, howled its delight over the ignominy of Pantaloon, the buffooneries of his sprightly lackey Harlequin, and the thrasonical strut and bellowing fierceness of the cowardly Rhodomont.
The success of the Binet troupe in Guichen was assured. That night the company drank Burgundy at M. Binet’s expense. The takings reached the sum of eight louis, which was as good business as M. Binet had ever done in all his career. He was very pleased. Gratification rose like steam from his fat body. He even condescended so far as to attribute a share of the credit for the success to M. Parvissimus.
“His suggestion,” he was careful to say, by way of properly delimiting that share, “was most valuable, as I perceived at the time.”
“And his cutting of quills,” growled Polichinelle. “Don’t forget that. It is most important to have by you a man who understands how to cut a quill, as I shall remember when I turn author.”
But not even that gibe could stir M. Binet out of his lethargy of content.
On Tuesday the success was repeated artistically and augmented financially.

===Chinese===

星期一下午四点，幕在《费加罗-斯卡拉穆乔》上开起，市场大厅坐满了四分之三。比内先生把这份好客况归功于赶集涌进吉尚的人潮，和他剧团挑一天里最热闹的时辰在镇上街头的盛大游行。安德烈-路易则把它全归功于剧名。正是「费加罗」这一点把上等市民招了来——二十苏的座位坐满了一多半，十二苏的座位坐满了四分之三。诱饵把他们引来了。它还能不能继续引，就要看剧团怎么演他那份为比内的荣耀呕心写就的幕表（canevas）了。幕表本身的好处，他毫不怀疑。他取材的那几位作家都靠得住，他取的是他们最好的——他自称这不过是给他们应得的公道。
剧团超水平发挥。观众津津有味地追着斯卡拉穆乔的刁滑机关，为克莱门的美貌与新鲜倾倒，为那让她的有情人莱安德罗整整四幕抱不到怀的苦命几乎落泪，为潘塔洛内的丢丑、他那伶俐跟班阿勒金的插科打诨、还有那胆小鬼罗多蒙的虚张声势和咆哮凶悍，笑得吼成一片。
比内剧团在吉尚的成功稳了。当晚全团喝比内先生请的勃艮第。进项高达八个路易——比内先生整个生涯也没做过这么好的生意。他快活极了。得意像蒸汽一样从他肥壮的身体里冒出来。他甚至屈尊降贵，肯把成功的一份功劳记给帕维西穆斯先生。
「他的建议，」他小心地说，好把这份数目划定界限，「极有价值——我当时就看出来了。」
「还有他削的鹅毛笔，」波利希奈勒咕噜道，「别忘了这个。身边有个懂削笔的人顶要紧，等我改行当作家，我就记着。」
可连这句挖苦也搅不动比内先生那知足的酣态。
星期二，成功在艺术上重演，在钱上翻了番。

===Original===

Ten louis and seven livres was the enormous sum that André-Louis, the doorkeeper, counted over to M. Binet after the performance. Never yet had M. Binet made so much money in one evening﻿—and a miserable little village like Guichen was certainly the last place in which he would have expected this windfall.
“Ah, but Guichen in time of fair,” André-Louis reminded him. “There are people here from as far as Nantes and Rennes to buy and sell. Tomorrow, being the last day of the fair, the crowds will be greater than ever. We should better this evening’s receipts.”
“Better them? I shall be quite satisfied if we do as well, my friend.”
“You can depend upon that,” André-Louis assured him. “Are we to have Burgundy?”
And then the tragedy occurred. It announced itself in a succession of bumps and thuds, culminating in a crash outside the door that brought them all to their feet in alarm.
Pierrot sprang to open, and beheld the tumbled body of a man lying at the foot of the stairs. It emitted groans, therefore it was alive. Pierrot went forward to turn it over, and disclosed the fact that the body wore the wizened face of Scaramouche, a grimacing, groaning, twitching Scaramouche.
The whole company, pressing after Pierrot, abandoned itself to laughter.
“I always said you should change parts with me,” cried Harlequin. “You’re such an excellent tumbler. Have you been practising?”
“Fool!” Scaramouche snapped. “Must you be laughing when I’ve all but broken my neck?”
“You are right. We ought to be weeping because you didn’t break it. Come, man, get up,” and he held out a hand to the prostrate rogue.
Scaramouche took the hand, clutched it, heaved himself from the ground, then with a scream dropped back again.
“My foot!” he complained.
Binet rolled through the group of players, scattering them to right and left. Apprehension had been quick to seize him. Fate had played him such tricks before.
“What ails your foot?” quoth he, sourly.
“It’s broken, I think,” Scaramouche complained.
“Broken? Bah! Get up, man.” He caught him under the armpits and hauled him up.
Scaramouche came howling to one foot; the other doubled under him when he attempted to set it down, and he must have collapsed again but that Binet supported him. He filled the place with his plaint, whilst Binet swore amazingly and variedly.
“Must you bellow like a calf, you fool? Be quiet. A chair here, someone.”

===Chinese===

十路易又七里弗尔——这是看门人安德烈-路易散戏后点交给比内先生的天文数字。比内先生从没在一晚上挣过这么多——而吉尚这样一个可怜的小村子，也实在是他最想不到会发这笔横财的地方。
「啊，可这是赶集时的吉尚，」安德烈-路易提醒他，「南特和雷恩都有人来赶买卖。明天是集末日，人会更多。咱们该胜过今晚的进项。」
「胜过？咱们赶得上今晚我就知足了，我的朋友。」
「这个您尽管放心。」安德烈-路易向他担保，「有勃艮第喝吗？」
就在这时，惨剧发生了。它以一连串的磕碰和闷响自我通报，末了在门外一声轰然巨响，把满屋子人都惊得站了起来。
皮埃罗抢去开门，看见楼梯脚下躺着一团摔作一堆的人。那团东西在呻吟，所以还活着。皮埃罗上前把它翻过来，露出事实：这具身体顶着一副斯卡拉穆乔的干瘪脸——一副龇牙咧嘴、哼哼唧唧、抽抽搐搐的斯卡拉穆乔。
全团人跟着皮埃罗拥过去，随即笑作一团。
「我早说你该跟我换角色，」阿勒金喊，「你翻跟头这么拿手。练过吧？」
「蠢货！」斯卡拉穆乔喝道，「我差点摔断脖子，你还笑？」
「是我不对。你没能摔断，我们才该哭呢。来，汉子，起来。」他向那趴着的家伙伸出手。
斯卡拉穆乔抓住手，攥紧，从地上撑起来，随即一声尖叫，又倒了回去。
「我的脚！」他哀叫。
比内从演员堆里滚过来，把众人左右分开。疑惧来得快。命运捉弄他不是头一回了。
「你那只脚怎么了？」他酸溜溜地问。
「断了，怕是。」斯卡拉穆乔诉苦。
「断了？呸！起来，汉子。」他抄起他的胳肢窝，把他拽起来。
斯卡拉穆乔嚎着站上一只脚；另一只一沾地就朝里折，若不是比内架着，他早又瘫下去了。他满屋子的哀号，比内则骂得五花八门、气象万千。
「你非得像牛犊子似的嚎么，蠢货？闭嘴。椅子，谁递一把。」

===Original===

A chair was thrust forward. He crushed Scaramouche down into it.
“Let us look at this foot of yours.”
Heedless of Scaramouche’s howls of pain, he swept away shoe and stocking.
“What ails it?” he asked, staring. “Nothing that I can see.” He seized it, heel in one hand, instep in the other, and gyrated it. Scaramouche screamed in agony, until Climène caught Binet’s arm and made him stop.
“My God, have you no feelings?” she reproved her father. “The lad has hurt his foot. Must you torture him? Will that cure it?”
“Hurt his foot!” said Binet. “I can see nothing the matter with his foot﻿—nothing to justify all this uproar. He has bruised it, maybe﻿ ﻿…”
“A man with a bruised foot doesn’t scream like that,” said Madame over Climène’s shoulder. “Perhaps he has dislocated it.”
“That is what I fear,” whimpered Scaramouche.
Binet heaved himself up in disgust.
“Take him to bed,” he bade them, “and fetch a doctor to see him.”
It was done, and the doctor came. Having seen the patient, he reported that nothing very serious had happened, but that in falling he had evidently sprained his foot a little. A few days’ rest and all would be well.
“A few days!” cried Binet. “God of God! Do you mean that he can’t walk?”
“It would be unwise, indeed impossible for more than a few steps.”
M. Binet paid the doctor’s fee, and sat down to think. He filled himself a glass of Burgundy, tossed it off without a word, and sat thereafter staring into the empty glass.
“It is of course the sort of thing that must always be happening to me,” he grumbled to no one in particular. The members of the company were all standing in silence before him, sharing his dismay. “I might have known that this﻿—or something like it﻿—would occur to spoil the first vein of luck that I have found in years. Ah, well, it is finished. Tomorrow we pack and depart. The best day of the fair, on the crest of the wave of our success﻿—a good fifteen louis to be taken, and this happens! God of God!”
“Do you mean to abandon tomorrow’s performance?”
All turned to stare with Binet at André-Louis.
“Are we to play *Figaro-Scaramouche* without Scaramouche?” asked Binet, sneering.
“Of course not.” André-Louis came forward. “But surely some rearrangement of the parts is possible. For instance, there is a fine actor in Polichinelle.”
Polichinelle swept him a bow. “Overwhelmed,” said he, ever sardonic.

===Chinese===

椅子递上前来。他把斯卡拉穆乔按坐进去。
「来瞧瞧你这只脚。」
他全不理会斯卡拉穆乔的痛嚎，扒掉鞋袜。
「怎么啦？」他瞪着那脚，「我瞧不出什么名堂。」他一把攥住——脚跟一手，脚背一手——拧了起来。斯卡拉穆乔痛得尖叫，直到克莱门拉住比内的胳膊，才叫他停了手。
「我的天，你没有一点心肝么？」她责备父亲，「孩子脚伤了。你非得折磨他？这样能治好么？」
「伤了脚！」比内说，「我瞧他这只脚什么事也没有——配不上这么一场大闹。挫伤罢了，兴许……」
「挫了脚的人不会那么尖叫，」比内太太隔着克莱门的肩膀说，「怕是脱了臼。」
「我怕正是。」斯卡拉穆乔抽抽搭搭。
比内厌恶地直起身。
「送他上床，」他吩咐，「请个大夫来看看。」
照办了，大夫也来了。看过病人，他报告说没出什么大毛病，只是摔下来时脚腕显然有些扭伤。歇几天就好。
「歇几天！」比内喊，「老天爷老天爷！你是说他走不了路？」
「多走几步不但不明智，简直办不到。」
比内先生付了诊金，坐下来想心事。他给自己倒了一杯勃艮第，一言不发仰头灌掉，此后就那么瞪着空杯子。
「这种事，自然，回回都轮到我头上。」他不知对着谁发牢骚。团员们默默站在他跟前，共着他的沮丧。「我早该料到——这一桩，或诸如此类的一桩——会冒出来，毁掉我这些年头一条运气。唉，完了。明天打铺盖走人。集上最好的一天，正踩在成功的浪尖上——稳稳当当十五路易的进项啊——偏偏出这事！老天爷老天爷！」
「您要放弃明天的演出？」
全团同比内一起转头，瞪着安德烈-路易。
「没了斯卡拉穆乔，您要我们怎么演《费加罗-斯卡拉穆乔》？」比内讥笑。
「当然不是。可角色重新调配一下，总归行得通。比如，波利希奈勒就是位好演员。」
波利希奈勒甩给他一躬。「承蒙抬举。」他说，照例带刺。

===Original===

“But he has a part of his own,” objected Binet.
“A small part, which Pasquariel could play.”
“And who will play Pasquariel?”
“Nobody. We delete it. The play need not suffer.”
“He thinks of everything,” sneered Polichinelle. “What a man!”
But Binet was far from agreement. “Are you suggesting that Polichinelle should play Scaramouche?” he asked, incredulously.
“Why not? He is able enough!”
“Overwhelmed again,” interjected Polichinelle.
“Play Scaramouche with that figure?” Binet heaved himself up to point a denunciatory finger at Polichinelle’s sturdy, thickset shortness.
“For lack of a better,” said André-Louis.
“Overwhelmed more than ever.” Polichinelle’s bow was superb this time. “Faith, I think I’ll take the air to cool me after so much blushing.”
“Go to the devil,” Binet flung at him.
“Better and better.” Polichinelle made for the door. On the threshold he halted and struck an attitude. “Understand me, Binet. I do not now play Scaramouche in any circumstances whatever.” And he went out. On the whole, it was a very dignified exit.
André-Louis shrugged, threw out his arms, and let them fall to his sides again. “You have ruined everything,” he told M. Binet. “The matter could easily have been arranged. Well, well, it is you are master here; and since you want us to pack and be off, that is what we will do, I suppose.”
He went out, too. M. Binet stood in thought a moment, then followed him, his little eyes very cunning. He caught him up in the doorway. “Let us take a walk together, M. Parvissimus,” said he, very affably.
He thrust his arm through André-Louis’, and led him out into the street, where there was still considerable movement. Past the booths that ranged about the market they went, and down the hill towards the bridge. “I don’t think we shall pack tomorrow,” said M. Binet, presently. “In fact, we shall play tomorrow night.”
“Not if I know Polichinelle. You have﻿ ﻿…”
“I am not thinking of Polichinelle.”
“Of whom, then?”
“Of yourself.”
“I am flattered, sir. And in what capacity are you thinking of me?” There was something too sleek and oily in Binet’s voice for André-Louis’ taste.
“I am thinking of you in the part of Scaramouche.”
“Daydreams,” said André-Louis. “You are amusing yourself, of course.”
“Not in the least. I am quite serious.”
“But I am not an actor.”
“You told me that you could be.”
“Oh, upon occasion﻿ ﻿… a small part, perhaps﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

「可他有自己的角色。」比内反对。
「小角色，帕斯夸里埃尔顶得下。」
「那谁演帕斯夸里埃尔？」
「没人演。删掉。戏吃不了亏。」
「他什么都想到了，」波利希奈勒讥讽，「真人才！」
可比内远谈不上同意。「你是提议让波利希奈勒演斯卡拉穆乔？」他难以置信地问。
「有什么不行？他本事尽够！」
「又承蒙抬举。」波利希奈勒插一句。
「就凭那个身段演斯卡拉穆乔？」比内直起身，指着波利希奈勒那敦实粗短的五短身材，控诉一般。
「矮子里拔将军嘛。」安德烈-路易说。
「越发承蒙抬举了。」波利希奈勒这一回的躬甩得漂亮至极。「真的，我得出去吹吹风，凉快凉快——脸红成这样。」
「见鬼去。」比内朝他一挥手。
「越说越好了。」波利希奈勒向门口走去。到了门槛，他站定，摆了个姿势。「听明白，比内。如今无论什么情形，我都不演斯卡拉穆乔。」说完他出去了。总的说，退场退得很有气派。
安德烈-路易耸耸肩，双臂一摊，又放回身侧。「您把一切都毁了，」他同比内先生说，「这事本来轻易就摆平了。罢了罢了，您是这儿的主人；您既然要我们卷铺盖走，那我们就走呗。」
他也出去了。比内先生站着想了一会儿，随后跟了出去，小眼睛里全是精明。他在门口赶上他。「咱们一起散散步，帕维西穆斯先生。」他和颜悦色地说。
他挽起安德烈-路易的胳膊，领他上了街，街上还很热闹。他们走过市场四围的货棚，下坡朝桥走去。「我看咱们明天不打铺盖了，」过了一阵，比内先生说，「其实，明晚咱们照演。」
「除非我认错了波利希奈勒。您已经……」
「我没想波利希奈勒。」
「那想谁？」
「想您。」
「承蒙抬举，先生。您指望我做什么呢？」比内嗓音里那份过分油滑，叫安德烈-路易不对胃口。
「我指望您演斯卡拉穆乔这个角色。」
「白日梦。」安德烈-路易说，「您自然是在说笑。」
「一点不说笑。我顶认真。」
「可我不是演员。」
「您自己说过，您当得了。」
「哦，偶尔客串……兴许演个小角色……」

===Original===

“Well, here is a big part﻿—the chance to arrive at a single stride. How many men have had such a chance?”
“It is a chance I do not covet, M. Binet. Shall we change the subject?” He was very frosty, as much perhaps because he scented in M. Binet’s manner something that was vaguely menacing as for any other reason.
“We’ll change the subject when I please,” said M. Binet, allowing a glimpse of steel to glimmer through the silk of him. “Tomorrow night you play Scaramouche. You are ready enough in your wits, your figure is ideal, and you have just the kind of mordant humour for the part. You should be a great success.”
“It is much more likely that I should be an egregious failure.”
“That won’t matter,” said Binet, cynically, and explained himself. “The failure will be personal to yourself. The receipts will be safe by then.”
“Much obliged,” said André-Louis.
“We should take fifteen louis tomorrow night.”
“It is unfortunate that you are without a Scaramouche,” said André-Louis.
“It is fortunate that I have one, M. Parvissimus.”
André-Louis disengaged his arm. “I begin to find you tiresome,” said he. “I think I will return.”
“A moment, M. Parvissimus. If I am to lose that fifteen louis﻿ ﻿… you’ll not take it amiss that I compensate myself in other ways?”
“That is your own concern, M. Binet.”
“Pardon, M. Parvissimus. It may possibly be also yours.” Binet took his arm again. “Do me the kindness to step across the street with me. Just as far as the post-office there. I have something to show you.”
André-Louis went. Before they reached that sheet of paper nailed upon the door, he knew exactly what it would say. And in effect it was, as he had supposed, that twenty louis would be paid for information leading to the apprehension of one André-Louis Moreau, lawyer of Gavrillac, who was wanted by the King’s Lieutenant in Rennes upon a charge of sedition.
M. Binet watched him whilst he read. Their arms were linked, and Binet’s grip was firm and powerful.
“Now, my friend,” said he, “will you be M. Parvissimus and play Scaramouche tomorrow, or will you be André-Louis Moreau of Gavrillac and go to Rennes to satisfy the King’s Lieutenant?”
“And if it should happen that you are mistaken?” quoth André-Louis, his face a mask.
“I’ll take the risk of that,” leered M. Binet. “You mentioned, I think, that you were a lawyer. An indiscretion, my dear.

===Chinese===

「这就是个大角色——一步登天的机会。几个人赶上过这样的机会？」
「这机会我不稀罕，比内先生。换个话题行么？」他的口吻冷冰冰的，一半也因为他在比内的做派里嗅到一丝说不清的威胁。
「我想换才换。」比内先生说，任他的绸子底下闪出一线钢来。「明晚您演斯卡拉穆乔。您的脑子够快，身段是理想的，您又有那种角色正需要的刻薄幽默。您必定大获成功。」
「更可能是我一败涂地。」
「那不要紧。」比内无动于衷地说，随后解释明白，「砸的是您个人的锅。那时候进项已经稳落袋了。」
「多谢关照。」安德烈-路易说。
「明晚咱们该收十五路易。」
「可惜您缺一个斯卡拉穆乔。」安德烈-路易说。
「幸好我有一个，帕维西穆斯先生。」
安德烈-路易抽出胳膊。「我开始觉得您烦人了，」他说，「我想回去了。」
「稍等，帕维西穆斯先生。倘若我注定失去那十五路易……我另想法子补偿自己，您不见怪吧？」
「那是您自己的事，比内先生。」
「对不起，帕维西穆斯先生。未必不是您的事。」比内又挽住他的胳膊。「赏个脸，同我过一趟街。就到那儿的邮局。有点东西给您看。」
安德烈-路易去了。还没走到那张钉在门上的纸跟前，他就完全知道上面写的什么了。果然不出所料：凡提供线索、缉获加夫里雅克律师安德烈-路易·莫罗一名者，赏路易二十；此人因煽动罪，被雷恩国王代理官缉拿。
比内先生看他读。两人胳膊相挽，比内的手劲又紧又沉。
「怎么样，我的朋友，」他说，「您是做帕维西穆斯先生、明晚上台演斯卡拉穆乔呢，还是做加夫里雅克的安德烈-路易·莫罗、去雷恩向国王代理官交代？」
「倘若您认错了人呢？」安德烈-路易说，脸成了一副面具。
「这个险我认了。」比内先生眯着眼笑，「我记得您提过，您是律师。欠考虑哪，亲爱的。

===Original===

It is unlikely that two lawyers will be in hiding at the same time in the same district. You see it is not really clever of me. Well, M. André-Louis Moreau, lawyer of Gavrillac, what is it to be?”
“We will talk it over as we walk back,” said André-Louis.
“What is there to talk over?”
“One or two things, I think. I must know where I stand. Come, sir, if you please.”
“Very well,” said M. Binet, and they turned up the street again, but M. Binet maintained a firm hold of his young friend’s arm, and kept himself on the alert for any tricks that the young gentleman might be disposed to play. It was an unnecessary precaution. André-Louis was not the man to waste his energy futilely. He knew that in bodily strength he was no match at all for the heavy and powerful Pantaloon.
“If I yield to your most eloquent and seductive persuasions, M. Binet,” said he, sweetly, “what guarantee do you give me that you will not sell me for twenty louis after I shall have served your turn?”
“You have my word of honour for that.” M. Binet was emphatic.
André-Louis laughed. “Oh, we are to talk of honour, are we? Really, M. Binet? It is clear you think me a fool.”
In the dark he did not see the flush that leapt to M. Binet’s round face. It was some moments before he replied.
“Perhaps you are right,” he growled. “What guarantee do you want?”
“I do not know what guarantee you can possibly give.”
“I have said that I will keep faith with you.”
“Until you find it more profitable to sell me.”
“You have it in your power to make it more profitable always for me to keep faith with you. It is due to you that we have done so well in Guichen. Oh, I admit it frankly.”
“In private,” said André-Louis.
M. Binet left the sarcasm unheeded.
“What you have done for us here with *Figaro-Scaramouche*, you can do elsewhere with other things. Naturally, I shall not want to lose you. That is your guarantee.”
“Yet tonight you would sell me for twenty louis.”
“Because﻿—name of God!﻿—you enrage me by refusing me a service well within your powers. Don’t you think, had I been entirely the rogue you think me, I could have sold you on Saturday last? I want you to understand me, my dear Parvissimus.”
“I beg that you’ll not apologize. You would be more tiresome than ever.”
“Of course you will be gibing. You never miss a chance to gibe. It’ll bring you trouble before you’re done with life.

同一个区里，同时藏着两个律师，未免不像话。您瞧，这实在算不得我聪明。那么，加夫里雅克律师安德烈-路易·莫罗先生，怎么说？」
「回去的路上再谈。」安德烈-路易说。
「还有什么可谈的？」
「我想总有一两件。我总得知道自己的立场。来吧，先生，请。」
「很好。」比内先生说。两人折回街上，可比内先生始终牢牢攥着这位年轻朋友的胳膊，警惕着这位年轻绅士也许要玩的任何花招。这防范其实多余。安德烈-路易不是白白浪费力气的人。他知道论膂力，他根本不是笨重壮大的潘塔洛内的对手。
「我若依从了您这番最雄辩、最诱人的劝说，比内先生，」他甜甜地说，「您拿什么担保，等我替您效完力，您不会为二十路易把我卖了？」
「我拿我的名誉担保。」比内先生斩钉截铁。
安德烈-路易笑了。「哦，谈名誉了，是么？真的假的，比内先生？看来您当我是傻子。」
黑暗里，他看不见比内先生圆脸上一蹿的红。隔了好一阵，比内才答话。
「也许你说得对，」他咕噜道，「你要什么担保？」
「我想不出您能给什么担保。」
「我说了，我会守信。」
「直到您发现卖我更合算。」
「您手里有本事，叫我守信永远比卖您合算。吉尚生意这么好，是您的功劳。哦，我坦白承认。」
「背着人承认。」安德烈-路易说。
比内先生全不理会这讥讽。
「您在这儿用《费加罗-斯卡拉穆乔》替我们挣的，换到别处、换个戏码照样挣。我自然不肯放您走。这就是您的担保。」
「可今晚您就肯为二十路易卖我。」
「因为——老天爷——您拒绝一件您明明办得到的事，把我气疯了。您不想想，我要真是您想的那个彻头彻尾的恶棍，上星期六不就卖您了？我要您明白我这个人，亲爱的帕维西穆斯。」
「请您千万别道歉。您会更烦人。」
「您自然要挖苦。您从不放过挖苦的机会。这毛病早晚会给您招祸。」

Come; here we are back at the inn, and you have not yet given me your decision.”
André-Louis looked at him. “I must yield, of course. I can’t help myself.”
M. Binet released his arm at last, and slapped him heartily upon the back. “Well declared, my lad. You’ll never regret it. If I know anything of the theatre, I know that you have made the great decision of your life. Tomorrow night you’ll thank me.”
André-Louis shrugged, and stepped out ahead towards the inn. But M. Binet called him back.
“M. Parvissimus!”
He turned. There stood the man’s great bulk, the moonlight beating down upon that round fat face of his, and he was holding out his hand.
“M. Parvissimus, no rancour. It is a thing I do not admit into my life. You will shake hands with me, and we will forget all this.”
André-Louis considered him a moment with disgust. He was growing angry. Then, realizing this, he conceived himself ridiculous, almost as ridiculous as that sly, scoundrelly Pantaloon. He laughed and took the outstretched hand. “No rancour?” M. Binet insisted.
“Oh, no rancour,” said André-Louis.

安德烈-路易厌恶地打量他片刻。他正在动怒。随即，察觉了这一点，他觉得自己可笑——几乎同那个刁滑恶棍潘塔洛内一样可笑。他笑了，握住那只伸来的手。「不记仇？」比内先生追问。
「哦，不记仇。」安德烈-路易说。

## XII Inferences / 十二 推断
===Original===

By fast driving André-Louis had reached the ground some minutes ahead of time, notwithstanding the slight delay in setting out. There he had found M. de La Tour d’Azyr already awaiting him, supported by a M. d’Ormesson, a swarthy young gentleman in the blue uniform of a captain in the Gardes du Corps.
André-Louis had been silent and preoccupied throughout that drive. He was perturbed by his last interview with Mademoiselle de Kercadiou and the rash inferences which he had drawn as to her motives.
“Decidedly,” he had said, “this man must be killed.”
Le Chapelier had not answered him. Almost, indeed, had the Breton shuddered at his compatriot’s cold-bloodedness. He had often of late thought that this fellow Moreau was hardly human. Also he had found him incomprehensibly inconsistent. When first this spadassinicide business had been proposed to him, he had been so very lofty and disdainful. Yet, having embraced it, he went about it at times with a ghoulish flippancy that was revolting, at times with a detachment that was more revolting still.
Their preparations were made quickly and in silence, yet without undue haste or other sign of nervousness on either side. In both men the same grim determination prevailed. The opponent must be killed; there could be no half-measures here. Stripped each of coat and waistcoat, shoeless and with shirtsleeves rolled to the elbow, they faced each other at last, with the common resolve of paying in full the long score that stood between them. I doubt if either of them entertained a misgiving as to what must be the issue.
Beside them, and opposite each other, stood Le Chapelier and the young captain, alert and watchful.
“*Allez, messieurs!*”
The slender, wickedly delicate blades clashed together, and after a momentary *glizade* were whirling, swift and bright as lightnings, and almost as impossible to follow with the eye. The Marquis led the attack, impetuously and vigorously, and almost at once André-Louis realized that he had to deal with an opponent of a very different mettle from those successive duellists of last week, not excluding La Motte-Royau, of terrible reputation.
Here was a man whom much and constant practice had given extraordinary speed and a technique that was almost perfect. In addition, he enjoyed over André-Louis physical advantages of strength and length of reach, which rendered him altogether formidable.

===Chinese===

安德烈-路易车赶得快，虽然动身略迟，还是提前几分钟到了场子。他发现德·拉图尔达齐尔先生已在等他，由一位多尔梅松先生陪同——一个黑脸膛的年轻绅士，穿王室卫队上尉的蓝色制服。
那一路上安德烈-路易沉默而出神。他为自己同德·凯尔卡迪乌小姐最后一面、为自己对她动机所下的轻率推断烦乱着。
「不用说了，」他当时说，「这个人必须死。」
勒·夏普利耶没有答话。这位布列塔尼人几乎要为他同胞的冷血打寒噪。他近来常常想，这个莫罗简直不食人间烟火。他还发现他矛盾得没法理解。当初这桩屠剑客的营生刚提给他时，他是那样清高、那样鄙夷；可一经接手，他干起来时而带着一种叫人作呕的、食尸鬼式的轻佻，时而带着一种更叫人作呕的超然。
两边的准备做得又快又静，却没有半点仓促，也没有半点紧张的形迹。两个人心里都是同一种森冷的决心：对手必须死；这里容不得留手。两人各自脱去外衣和背心，赤着脚，衬衫袖子挽到肘弯，终于面对面站定，抱着共同的意愿：把横在他们之间那笔长长的账，一次付清。我怀疑他们两位有谁对结局起过一丝疑虑。
勒·夏普利耶和那位年轻上尉站在两侧，彼此相对，机警地注视着。
「请吧，两位先生！（Allez, messieurs!）」
细长而险恶地精巧的剑刃鏗然相击，一记短暂的滑步之后，旋舞起来，迅捷明亮如闪电，也几乎同闪电一样叫肉眼无法追随。侯爵领先进攻，凶猛而有力，几乎立刻，安德烈-路易便明白：他面对的对手，同上星期那接连几位决斗者完全不是一种材料——连名声骇人的拉莫特-鲁瓦约也不算数。
眼前这位，经年不辍的练习给了他非凡的速度和近乎完美的技术。此外，他在膂力和臂长上都胜过安德烈-路易，这就使他全然可怕。

===Original===

And he was cool, too; cool and self-contained; fearless and purposeful. Would anything shake that calm, wondered André-Louis?
He desired the punishment to be as full as he could make it. Not content to kill the Marquis as the Marquis had killed Philippe, he desired that he should first know himself as powerless to avert that death as Philippe had been. Nothing less would content André-Louis. M. le Marquis must begin by tasting of that cup of despair. It was in the account; part of the quittance due.
As with a breaking sweep André-Louis parried the heavy lunge in which that first series of passes culminated, he actually laughed﻿—gleefully, after the fashion of a boy at a sport he loves.
That extraordinary, ill-timed laugh made M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s recovery hastier and less correctly dignified than it would otherwise have been. It startled and discomposed him, who had already been discomposed by the failure to get home with a lunge so beautifully timed and so truly delivered.
He, too, had realized that his opponent’s force was above anything that he could have expected, fencing-master though he might be, and on that account he had put forth his utmost energy to make an end at once.
More than the actual parry, the laugh by which it was accompanied seemed to make of that end no more than a beginning. And yet it was the end of something. It was the end of that absolute confidence that had hitherto inspired M. de La Tour d’Azyr. He no longer looked upon the issue as a thing forgone. He realized that if he was to prevail in this encounter, he must go warily and fence as he had never fenced yet in all his life.
They settled down again; and again﻿—on the principle this time that the soundest defence is in attack﻿—it was the Marquis who made the game. André-Louis allowed him to do so, desired him to do so; desired him to spend himself and that magnificent speed of his against the greater speed that whole days of fencing in succession for nearly two years had given the master. With a beautiful, easy pressure of forte on foible André-Louis kept himself completely covered in that second bout, which once more culminated in a lunge.
Expecting it now, André-Louis parried it by no more than a deflecting touch.

===Chinese===

而且他冷静；冷静而自持；无畏而有目的。有什么能撼动那份沉着么，安德烈-路易想。
他要这场惩罚重到无以复加。他不满足于像侯爵杀菲利普那样杀掉侯爵；他要他先尝到菲利普当初那种眼睁睁无力回天的滋味。少于此不能叫安德烈-路易满足。侯爵先生必须先饮那一杯绝望。这记在账上；是应付清款的一部分。
当那第一轮连续进攻以一记沉重直刺收尾时，安德烈-路易以一记挥压荡开它，竟笑了——快活地，像玩着心爱游戏的少年。
那声不合时宜的怪笑，叫德·拉图尔达齐尔先生的回防乱了分寸，失了往日端正的从容。它叫他吃惊，叫他心乱——此前那一记时机拿捉得那样漂亮、递得那样正的直刺竟没有得手，已经叫他心乱了。
他同样明白了对手的实力远出他意料之外——哪怕他自己也是剑术行家——为此他早已使出全力，想立时分出个了断。
比那实际的格挡更厉害的，是随同它而来的那声笑，仿佛把「了断」变成了「开场」。可它到底了结了一样东西：了结了至今支撑着德·拉图尔达齐尔先生的那份绝对的自信。他不再把结局看作囊中之物。他明白，要想赢下这一场，他必须步步小心，拿出他此生从未拿出过的剑来。
两人重新站稳；这一回，本着「最稳的防守在进攻」的原则，又是侯爵掌控局面。安德烈-路易由他掌控，巴望他掌控；巴望他耗费自己、耗费那份漂亮的速度，去撞一份更大的速度——那是一位老师傅将近两年、连日累日的击剑换来的。安德烈-路易以剑根对剑尖那种漂亮从容的压制，在第二轮里把自己护得滴水不漏；这一轮又以一记直刺收尾。
这一回他等着它，只轻轻一触，把剑格偏。

===Original===

At the same moment he stepped suddenly forward, right within the other’s guard, thus placing his man so completely at his mercy that, as if fascinated, the Marquis did not even attempt to recover himself.
This time André-Louis did not laugh: He just smiled into the dilating eyes of M. de La Tour d’Azyr, and made no shift to use his advantage.
“Come, come, monsieur!” he bade him sharply. “Am I to run my blade through an uncovered man?” Deliberately he fell back, whilst his shaken opponent recovered himself at last.
M. d’Ormesson released the breath which horror had for a moment caught. Le Chapelier swore softly, muttering:
“Name of a name! It is tempting Providence to play the fool in this fashion!”
André-Louis observed the ashen pallor that now over spread the face of his opponent.
“I think you begin to realize, monsieur, what Philippe de Vilmorin must have felt that day at Gavrillac. I desired that you should first do so. Since that is accomplished, why, here’s to make an end.”
He went in with lightning rapidity. For a moment his point seemed to La Tour d’Azyr to be everywhere at once, and then from a low engagement *in sixte*, André-Louis stretched forward with swift and vigorous ease to lunge *in tierce*. He drove his point to transfix his opponent whom a series of calculated disengages uncovered in that line. But to his amazement and chagrin, La Tour d’Azyr parried the stroke; infinitely more to his chagrin La Tour d’Azyr parried it just too late. Had he completely parried it, all would yet have been well. But striking the blade in the last fraction of a second, the Marquis deflected the point from the line of his body, yet not so completely but that a couple of feet of that hard-driven steel tore through the muscles of his sword-arm.
To the seconds none of these details had been visible. All that they had seen had been a swift whirl of flashing blades, and then André-Louis stretched almost to the ground in an upward lunge that had pierced the Marquis’ right arm just below the shoulder.
The sword fell from the suddenly relaxed grip of La Tour d’Azyr’s fingers, which had been rendered powerless, and he stood now disarmed, his lip in his teeth, his face white, his chest heaving, before his opponent, who had at once recovered.

同时他突然抢步上前，直入对方剑门之内，把对手整个儿置于自己掌中——侯爵竟像被摄住了一样，连回防都忘了试。
这一次安德烈-路易没有笑。他只对德·拉图尔达齐尔先生睸大的眼睛莞尔一笑，并不乘势进招。
「来吧，来吧，先生！」他厉声催他，「莫非要我把剑捅进一个门户洞开的人身上？」他从容退开，任惊魂未定的对手缓过气来。
多尔梅松先生把被惊骇噔住的那口气吐了出来。勒·夏普利耶低低骂了一句，咕哝道：
「名堂啊名堂！这样儿戏，是招天谴！」
安德烈-路易看着对手脸上漫开的死灰。
「我想您开始明白了，先生，菲利普·德·维尔莫兰那天在加夫里雅克是什么滋味。我要您先明白这个。既然明白了，好，这就了账。」
他闪电般抢攻。有一瞬，他的剑尖在拉图尔达齐尔眼里仿佛无处不在；随后，从一个第六姿的低位交剑，安德烈-路易舒展身形，迅捷而从容地刺向第三姿。他一气连用几个算度好的换线逼开对手的剑门，剑尖直取对方中线。可叫他惊愕惆怅的是，拉图尔达齐尔格开了这一剑；更叫他惆怅十倍的是，拉图尔达齐尔格得太迟。倘若他完全格开，一切还有可为。可侯爵在最后的一瞬擦着剑身一击，把剑尖荡离了自己身体的直线，却没荡干净——两英尺势大力沉的钢条，撕开了他持剑臂的肌肉。
这些细节，两位助手谁也没有看清。他们看见的只是一阵剑光飞旋，随后安德烈-路易几乎贴地舒展，一记上挑的直刺，穿透了侯爵右臂，肩头略下。
剑从拉图尔达齐尔骤然瘫软的手指间落下——那只手已经不听使唤——他站在那儿，解除了武装，嘴唇咬在齿间，脸色惨白，胸口起伏，面对着早已收回剑、重新立定的对手。

===Original===

With the blood-tinged tip of his sword resting on the ground, André-Louis surveyed him grimly, as we survey the prey that through our own clumsiness has escaped us at the last moment.
In the Assembly and in the newspapers this might be hailed as another victory for the Paladin of the Third Estate; only himself could know the extent and the bitternest of the failure.
M. d’Ormesson had sprung to the side of his principal.
“You are hurt!” he had cried stupidly.
“It is nothing,” said La Tour d’Azyr. “A scratch.” But his lip writhed, and the torn sleeve of his fine cambric shirt was full of blood.
D’Ormesson, a practical man in such matters, produced a linen kerchief, which he tore quickly into strips to improvise a bandage.
Still André-Louis continued to stand there, looking on as if bemused. He continued so until Le Chapelier touched him on the arm. Then at last he roused himself, sighed, and turned away to resume his garments, nor did he address or look again at his late opponent, but left the ground at once.
As, with Le Chapelier, he was walking slowly and in silent dejection towards the entrance of the Bois, where they had left their carriage, they were passed by the caleche conveying La Tour d’Azyr and his second﻿—which had originally driven almost right up to the spot of the encounter. The Marquis’ wounded arm was carried in a sling improvised from his companion’s sword-belt. His sky-blue coat with three collars had been buttoned over this, so that the right sleeve hung empty. Otherwise, saving a certain pallor, he looked much his usual self.
And now you understand how it was that he was the first to return, and that seeing him thus returning, apparently safe and sound, the two ladies, intent upon preventing the encounter, should have assumed that their worst fears were realized.
Mme. de Plougastel attempted to call out, but her voice refused its office. She attempted to throw open the door of her own carriage; but her fingers fumbled clumsily and ineffectively with the handle. And meanwhile the caleche was slowly passing, La Tour d’Azyr’s fine eyes sombrely yet intently meeting her own anguished gaze. And then she saw something else. M. d’Ormesson, leaning back again from the forward inclination of his body to join his own to his companion’s salutation of the Countess, disclosed the empty right sleeve of M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s blue coat.

===Chinese===

安德烈-路易剑尖拄地，尖上染着血，冷冷地打量他，像我们打量那因自己笨拙、在最后一刻脱手的猎物。
在议会里、在报纸上，这兴许又会被欢呼为第三等级游侠骑士的一次大捷；唯有他自己知道这场败绩有多深、多苦。
多尔梅松先生早扑到他的委托人身边。
「您受伤了！」他傻乎乎地喊。
「不算什么，」拉图尔达齐尔说，「一点擦伤。」可他的嘴唇在抽搐，他那件细麻布好衬衫的袖子浸满了血。
多尔梅松是个干这类事的行家，掏出一方亚麻手帕，飞快斯成布条，权当绷带。
安德烈-路易仍旧站在那里，茫茫然看着，仿佛失了神。他这样站着，直到勒·夏普利耶碰碰他的胳膊，才终于醒转，叹了口气，转身去穿衣裳，再没有同方才的对手说话、看他一眼，径自离开了场地。
他同勒·夏普利耶一起，郁郁地慢慢朝林苑入口走去——马车停在那儿。半路，拉图尔达齐尔和他助手的那辆双轮篷车从他们身边驶过——那车原是几乎一直赶到决斗场边的。侯爵受伤的胳膊用他同伴的剑带临时吊着，天蓝色的三重领外衣扣在吊臂外面，右袖空空垂着。除此以外，除了脸色略白，他看上去同平常没什么两样。
现在您就明白他为什么头一个回来了；也就明白两位一心要拦下这场决斗的夫人，见他这样回来、显然安然无恙，为什么认定她们最坏的担心成了事实。
德·普卢加斯特尔夫人想喊，嗓子里却发不出声。她想推开自己车室的门；手指却在门把上徒劳地打滑。这时那辆篷车正慢慢驶过，拉图尔达齐尔那双俊眼里含着阴郁而专注的光，迎着她痛苦的凝望。随后她看见了别的：多尔梅松先生原本前倾着身子同伯爵夫人见礼，此刻向后一靠，露出了德·拉图尔达齐尔先生蓝色外衣那条空空的右袖。

===Original===

More, the near side of the coat itself turned back from the point near the throat where it was caught together by a single button, revealed the slung arm beneath in its blood-sodden cambric sleeve.
Even now she feared to jump to the obvious conclusion﻿—feared lest perhaps the Marquis, though himself wounded, might have dealt his adversary a deadlier wound.
She found her voice at last, and at the same moment signalled to the driver of the caleche to stop.
As it was pulled to a standstill, M. d’Ormesson alighted, and so met madame in the little space between the two carriages.
“Where is M. Moreau?” was the question with which she surprised him.
“Following at his leisure, no doubt, madame,” he answered, recovering.
“He is not hurt?”
“Unfortunately it is we who﻿ ﻿…” M. d’Ormesson was beginning, when from behind him M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s voice cut in crisply:
“This interest on your part in M. Moreau, dear Countess﻿ ﻿…”
He broke off, observing a vague challenge in the air with which she confronted him. But indeed his sentence did not need completing.
There was a vaguely awkward pause. And then she looked at M. d’Ormesson. Her manner changed. She offered what appeared to be an explanation of her concern for M. Moreau.
“Mademoiselle de Kercadiou is with me. The poor child has fainted.”
There was more, a deal more, she would have said just then, but for M. d’Ormesson’s presence.
Moved by a deep solicitude for Mademoiselle de Kercadiou, de La Tour d’Azyr sprang up despite his wound.
“I am in poor case to render assistance, madame,” he said, an apologetic smile on his pale face. “But﻿ ﻿…”
With the aid of d’Ormesson, and in spite of the latter’s protestations, he got down from the caleche, which then moved on a little way, so as to leave the road clear﻿—for another carriage that was approaching from the direction of the Bois.
And thus it happened that when a few moments later that approaching cabriolet overtook and passed the halted vehicles, André-Louis beheld a very touching scene. Standing up to obtain a better view, he saw Aline in a half-swooning condition﻿—she was beginning to revive by now﻿—seated in the doorway of the carriage, supported by Mme. de Plougastel. In an attitude of deepest concern, M. de La Tour d’Azyr, his wound notwithstanding, was bending over the girl, whilst behind him stood M. d’Ormesson and madame’s footman.

更有甚者，外衣的近身一侧从领口那颗单独扣住的地方翻开来，露出底下吊着的那条胳膊——细麻布袖子浸得透湿，全是血。
即使这时她仍不敢往那明白不过的结论上跳——她怕，怕侯爵虽然自己受了伤，也许给了对手更致命的一击。
她终于找回声音，同时打手势叫篷车停下。
车被拉停，多尔梅松先生下了车，在两辆车之间那小块空地上迎着夫人。
「莫罗先生在哪儿？」她劈头一问，问得他一怔。
「多半在后头从容跟着呢，夫人。」他缓过神来答。
「他没受伤吧？」
「不巧的是我们……」多尔梅松先生刚开口，德·拉图尔达齐尔先生的声音从他背后干脆地插进来：
「您对莫罗先生这份关切，亲爱的伯爵夫人……」
他顿住了，察觉她迎上来的神色里含着一点无名的挑战。其实他那句话也无需说完。
有一阵莫名尴尬的沉默。随后她望向多尔梅松先生，神色一变，为她对莫罗先生的关切给出了一番交代。
「德·凯尔卡迪乌小姐同我在一起。可怜的孩子晕过去了。」
还有更多、多得得多的话，她本想当场说，只是碍着多尔梅松先生在场。
出于对德·凯尔卡迪乌小姐深切的关怀，拉图尔达齐尔不顾伤，一跃而起。
「我这副样子帮不上忙了，夫人，」他说，苍白的脸上带着歉意的笑，「不过……」
他不顾多尔梅松的劝阻，由他扶着，下了篷车；篷车随即往前挪了一段，把路让开——给另一辆正从林苑方向驶来的马车。
于是，几分钟后，当那辆驶来的轻便双轮马车追上并驶过停着的车辆时，安德烈-路易目睹了一幅非常动人的景象。他站起身来望得更清楚：阿琳半昏迷地——这时已开始苏醒——坐在车门口，由德·普卢加斯特尔夫人扶着；德·拉图尔达齐尔先生不顾伤势，俯身向那姑娘，一副最最深切关怀的姿态；他身后站着多尔梅松先生和夫人的跟班。

===Original===

The Countess looked up and saw him as he was driven past. Her face lighted; almost it seemed to him she was about to greet him or to call him, wherefore, to avoid a difficulty, arising out of the presence there of his late antagonist, he anticipated her by bowing frigidly﻿—for his mood was frigid, the more frigid by virtue of what he saw﻿—and then resumed his seat with eyes that looked deliberately ahead.
Could anything more completely have confirmed him in his conviction that it was on M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s account that Aline had come to plead with him that morning? For what his eyes had seen, of course, was a lady overcome with emotion at the sight of blood of her dear friend, and that same dear friend restoring her with assurances that his hurt was very far from mortal. Later, much later, he was to blame his own perverse stupidity. Almost is he too severe in his self-condemnation. For how else could he have interpreted the scene he beheld, his preconceptions being what they were?
That which he had already been suspecting, he now accounted proven to him. Aline had been wanting in candour on the subject of her feelings towards M. de La Tour d’Azyr. It was, he supposed, a woman’s way to be secretive in such matters, and he must not blame her. Nor could he blame her in his heart for having succumbed to the singular charm of such a man as the Marquis﻿—for not even his hostility could blind him to M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s attractions. That she had succumbed was betrayed, he thought, by the weakness that had overtaken her upon seeing him wounded.
“My God!” he cried aloud. “What must she have suffered, then, if I had killed him as I intended!”
If only she had used candour with him, she could so easily have won his consent to the thing she asked. If only she had told him what now he saw, that she loved M. de La Tour d’Azyr, instead of leaving him to assume her only regard for the Marquis to be based on unworthy worldly ambition, he would at once have yielded.
He fetched a sigh, and breathed a prayer for forgiveness to the shade of Vilmorin.
“It is perhaps as well that my lunge went wide,” he said.
“What do you mean?” wondered Le Chapelier.
“That in this business I must relinquish all hope of recommencing.”

===Chinese===

马车驶过时，伯爵夫人抬头看见了他。她的脸亮了；在他看来，她几乎要招呼他、唤他；因此，为免在场有方才对手而起的尴尬，他抢先冷冷一躬——他的心绪正是冷的，眼见的一切叫它更冷——随后坐回去，眼睛故意望着前方。
还有什么能比这更彻底地证实他的信念呢：阿琳那天早晨来求他，是为了拉图尔达齐尔先生。他眼睛看见的，自然是一位夫人见到亲爱的朋友见了血、伤心欲绝，而那位亲爱的朋友正连声安慰她，保证自己伤得远非致命。很久以后，很久很久以后，他才怨自己那份执拗的愚蠢。他对自己的责罚，几乎又太重了些。因为，凭着他那些先入之见，眼前这景象，叫他还能怎样解释呢？
他早已疑心的，此刻在他看来已是铁证。在对待拉图尔达齐尔先生的感情上，阿琳不曾坦白。他想，女人在这种事上讳莫如深，本是天性，他不能怪她。他心里也不能怪她被这样一位男子独特的魅力征服——因为连他的敌意也无法叫他对拉图尔达齐尔先生的魅力闭眼。她被征服了——他以为，见到他受伤便支持不住，就是明证。
「我的上帝！」他出声喊，「倘若我照本意杀了他，她该受多大的苦啊！」
只要她当初对他坦白，要他答应她求的事，原是轻而易举。只要她告诉他如今他看见的一切——她爱德·拉图尔达齐尔先生——而不是由着他认定，她对侯爵的心思不过是出于卑劣的世俗图谋，他早就一口应允了。
他长叹一声，向维尔莫兰的亡灵祷求宽恕。
「我那一刺刺偏了，也许倒好。」他说。
「什么意思？」勒·夏普利耶不解。
「意思是，这桩事里，我必须断掉一切重起的指望。」

## IV Interlude / 四 幕间
===Original===

A few days later Le Chapelier returned André Louis’ visit. He came to the Rue du Hasard with definite news that all was well at Gavrillac, and that the people of M. de Kercadiou had taken no part in the recent provincial disturbances, now happily quelled.
And now, save that the pinch of want was still being felt by the poor, and that the queues outside the bakers’ shops increased as the autumn advanced, life resumed its habitual course. Naturally there were constant explosions of feeling in Paris, but the Parisians were becoming accustomed to living in an explosive atmosphere, and they no longer permitted it seriously to interfere with their affairs and their amusements. Even those explosions might have been avoided, but that Privilege determined to fight to the last ditch, still offered a stubborn resistance on one side, even whilst on the other it was already flinging great offerings on the altar of the fatherland. In the coming of the regiment of Flanders to Versailles in September the people saw a new menace, perceived in it a sign that Privilege was rearing again its hideous greedy head. There was a plot afoot to coerce them, to starve them at need into submission. Hence that excursion, so-called of Maenads, that march to Versailles of the market-women of Paris, led by the unique Maillard, and as an outcome in early October, the Palace of the Tuileries was cleared of all the vermin, human and otherwise, that infested it, to make room for the King. The King was to come and live among his people. His loving people desired to have him in their midst﻿—as a hostage for their own safety. If they must starve, he should starve with them.
André-Louis observed these things, and began to wonder where they would end. The only sane nobles appeared to him to be those who had crossed the frontier before the hotheads constituting the large majority of their party should have brought destruction upon the whole class. Meanwhile, he, himself, was kept busy in his flourishing academy; so busy that he contemplated acquiring the ground-floor of No. 13 and a third assistant; he was restrained, however, by the impossibility of inducing the ground-floor tenant, a haberdasher who throve on the custom of the fencing-school upstairs, to evacuate such advantageous premises.
With that exception the whole of No. 13 was now in his hands.

===Chinese===

几天后，勒·夏普利耶回访了安德烈-路易。他到阿扎尔街（Rue du Hasard）来，带来确切的消息：加夫里雅克（Gavrillac）一切安好，德·凯尔卡迪乌先生的人手没有卷入外省近来那场骚乱——如今所幸已经平息。
如今，除了穷人仍尝着匮乏的苦头、面包铺门前的长队随着秋深一天天见长之外，生活又回到了惯常的轨道。巴黎自然还是不断地爆发情绪，可巴黎人已渐渐过惯了这种一点就着的空气，不再容许它当真搅扰自己的正事和消遣。其实连那些爆发也原可免掉，怎奈特权阶级存心负隅顽抗到底，一面在一边咬牙死拒，一面却已忙着往祖国的祭坛上大把大把地供献祭品。九月里佛兰德斯军团开到凡尔赛，人民从中看出了新的威胁，看出一个迹象：特权阶级又扬起了它那丑恶而贪婪的头。有阴谋在酝酿，要胁迫他们，必要时用饥饿逼他们屈服。于是有了那一场号称酒神女祭的出征——巴黎的市场妇人在独一无二的马亚尔率领下向凡尔赛进军；其结果是十月初，杜伊勒里宫里外一切害虫——人的、非人的——被扫荡一空，腾出地方来迎驾国王。国王要来住在人民中间。他可爱的人民要把他留在自己当中——当作担保自身安全的人质。倘若大家必须挨饿，他也得跟着挨饿。
安德烈-路易冷眼看着这一切，开始纳闷这一切收场收在哪里。在他看来，头脑清楚的贵族只有一种：趁着一班鲁莽灭裂之徒——他们占本阶级的绝大多数——还没把整个阶级拖进毁灭，先就越过国境去了的那些人。这期间，他自己那家风生水起的剑术学校忙得他团团转；忙到他盘算着把十三号的底层也盘下来，再添第三名助教；只是无法可想——楼下的底租客是家针线杂货铺，全指着楼上剑术学校的主顾过活，怎么也不肯腾出这样一块风水宝地。
除去这一层，十三号整幢如今都在他手里。

===Original===

He had lately acquired the first floor, converting it into a comfortable dwelling for himself and his two assistants, installing a housekeeper and a boy as page.
Now that the National Assembly sat in Paris he frequently saw Le Chapelier, and the intimacy grew between them. They commonly dined together at the Palais Royal or elsewhere, and through Le Chapelier André-Louis began to make a few friends; but he avoided the salons to which he was freely invited, those salons in which the fine new republican and philosophic spirit presided.
He was present at the Comédie Française one night in the following spring when Chénier’s *Charles IX* was being performed more or less under protest.
It was a stormy evening. Allusions from the stage were taken up now by one section, now by another, of the audience, to be used as catchwords between the politically hostile parties between the old regime and the new. The climax was ludicrously provided by some men in the pit, who insisted upon remaining covered. The Comédie Française contained a royal box, and it was an unwritten law that respect for royalty should go the length of demanding bared heads even when this royal box was untenanted.
The men who chose to remain covered did so as a republican protest against what they considered an empty sham. But, before the storm that arose when their action was denounced, before an uproar that rendered inaudible a word from the stage, they made haste to beat a retreat from their republican arrogance﻿—with one exception. One man stubbornly remained covered, and turned about his broad leonine head to laugh at those who demanded that he should take off his hat, his great voice trumpeting through the house: “Do you imagine that you can force me to take off my hat?”
It was the last provocation. Menaces were hurled at him. He rose to them undaunted, heaved himself up, displaying an enormous athletic frame, a Herculean neck bare to the breast below his head, an unspeakably hideous countenance. He laughed in their faces. He pressed his round headgear down more securely about his brows.
“Firm as the hat of Servandony!” he mocked them, and flourished an arm in defiance.
André-Louis laughed. There was something at once grotesque and magnificently heroic about that great figure, mocking and undaunted amid the ever increasing uproar.

===Chinese===

不久前他又盘下了二楼，改造成他和两名助教的舒适居所，雇了一位管家妇，又雇了个孩子当小听差。
国民议会既已移驻巴黎，他同勒·夏普利耶常见面了，两人的交情日见其深。他们常在王宫花园或别处一同用饭；经勒·夏普利耶引路，安德烈-路易也结识了几个朋友。可人家殷勤相邀的沙龙，他一概避而不去——那些由时髦的新共和精神和哲学精神做主人的沙龙。
第二年春天的一个晚上，他在法兰西喜剧院（Comédie Française），正赶上谢尼耶的《查理九世》多少顶着抗议在上演。
那是个多风暴的夜晚。台上的影射之词，观众这一派接过去，那一派接过去，当作旧制度与新制度这两个敌对阵营互相叫阵的口号。戏的高潮却来得滑稽：池座里几个人硬是不肯摘帽子。法兰西喜剧院设有一间御用包厢，有条不成文的规矩：敬重王室要敬到哪怕御包厢空着，也人人得光着头。
那几个存心不摘帽子的，是拿共和党的姿态，抗议他们眼中那套空洞的虚礼。可他们的行径一被喝破，风暴顿起，满场哗然，台上一个字也听不见；他们慌忙从自己的共和气焰上败下阵来——只除了一人。那人死也不肯摘帽，转着那颗雄狮般的大脑袋，冲着喝令他脱帽的人哈哈大笑，洪亮的嗓门响彻全场：「你们当真以为，能逼我摘下帽子？」
这一下把人逼到了绝处。威吓劈头盖脸朝他砸去。他毫无惧色，应声而起，直起身来，露出魁梧绝伦的运动员身架，一颗硕大的头颅下，赫拉克勒斯式的脖颈赤裸到胸口，一张脸丑得没法形容。他冲着众人当面大笑，把那顶圆帽往额上又摁结实了些。
「稳如塞尔凡多尼的帽子！」他嘲弄他们，挥舞一条胳膊以示不服。
安德烈-路易笑了。这个大个子里有一种既荒诞又豪迈得堂堂正正的东西，在越来越高的喧嚣中嘲弄一切，毫无惧色。

===Original===

The affair might have ended badly had not the police intervened to arrest him and lead him out a prisoner. He was clearly not of those who yield.
“Who is he?” André-Louis asked of a neighbour, as the house was settling down again after the disorder.
“I don’t know,” he was answered. “I am told that his name is Danton, and that he is the president of the Cordeliers. He will come to a bad end, of course; madman, an original.”
Next day it was the talk of Paris, floating for a moment on the surface of more serious matter. In the fencing-academy nothing else was discussed but the Cômédie Française and the quarrel between Talma and Naudet that was at the root of all the mischief. But soon André-Louis had something else to engage his attention. He received a visit from Le Chapelier just before noon.
“I have news for you, André. Your godfather is at Meudon. He arrived there two days ago. Had you heard?”
“But no. How should I hear? Why is he at Meudon?”
He was conscious of a faint excitement, which he could hardly have explained.
“I don’t know. There have been fresh disturbances in Brittany. It may be due to that.”
“And so he has come for shelter to his brother?” asked André-Louis.
“To his brother’s house, yes; but not to his brother. Where do you live at all, André? Do you never hear any of the news? Étienne de Gavrillac emigrated months ago. He was of the household of M. d’Artois, and he crossed the frontier with him. By now, no doubt, he is in Germany with him, conspiring against France. For that is what the émigrés are doing. That Austrian woman at the Tuileries will end by destroying the monarchy.”
“Yes, yes,” said André-Louis, impatiently. Politics interested him not at all this morning. “But about Gavrillac?”
“Why, haven’t I told you that Gavrillac is at Meudon, installed in the house his brother has left? *Dieu de Dieu!* Don’t I speak French? I believe that Rabouillet, his intendant, is in charge of Gavrillac. I have brought you the news the moment I received it. I thought you would probably wish to go out to Meudon.”
“Of course. I will go at once﻿—that is, as soon as I can. I can’t today, nor yet tomorrow. I am too busy here.” He waved a hand towards the inner room, where proceeded the click-click of blades, the quick moving of feet, and the voice of the instructor Le Duc.
“Well, well, this is your own affair. You are busy. I leave you now.

===Chinese===

若不是警察进来拿人，把他当囚犯押了出去，这事只怕要收不了场。他显然不是肯让步的那号人。
「他是谁？」乱子过后全场渐渐平复，安德烈-路易问旁边的人。
「不知道，」对方答道，「听说他叫丹东，是科德利埃的主席。他不会有好下场，这是自然的；疯子，一个怪物。」
第二天，这事成了巴黎的谈资，在更要紧的正事的面上浮了一小会儿。剑术学校里，大家议论的也无非是法兰西喜剧院，以及这场祸事根子上塔尔马同诺代的那场龃龉。可不久，安德烈-路易就有别的事分神了。晌午前，勒·夏普利耶来探访他。
「有消息给你，安德烈。你教父在默东（Meudon）。两天前到的。你听说了吗？」
「可没有。我从哪儿听说去？他到默东做什么？」
他觉出自己有点隐隐的兴奋，却也说不上来为什么。
「我不知道。布列塔尼又出了新的乱子。兴许是为这个。」
「所以他跑到他弟弟那儿避风头了？」安德烈-路易问。
「到他弟弟的宅子，不错；可不是投他弟弟。你到底住在哪个世界，安德烈？新闻你一条也不听吗？艾蒂安·德·加夫里雅克几个月前就流亡了。他原在德·阿图瓦先生手下当差，跟着他一起出的国境。这会儿，不用说，正跟着他在德国，一块儿密谋反对法国呢。流亡者（émigrés）干的就是这营生。杜伊勒里宫里那个奥地利女人，早晚要断送了君主制。」
「是，是，」安德烈-路易不耐烦地说。这天早晨，政治半点也提不起他的兴致。「加夫里雅克那边呢？」
「嗐，我不是说了吗，加夫里雅克老爷人在默东，住进了他弟弟留下的宅子。我的上帝（*Dieu de Dieu*）！我说的不是法国话吗？我想他那总管拉布耶眼下代管着加夫里雅克。消息一到手我就给你送来了。我想你八成愿意跑一趟默东。」
「当然。我这就去——就是说，一得空就去。今天不成，明天也不成。我这儿实在脱不开身。」他朝里间摆了摆手，那边剑刃咔哒咔哒响着，脚步轻快地移动，还传来教官勒·迪克的口令声。
「好好，这是你自己的事。你忙。我这走了。

===Original===

Let us dine this evening at the Café de Foy. Kersain will be of the party.”
“A moment!” André-Louis’ voice arrested him on the threshold. “Is Mademoiselle de Kercadiou with her uncle?”
“How the devil should I know? Go and find out!”
He was gone, and André-Louis stood there a moment deep in thought. Then he turned and went back to resume with his pupil, the Vicomte de Villeniort, the interrupted exposition of the demi-contre of Danet, illustrating with a small-sword the advantages to be derived from its adoption.
Thereafter he fenced with the Vicomte, who was perhaps the ablest of his pupils at the time, and all the while his thoughts were on the heights of Meudon, his mind casting up the lessons he had to give that afternoon and on the morrow, and wondering which of these he might postpone without deranging the academy. When having touched the Vicomte three times in succession, he paused and wrenched himself back to the present, it was to marvel at the precision to be gained by purely mechanical action. Without bestowing a thought upon what he was doing, his wrist and arms and knees had automatically performed their work, like the accurate fighting engine into which constant practice for a year and more had combined them.
Not until Sunday was André-Louis able to satisfy a wish that had meanwhile grown to the proportions of a yearning. Dressed with more than ordinary care, his head elegantly coiffed﻿—by one of those hairdressers to the nobility of whom so many were being thrown out of employment by the stream of emigration which was now flowing freely﻿—André-Louis mounted his hired carriage, and drove out to Meudon.
The house of the younger Kercadiou no more resembled that of the head of the family than did his person. A man of the Court, where his brother was essentially a man of the soil, an officer of the household of M. le Comte d’Artois, he had built for himself and his family an imposing villa on the heights of Meudon in a miniature park, conveniently situated for him midway between Versailles and Paris, and easily accessible from either. M. d’Artois﻿—the royal tennis-player﻿—had been amongst the very first to emigrate.

今晚咱们上福伊咖啡馆吃饭吧。凯尔桑也来。」
「慢着！」安德烈-路易的声音在门口把他叫住。「德·凯尔卡迪乌小姐同她叔叔一起吗？」
「鬼才知道！你自己去看嘛！」
他走了。安德烈-路易站在原地出了一会儿神，随后转身回去，接着同弟子德·维勒尼奥尔子爵讲解方才讲了一半的达内半反击，握着一柄小剑演示采用这一招的好处。
之后他同子爵过了剑。子爵在当时也许是他弟子中最有本事的一个；而他一路心里想的尽是默东高地，脑子里盘算着当天下午和第二天要上的课，琢磿哪几课可以挪一挪而不至搅乱了学校。等他一连三次刺中子爵，停下来把自己硬拽回眼前时，不禁惊异于纯机械的动作竟能如此精准。心思完全没放在手上，他的腕、臂、膝却自动各司其职，像一台精确的战斗机器——一年多不辍的练习，早已把它们锻成了一体。
直到星期日，安德烈-路易才得偿这桩心愿——它在这几天里已经长成了渴望。他穿戴得比往常格外讲究，头发也梳得漂亮——出自一位专为贵族做头发的理发师之手；这路人如今正成把成把地被滚滚的流亡潮抛进失业的堆里。随后他登上雇来的马车，出城往默东去。
小凯尔卡迪乌的宅子，同他这个人一样，一点不像族长那一房的样式。哥哥骨子里是个土里刨食的乡绅，他却是宫廷里的人，德·阿图瓦伯爵先生府上的属官；他在默东高地上、一座小巧的园林中间，为自己和家眷盖了一幢气派的别墅，位置于他正便利——恰在凡尔赛与巴黎中间，两头来去都近便。德·阿图瓦先生——那位王室网球迷——是最早流亡的那一批人里的一个。

===Original===

Together with the Condés, the Contis, the Polignacs, and others of the Queen’s intimate council, old Marshal de Broglie and the Prince de Lambesc, who realised that their very names had become odious to the people, he had quitted France immediately after the fall of the Bastille. He had gone to play tennis beyond the frontier﻿—and there consummate the work of ruining the French monarchy upon which he and those others had been engaged in France. With him, amongst several members of his household, went Étienne de Kercadiou, and with Étienne de Kercadiou went his family, a wife and four children. Thus it was that the Seigneur of Gavrillac, glad to escape from a province so peculiarly disturbed as that of Brittany﻿—where the nobles had shown themselves the most intransigent of all France﻿—had come to occupy in his brother’s absence the courtier’s handsome villa at Meudon.
That he was quite happy there is not to be supposed. A man of his almost Spartan habits, accustomed to plain fare and self-help, was a little uneasy in this sybaritic abode, with its soft carpets, profusion of gilding, and battalion of sleek, silent-footed servants﻿—for Kercadiou the younger had left his entire household behind. Time, which at Gavrillac he had kept so fully employed in agrarian concerns, here hung heavily upon his hands. In self-defence he slept a great deal, and but for Aline, who made no attempt to conceal her delight at this proximity to Paris and the heart of things, it is possible that he would have beat a retreat almost at once from surroundings that sorted so ill with his habits. Later on, perhaps, he would accustom himself and grow resigned to this luxurious inactivity. In the meantime the novelty of it fretted him, and it was into the presence of a peevish and rather somnolent M. de Kercadiou that André-Louis was ushered in the early hours of the afternoon of that Sunday in June.

巴士底陷落之后，他立刻同孔代家、孔蒂家、波利尼亚克家，还有王后心腹圈子里的其他人，以及老元帅德·布罗伊和朗贝斯克亲王——这两人心里明白，连自己的名字都已叫人民深恶痛绝——一道离开了法国。他到国境那边打网球去了——并在那边，把他和那帮人在法国接手的败坏法国君主国的营生，做个了结。他府上属员好几个随他同行，其中就有艾蒂安·德·凯尔卡迪乌；艾蒂安·德·凯尔卡迪乌又携了家眷——一位太太，四个孩子。就这样，加夫里雅克领主乐得躲开布列塔尼这样一个乱得格外邪乎的省份——那儿贵族们的不妥协在全法国数第一——趁弟弟不在，住进了这位廷臣在默东的漂亮别墅。
要说他在那儿过得十分快活，那可不能指望。他这个人几乎过着斯巴达式的日子，吃惯了粗茶淡饭，事事亲力亲为；在这座骄奢的宅子里——软地毯，满眼的描金，一支脚步轻悄、油光水滑的仆役大军（小凯尔卡迪乌把全副家仆都摺在了此地）——他浑身不自在。在加夫里雅克，他把光阴全打发在田庄的事上；在这里，光阴却沉沉地压在手上。为求自保，他大睡其觉。若不是阿琳——她毫不掩饰自己的欢喜，这里离巴黎、离一切的心脏这样近——他恐怕几乎立刻就要从这同他习惯如此不合的环境里败退回去。再往后，他兴许会渐渐惯了，会对这种奢华的无所事事认了命。眼下的新鲜劲儿还磨得他心烦。六月那个星期日的午后头几个钟头，安德烈-路易被领进去见到的德·凯尔卡迪乌先生，正是这么一位烦躁易怒、睡眼惺忪的老爷。

## VI Madame de Plougastel / 六 德·普卢加斯特尔夫人
===Original===

The postilion drew rein, and the footman opened the door, letting down the steps and proffering his arm to his mistress to assist her to alight, since that was the wish she had expressed. Then he opened one wing of the iron gates, and held it for her. She was a woman of something more than forty, who once must have been very lovely, who was very lovely still with the refining quality that age brings to some women. Her dress and carriage alike advertised great rank.
“I take my leave here, since you have a visitor,” said André-Louis.
“But it is an old acquaintance of your own, André. You remember Mme. la Comtesse de Plougastel?”
He looked at the approaching lady, whom Aline was now hastening forward to meet, and because she was named to him he recognized her. He must, he thought, had he but looked, have recognized her without prompting anywhere at any time, and this although it was some sixteen years since last he had seen her. The sight of her now brought it all back to him﻿—a treasured memory that had never permitted itself to be entirely overlaid by subsequent events.
When he was a boy of ten, on the eve of being sent to school at Rennes, she had come on a visit to his godfather, who was her cousin. It happened that at the time he was taken by Rabouillet to the Manor of Gavrillac, and there he had been presented to Mme. de Plougastel. The great lady, in all the glory then of her youthful beauty, with her gentle, cultured voice﻿—so cultured that she had seemed to speak a language almost unknown to the little Breton lad﻿—and her majestic air of the great world, had scared him a little at first. Very gently had she allayed those fears of his, and by some mysterious enchantment she had completely enslaved his regard. He recalled now the terror in which he had gone to the embrace to which he was bidden, and the subsequent reluctance with which he had left those soft round arms. He remembered, too, how sweetly she had smelled and the very perfume she had used, a perfume as of lilac﻿—for memory is singularly tenacious in these matters.
For three days whilst she had been at Gavrillac, he had gone daily to the manor, and so had spent hours in her company. A childless woman with the maternal instinct strong within her, she had taken this precociously intelligent, wide-eyed lad to her heart.

车夫勒住缰绳，仆役打开车门，放下踏板，伸出手臂扶女主人下车——这是她吩咐过的。随后他推开铁门的一扇，替她扶着。这是位四十出头的妇人，从前想必非常美丽，如今依然美丽，带着岁月赋予某些女人的那种愈磨愈醇的风韵。她的衣着和仪态，无不显出尊贵的门第。
“既有客来，我就此告辞了。”安德烈-路易说。
“可来的正是你的旧识呀，安德烈。你总记得德·普卢加斯特尔伯爵夫人（Mme. la Comtesse de Plougastel）吧？”
他望着走近的夫人——阿琳（Aline）正快步迎上去——经人一提，他认出了她。他想，哪怕无人提醒，只要看上一眼，无论何时何地他也必定认得出她，虽说上一回见她，已是大约十六年前了。此刻一见到她，旧事全涌了回来——那是一段珍藏的记忆，从不曾容许后来的种种把它整个压没。
他十岁那年，正要被送去雷恩（Rennes）上学，她来探望她的堂兄——他的教父。赶巧那时拉布耶（Rabouillet）把他带到了加夫里雅克庄院，就在那里，他被引见给德·普卢加斯特尔夫人。那位贵妇人，正当青春韶华的极盛，嗓音温柔文雅——文雅得在这布列塔尼小儿郎听来，她说的几乎是一种他全然不懂的语言——再加上她那大世家的雍容气派，起初叫他有点怯。她温温柔柔地消了他的怕，又不知施了什么迷人的法术，把他的心整个儿收服了。他此刻还记得，奉命上前拥抱她时自己是何等战战兢兢，过后离开那双绵软圆润的手臂时又是何等恋恋不舍。他还记得她身上的香气何等甜美，记得她用的正是哪一种香水——一种丁香似的香气——记忆在这些事上，真是出奇地牢。
她在加夫里雅克盘桓的三天里，他天天往庄院跑，就这样在她身边消磨了几个钟头又一个钟头。她是个没有孩子的女人，母性的本能正当旺盛，便把这个早慧的、瞪着一双大眼睛的孩子搂进了心里。

===Original===

“Give him to me, Cousin Quintin,” he remembered her saying on the last of those days to his godfather. “Let me take him back with me to Versailles as my adopted child.”
But the Seigneur had gravely shaken his head in silent refusal, and there had been no further question of such a thing. And then, when she said goodbye to him﻿—the thing came flooding back to him now﻿—there had been tears in her eyes.
“Think of me sometimes, André-Louis,” had been her last words.
He remembered how flattered he had been to have won within so short a time the affection of this great lady. The thing had given him a sense of importance that had endured for months thereafter, finally to fade into oblivion.
But all was vividly remembered now upon beholding her again, after sixteen years, profoundly changed and matured, the girl﻿—for she had been no more in those old days﻿—sunk in this worldly woman with the air of calm dignity and complete self-possession. Yet, he insisted, he must have known her anywhere again.
Aline embraced her affectionately, and then answering the questioning glance with faintly raised eyebrows that madame was directing towards Aline’s companion﻿—
“This is André-Louis,” she said. “You remember André-Louis, madame?”
Madame checked. André-Louis saw the surprise ripple over her face, taking with it some of her colour, leaving her for a moment breathless.
And then the voice﻿—the well-remembered rich, musical voice﻿—richer and deeper now than of yore, repeated his name:
“André-Louis!”
Her manner of uttering it suggested that it awakened memories, memories perhaps of the departed youth with which it was associated. And she paused a long moment, considering him, a little wide-eyed, what time he bowed before her.
“But of course I remember him,” she said at last, and came towards him, putting out her hand. He kissed it dutifully, submissively, instinctively. “And this is what you have grown into?” She appraised him, and he flushed with pride at the satisfaction in her tone. He seemed to have gone back sixteen years, and to be again the little Breton lad at Gavrillac. She turned to Aline. “How mistaken Quintin was in his assumptions. He was pleased to see him again, was he not?”
“So pleased, madame, that he has shown me the door,” said André-Louis.
“Ah!” She frowned, conning him still with those dark, wistful eyes of hers. “We must change that, Aline.

可十六年后再见她，一切又历历如在眼前。她已深深变了、成熟了——当年她其实还只是个少女——那少女沉没了，融在这位沉着高贵、稳如磐石的交际场妇人身上。然而，他认定，无论在哪里再见，他都认得出她。
阿琳亲亲热热拥抱了她，随后应答着夫人投向阿琳同伴的那询问的一瞥——眉梢微微挑起——
“这是安德烈-路易，”她说。“您记得安德烈-路易的吧，夫人？”
夫人怔住了。安德烈-路易看见惊愕从她脸上漾过，带走了几分血色，有一瞬叫她喘不上气来。
随后，那个嗓音——记忆里那丰润如乐的嗓音——比往昔更丰润、更深沉了，念出了他的名字：
“安德烈-路易！”
她念这名字的口气，表明它唤醒了记忆——兴许是关于那段一去不返的青春的记忆，这名字正是同它连在一起的。她定定打量了他好一会儿，眼睛微微睁大，他则在她面前躬身行礼。
“我当然记得他，”她终于说，朝他走过来，伸出手。他恭恭敬敬、服服帖帖、出于本能地吻了它。“你就长成了这副模样？”她端详着他，她话音里的满意叫他得意得红了脸。他仿佛退回了十六年，又是加夫里雅克那个布列塔尼小家儿了。她转向阿琳。“昆坦的猜想错得多离谱。他见了他是高兴的，是不是？”

===Original===

He is of course very angry with you. But it is not the way to make converts. I will plead for you, André-Louis. I am a good advocate.”
He thanked her and took his leave.
“I leave my case in your hands with gratitude. My homage, madame.”
And so it happened that in spite of his godfather’s forbidding reception of him, the fragment of a song was on his lips as his yellow chaise whirled him back to Paris and the Rue du Hasard. That meeting with Mme. de Plougastel had enheartened him; her promise to plead his case in alliance with Aline gave him assurance that all would be well.
That he was justified of this was proved when on the following Thursday towards noon his academy was invaded by M. de Kercadiou. Gilles, the boy, brought him word of it, and breaking off at once the lesson upon which he was engaged, he pulled off his mask, and went as he was﻿—in a chamois waistcoat buttoned to the chin and with his foil under his arm to the modest salon below, where his godfather awaited him.
The florid little Lord of Gavrillac stood almost defiantly to receive him.
“I have been over-persuaded to forgive you,” he announced aggressively, seeming thereby to imply that he consented to this merely so as to put an end to tiresome importunities.
André-Louis was not misled. He detected a pretence adopted by the Seigneur so as to enable him to retreat in good order.
“My blessings on the persuaders, whoever they may have been. You restore me my happiness, monsieur my godfather.”
He took the hand that was proffered and kissed it, yielding to the impulse of the unfailing habit of his boyish days. It was an act symbolical of his complete submission, reestablishing between himself and his godfather the bond of protected and protector, with all the mutual claims and duties that it carries. No mere words could more completely have made his peace with this man who loved him.
M. de Kercadiou’s face flushed a deeper pink, his lip trembled, and there was a huskiness in the voice that murmured “My dear boy!” Then he recollected himself, threw back his great head and frowned. His voice resumed its habitual shrillness. “You realize, I hope, that you have behaved damnably﻿ ﻿… damnably, and with the utmost ingratitude?”
“Does not that depend upon the point of view?” quoth André-Louis, but his tone was studiously conciliatory.
“It depends upon a fact, and not upon any point of view.

他自然是生你的气生得很。可这不是收服人心的法子。我来替你说情，安德烈-路易。我是个好辩士。”
他谢过她，告辞了。
“我这桩案子就感恩托付您了。敬颂崇仰，夫人。”
就这样，虽然教父给他的见面礼是一副冷面，可当那辆黄篷车把他载回巴黎、载回阿扎尔街时，他唇边竟挂着半支歌。同德·普卢加斯特尔夫人的这番重逢给他壮了胆；她应承联合阿琳替他说情，他于是笃定，一切都会好起来。
他这份笃定没有落空——下个星期四临近中午，德·凯尔卡迪乌先生闯进了他的剑术学校。小听差吉勒来报信，他当即掠下正在上的课，摘下面罩，就那么一身——羚羊皮背心直扣到下巴，腋下夹着花剑——下楼到朴素的客厅去，教父正在那儿等他。
红光满面的小个子加夫里雅克领主迎着他站起来，那架势几乎是挑衅的。
“我是被磨得没法子，才饶了你。”他气势汹汹地宣布，言下之意仿佛是：他之所以依了，不过为了摆脱那纠缠不清的烦扰。
安德烈-路易没有受蒙蔽。他看穿了：这是领主给自己搭的台阶，好体体面面地收兵。
“劝的人不管是哪几位，都愿上天降福给他们。您这是把我的幸福还给了我，教父先生。”
他握住递来的手，吻了它——少年时代那难改的习惯一时涌上来。这是彻底降心相从的表示，把他同教父之间受庇护者与庇护人的纽带重新结上，连同这条纽带带来的一切相互的权利和义务。单靠言语，无论如何不能像这样同这个爱他的人言归于好。
德·凯尔卡迪乌先生的脸涨得更红了，嘴唇发颤，那声喃喃的“我亲爱的孩子！”带着沙哑。随后他回过神来，把大脑袋往后一仰，皱起眉，嗓音又回到了惯常的尖利：“想必你自己明白，你行事混账……混账透顶，还负义到了极处？”
“这难道不取决于看问题的角度么？”安德烈-路易应道，口吻却刻意求和。
“它取决于事实，不取决于什么角度。
