侯爵的目光从维尔莫兰移到骑士身上，骑士又笑了，这回还拍起大腿来。
「我想，」拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵慢吞吞地说，「咱们说的不是一回事。我请您到这里来，是因为加夫里雅克庄院实在不是把咱们的争论继续下去的合适地方，我又不愿劳您大驾、叫您一路跑到阿齐尔来。可我的目的，跟您在那上头漏出的某些话有关。正是关于那些话，先生，我才要再听听您的高见——如果您肯赏脸。」
安德烈-路易开始觉出空气里有几分凶险。他这人直觉极快，远快过维尔莫兰先生——后者此刻流露的不过是一点淡淡的惊讶。
「我糊涂了，先生，」他说，「先生指的是哪些话？」
「看来，先生，我得替您把记性唤醒了。」侯爵跷起腿，连人带椅侧转过来，这才正面对着维尔莫兰先生。「您说过，先生——且不论您错到什么地步，您说得极有口才，依我看几乎口才过了头——说对那偷猎的家伙马贝，不管他姓甚名谁，处以此种即决的刑罚，是桩无耻的行径。无耻，正是您用的字眼。等我荣幸地奉告您，我的猎场看守贝内（Benet）是奉我的号令行事，您也没有收回那个字眼。」
「倘若，」维尔莫兰先生说

===Original===

de Vilmorin, “the deed was infamous, its infamy is not modified by the rank, however exalted, of the person responsible. Rather is it aggravated.”
“Ah!” said M. le Marquis, and drew a gold snuffbox from his pocket. “You say, ‘if the deed was infamous,’ monsieur. Am I to understand that you are no longer as convinced as you appeared to be of its infamy?”
M. de Vilmorin’s fine face wore a look of perplexity. He did not understand the drift of this.
“It occurs to me, M. le Marquis, in view of your readiness to assume responsibility, that you must believe justification for the deed which is not apparent to myself.”
“That is better. That is distinctly better.” The Marquis took snuff delicately, dusting the fragments from the fine lace at his throat. “You realize that with an imperfect understanding of these matters, not being yourself a landowner, you may have rushed to unjustifiable conclusions. That is indeed the case. May it be a warning to you, monsieur. When I tell you that for months past I have been annoyed by similar depredations, you will perhaps understand that it had become necessary to employ a deterrent sufficiently strong to put an end to them. Now that the risk is known, I do not think there will be any more prowling in my coverts. And there is more in it than that, M. de Vilmorin. It is not the poaching that annoys me so much as the contempt for my absolute and inviolable rights. There is, monsieur, as you cannot fail to have observed, an evil spirit of insubordination in the air, and there is one only way in which to meet it. To tolerate it, in however slight a degree, to show leniency, however leniently disposed, would entail having recourse to still harsher measures tomorrow. You understand me, I am sure, and you will also, I am sure, appreciate the condescension of what amounts to an explanation from me where I cannot admit that any explanations were due. If anything in what I have said is still obscure to you, I refer you to the game laws, which your lawyer friend there will expound for you at need.”
With that the gentleman swung round again to face the fire. It appeared to convey the intimation that the interview was at an end. And yet this was not by any means the intimation that it conveyed to the watchful, puzzled, vaguely uneasy André-Louis. It was, thought he, a very curious, a very suspicious oration.

===Chinese===

「那行径无耻，那么任凭肇事者地位何等尊贵，也无损其无耻分毫。反倒更变本加厉。」
「啊！」侯爵先生说，从口袋里掏出一只金鼻烟盒。「您说『倘若那行径无耻』，先生。我是否可以理解为，您不再像先前表现的那样，确信它无耻了？」
维尔莫兰先生那张俊脸浮起一层困惑。他不明白这话的用意。
「我想到，侯爵先生，既然您这样乐于承揽责任，您必定相信这行径自有其道理，只是那道理我尚未看得明白。」
「这就对了。这就明显对了。」侯爵优雅地取了一撮鼻烟，掸去落在领口细花边上的碎末。「您明白了：您对这类事情了解不深——您毕竟不是田产主——也许仓促得出了站不住的结论。事实正是如此。愿这事给您一个教训，先生。等我告诉您，几个月来我一直为类似的盗猎所扰，您或许就明白，采用一种足够严厉的惩戒手段来根绝它们，已属必要。如今风险既已人人皆知，我想不会再有人到我的猎苑里来打转了。这还不算完，维尔莫兰先生。叫我恼火的与其说是盗猎，不如说是对我绝对而不容侵犯的权利的轻慢。先生，空气里有一股忤逆的邪气，这一点您不可能不曾察觉；对付它只有一条路子。姑息它，姑息到一星半点；施以宽仁，任凭您宅心怎样仁厚——都免得明天不得不动用更严酷的手段。您明白我的意思，我确信；您也必定领情，我这一番近乎解释的话，实属屈尊——我本不承认有解释的必要。我这一番话里若还有什么您听不明白的，请去查猎场法，您那位律师朋友自会按需替您讲解。」
说完，这位老爷又转回去对火了。这话似乎在示意会见已毕。可在留神观着、困惑而不安的安德烈-路易听来，它传给他的远不是这个意思。这一篇演说，他想，透着古怪，透着可疑。

===Original===

It affected to explain, with a politeness of terms and a calculated insolence of tone; whilst in fact it could only serve to stimulate and goad a man of M. de Vilmorin’s opinions. And that is precisely what it did. He rose.
“Are there in the world no laws but game laws?” he demanded, angrily. “Have you never by any chance heard of the laws of humanity?”
The Marquis sighed wearily. “What have I to do with the laws of humanity?” he wondered.
M. de Vilmorin looked at him a moment in speechless amazement.
“Nothing, M. le Marquis. That is﻿—alas!﻿—too obvious. I hope you will remember it in the hour when you may wish to appeal to those laws which you now deride.”
M. de La Tour d’Azyr threw back his head sharply, his high-bred face imperious.
“Now what precisely shall that mean? It is not the first time today that you have made use of dark sayings that I could almost believe to veil the presumption of a threat.”
“Not a threat, M. le Marquis﻿—a warning. A warning that such deeds as these against God’s creatures﻿ ﻿… Oh, you may sneer, monsieur, but they are God’s creatures, even as you or I﻿—neither more nor less, deeply though the reflection may wound your pride, In His eyes﻿ ﻿…”
“Of your charity, spare me a sermon, M. l’abbé!”
“You mock, monsieur. You laugh. Will you laugh, I wonder, when God presents His reckoning to you for the blood and plunder with which your hands are full?”
“Monsieur!” The word, sharp as the crack of a whip, was from M. de Chabrillane, who bounded to his feet. But instantly the Marquis repressed him.
“Sit down, Chevalier. You are interrupting M. l’abbé, and I should like to hear him further. He interests me profoundly.”
In the background André-Louis, too, had risen, brought to his feet by alarm, by the evil that he saw written on the handsome face of M. de La Tour d’Azyr. He approached, and touched his friend upon the arm.
“Better be going, Philippe,” said he.
But M. de Vilmorin, caught in the relentless grip of passions long repressed, was being hurried by them recklessly along.
“Oh, monsieur,” said he, “consider what you are and what you will be. Consider how you and your kind live by abuses, and consider the harvest that abuses must ultimately bring.”
“Revolutionist!” said M. le Marquis, contemptuously. “You have the effrontery to stand before my face and offer me this stinking cant of your modern so-called intellectuals!”

===Chinese===

它摆出解释的架势，措辞客气，腔调里却藏着算计好的侮慢；而实际上，它只可能刺激、撩拨维尔莫兰先生这样见解的人。它也正起了这个作用。他站起身来。
「难道世上除了猎场法就没有别的法了？」他怒冲冲地喝问。「您就从来没有听说过——哪怕碰巧——人道的法律么？」
侯爵倦怠地叹了口气。「人道的法律，与我何干？」他纳罕道。
维尔莫兰先生一言不发地瞪了他半晌，惊得说不出话。
「与您无关，侯爵先生。这——唉！——再明白不过了。我只愿您记住：有朝一日您想诉求这些如今被您讥笑的法律时，会想起这句话。」
拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵猛地一仰头，那张高贵的脸透着专横。
「这话究竟是什么意思？今天您已经不是头一回了，用这些阴话，我几乎可以相信那底下藏着威胁的狂妄。」
「不是威胁，侯爵先生——是警告。警告您：这等残害上帝生灵的行径……哦，您尽可以嗤笑，先生，可他们正是上帝的生灵，同您同我一模一样——不多也不少，尽管这一想会刺痛您的骄傲。在上帝眼里……」
「行行好，饶了我一篇布道吧，教士先生！」
「您讥笑，先生。您笑。我不知道，等上帝找您清算满手的血污和掠夺时，您可还笑得出来？」
「放肆！」这一声像鞭梢炸响，出自夏布里扬先生之口，他一跃而起。可侯爵立刻按住了他。
「坐下，骑士。您打断了教士先生，我还想再听他说呢。他引起我极大的兴趣。」
在后面，安德烈-路易也站了起来——是惊惧把他带起来的，他看见拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵那张俊脸上写着恶意。他走上前，碰了碰朋友的手臂。
「还是走吧，菲利普。」他说。
可维尔莫兰先生被长期压抑的激情紧紧攥住，正由着它们鲁莽地拖着往前冲。
「哦，先生，」他说，「想想您是什么人，将会落个什么下场。想想您和您这一类人是靠种种弊端过活的，再想想弊端最终要结出什么收成。」
「造反派！」侯爵先生轻蔑地说。「您竟有脸皮站在我面前，向我兜售您那些所谓现代知识分子的腐臭滥调！」

===Original===

“Is it cant, monsieur? Do you think﻿—do you believe in your soul﻿—that it is cant? Is it cant that the feudal grip is on all things that live, crushing them like grapes in the press, to its own profit? Does it not exercise its rights upon the waters of the river, the fire that bakes the poor man’s bread of grass and barley, on the wind that turns the mill? The peasant cannot take a step upon the road, cross a crazy bridge over a river, buy an ell of cloth in the village market, without meeting feudal rapacity, without being taxed in feudal dues. Is not that enough, M. le Marquis? Must you also demand his wretched life in payment for the least infringement of your sacred privileges, careless of what widows or orphans you dedicate to woe? Will naught content you but that your shadow must lie like a curse upon the land? And do you think in your pride that France, this Job among the nations, will suffer it forever?”
He paused as if for a reply. But none came. The Marquis considered him, strangely silent, a half smile of disdain at the corners of his lips, an ominous hardness in his eyes.
Again André-Louis tugged at his friend’s sleeve.
“Philippe.”
Philippe shook him off, and plunged on, fanatically.
“Do you see nothing of the gathering clouds that herald the coming of the storm? You imagine, perhaps, that these States General summoned by M. Necker, and promised for next year, are to do nothing but devise fresh means of extortion to liquidate the bankruptcy of the State? You delude yourselves, as you shall find. The Third Estate, which you despise, will prove itself the preponderating force, and it will find a way to make an end of this canker of privilege that is devouring the vitals of this unfortunate country.”
M. le Marquis shifted in his chair, and spoke at last.
“You have, monsieur,” said he, “a very dangerous gift of eloquence. And it is of yourself rather than of your subject. For after all, what do you offer me? A rechauffe of the dishes served to out-at-elbow enthusiasts in the provincial literary chambers, compounded of the effusions of your Voltaires and Jean-Jacques and such dirty-fingered scribblers. You have not among all your philosophers one with the wit to understand that we are an order consecrated by antiquity, that for our rights and privileges we have behind us the authority of centuries.”

===Chinese===

「是滥调么，先生？您想——您扪心自问——当真是滥调么？封建的魔爪攥住一切活物，像榨葡萄似的把它们榨瘪，喂肥自己，这也是滥调？它连河里的水、烤穷人那份掺草和大麦的面包的火、推动风车的风，都要行使权利，这也是滥调？庄稼人在路上走一步，过一道河上的朽桥，在村集上买一肘布，都得撞上封建的贪横，都得交封建的捐税，这也是滥调？这还不够么，侯爵先生？您还要搭上他那条苦命，来抵偿您那神圣特权所受的最轻微的冒犯，全不顾您把多少寡妇孤儿扔给苦难，这才称心么？莫非非要您的影子像一道诅咒压在这片土地上，您才罢休？您还当真以为，凭您的骄横，法兰西——万国中的约伯——会永远忍下去么？」
他顿了顿，像是等一句回答。回答没有来。侯爵打量着他，出奇地沉默，唇角挂着一丝轻蔑的浅笑，眼里是一种不祥的冷硬。
安德烈-路易又拽朋友的衣袖。
「菲利普。」
菲利普甩开他，狂热地冲了下去。
「那聚拢的乌云，那风暴将至的先兆，您就一点看不见？您大约在想，内克尔先生召集的、许诺来年召开的三级会议，除了想出新的搜刮名目、去填补国库的破产，不会有别的作为？你们这是自欺，日后自会明白。您所不耻的第三等级，将证明自己是压倒一切的力量，它自有办法，了断这个特权毒瘤——它正在啃噬这个不幸国家的脏腑。」
侯爵先生在椅子里挪了挪身子，终于开口了。
「先生，」他说，「您有一份很危险的口才。而且这口才是您自己的，不关题目什么事。说到底，您端给我的算什么？省级文学会堂里那些穷酸热心人吃的残羹回锅，拿您那些伏尔泰、让-雅克之流以及满手污垢的舞文弄墨之徒的喷沫拌成的。您那帮哲人里头，没有一个聪明到懂得：我们是一个由古老岁月祝圣的等级，我们的权利和特权，背后有几百年的威权撑着。」

===Original===

“Humanity, monsieur,” Philippe replied, “is more ancient than nobility. Human rights are contemporary with man.”
The Marquis laughed and shrugged.
“That is the answer I might have expected. It has the right note of cant that distinguishes the philosophers.”
And then M. de Chabrillane spoke.
“You go a long way round,” he criticized his cousin, on a note of impatience.
“But I am getting there,” he was answered. “I desired to make quite certain first.”
“Faith, you should have no doubt by now.”
“I have none.” The Marquis rose, and turned again to M. de Vilmorin, who had understood nothing of that brief exchange. “M. l’abbé,” said he once more, “you have a very dangerous gift of eloquence. I can conceive of men being swayed by it. Had you been born a gentleman, you would not so easily have acquired these false views that you express.”
M. de Vilmorin stared blankly, uncomprehending.
“Had I been born a gentleman, do you say?” quoth he, in a slow, bewildered voice. “But I was born a gentleman. My race is as old, my blood as good as yours, monsieur.”
From M. le Marquis there was a slight play of eyebrows, a vague, indulgent smile. His dark, liquid eyes looked squarely into the face of M. de Vilmorin.
“You have been deceived in that, I fear.”
“Deceived?”
“Your sentiments betray the indiscretion of which madame your mother must have been guilty.”
The brutally affronting words were sped beyond recall, and the lips that had uttered them, coldly, as if they had been the merest commonplace, remained calm and faintly sneering.
A dead silence followed. André-Louis’ wits were numbed. He stood aghast, all thought suspended in him, what time M. de Vilmorin’s eyes continued fixed upon M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s, as if searching there for a meaning that eluded him. Quite suddenly he understood the vile affront. The blood leapt to his face, fire blazed in his gentle eyes. A convulsive quiver shook him. Then, with an inarticulate cry, he leaned forward, and with his open hand struck M. le Marquis full and hard upon his sneering face.
In a flash M. de Chabrillane was on his feet, between the two men.
Too late André-Louis had seen the trap. La Tour d’Azyr’s words were but as a move in a game of chess, calculated to exasperate his opponent into some such countermove as this﻿—a countermove that left him entirely at the other’s mercy.
M. le Marquis looked on, very white save where M.

===Chinese===

「人类，先生，」菲利普答道，「比贵族古老。人的权利同人一样古老。」
侯爵笑了笑，耸耸肩。
「这正是我料得到的回答。哲人的滥调腔，一耳朵就听出来了。」
随后夏布里扬先生开了口。
「你绕的弯子未免太大。」他不耐烦地批评他的表亲。
「可我这就到地方了。」对方答他，「我要先弄得十拿九稳。」
「说实话，事到如今你还有什么可疑的。」
「一无疑问了。」侯爵站起身，又转向维尔莫兰先生——那一段简短的交谈，后者一字未懂。「教士先生，」他又说了一遍，「您有一份很危险的口才。我想得出有人会被它煽动起来。您若生为绅士，就不至于这样轻易沾上您现在发表的这套谬见。」
维尔莫兰先生直愣愣地瞪着眼，莫名其妙。
「您说我若生为绅士？我本来生为绅士。我的门第同您的一样古老，我的血统同您的一样高贵，先生。」
侯爵先生眉梢轻轻一挑，浮起一个含糊而纵容的微笑。他那双深色的、水汪汪的眼睛直视维尔莫兰先生的脸。
「这一点上，您怕是受骗了。」
「受骗？」
「您这番情操，泄露了令堂当年想必犯下的失慎。」
这句横暴的辱骂已经出口，再也收不回；说出它的那两片嘴唇，冷冷的，仿佛方才只是句最平常的话——此刻依然平静，挂着淡淡的讥诮。
死一般的沉寂。安德烈-路易的头脑木了。他站在那里发怔，思绪全停了，维尔莫兰先生的眼睛却仍旧钉在拉图尔·达齐尔的眼睛上，像是要在那里寻出他所抓不住的意思。陡然间他懂了这卑污的侮辱。血涌上他的脸，温和的眼睛里烧起火来。一阵痉挛的战栗撼动他。随后，他喊出一声不成调的喊声，抢上前去，抡起手掌，朝侯爵先生那张讥诮的脸，结结实实扇了下去。
一眨眼，夏布里扬先生已站在两人中间。
安德烈-路易看清这圈套，已经迟了。拉图尔·达齐尔的话不过是棋局里的一步，算准了要激怒对手走出这样一步应手——而这步应手，叫他全然落进对方掌心。
侯爵先生望着，脸上煞白，只除了维尔莫兰先生的掌痕

===Original===

de Vilmorin’s fingerprints began slowly to colour his face; but he said nothing more. Instead, it was M. de Chabrillane who now did the talking, taking up his preconcerted part in this vile game.
“You realize, monsieur, what you have done,” said he, coldly, to Philippe. “And you realize, of course, what must inevitably follow.”
M. de Vilmorin had realized nothing. The poor young man had acted upon impulse, upon the instinct of decency and honour, never counting the consequences. But he realized them now at the sinister invitation of M. de Chabrillane, and if he desired to avoid these consequences, it was out of respect for his priestly vocation, which strictly forbade such adjustments of disputes as M. de Chabrillane was clearly thrusting upon him.
He drew back. “Let one affront wipe out the other,” said he, in a dull voice. “The balance is still in M. le Marquis’s favour. Let that content him.”
“Impossible.” The Chevalier’s lips came together tightly. Thereafter he was suavity itself, but very firm. “A blow has been struck, monsieur. I think I am correct in saying that such a thing has never happened before to M. le Marquis in all his life. If you felt yourself affronted, you had but to ask the satisfaction due from one gentleman to another. Your action would seem to confirm the assumption that you found so offensive. But it does not on that account render you immune from the consequences.”
It was, you see, M. de Chabrillane’s part to heap coals upon this fire, to make quite sure that their victim should not escape them.
“I desire no immunity,” flashed back the young seminarist, stung by this fresh goad. After all, he was nobly born, and the traditions of his class were strong upon him﻿—stronger far than the seminarist schooling in humility. He owed it to himself, to his honour, to be killed rather than avoid the consequences of the thing he had done.
“But he does not wear a sword, messieurs!” cried André-Louis, aghast.
“That is easily amended. He may have the loan of mine.”
“I mean, messieurs,” André-Louis insisted, between fear for his friend and indignation, “that it is not his habit to wear a sword, that he has never worn one, that he is untutored in its uses. He is a seminarist﻿—a postulant for holy orders, already half a priest, and so forbidden from such an engagement as you propose.”

===Chinese===

慢慢显出红印的地方；他再没说什么。接下去说话的是夏布里扬先生，他在这场卑污的把戏里接过了预派的角色。
「先生，您明白自己干了什么，」他冷冷地对菲利普说，「当然也明白这之后必然是什么。」
维尔莫兰先生原本什么都不曾明白。这可怜的年轻人凭冲动行事，凭体面和荣誉的本能，全没计较后果。此刻在夏布里扬先生阴森的敦请之下，他明白了；倘若他想避开这些后果，那也是出于对神职的敬重——神职是严禁夏布里扬先生分明要强加于他的那种「了断」的。
他退了一步。「让这桩侮辱抵了那桩吧，」他声音发闷地说，「天平还偏向侯爵先生一头呢。让他以此为足吧。」
「不可能。」骑士的两片嘴唇紧紧抿住。接下来他客气到了十分，却毫不动摇。「打了一记耳光，先生。我想我说得不错：侯爵先生这辈子还从没遇到过这种事。您若觉得受了冒犯，只须要求一位绅士对另一位绅士应给的交代。您这一手，倒像是坐实了您方才觉得那样不堪的猜疑。可这并不能叫您免掉后果。」
瞧见了么，夏布里扬先生的本分，就是往这堆火上再加炭，好教他们的猎物万无一失，休想脱身。
「我不要豁免，」年轻的神学院学生被这根新的针刺扎得火起，朗声回敬。说到底，他生在贵族门第，他的阶级传统牢牢压着他——远比修院里教人谦卑的功课压得紧。为了他自己，为了他的荣誉，他宁受一死，也不能躲避自己招来的后果。
「可他不带剑哪，先生们！」安德烈-路易惊叫。
「这好办。用我的。」
「我的意思是，先生们，」安德烈-路易又惧又愤，坚持道，「他没有佩剑的习惯，他从没佩过剑，剑上的功夫他一窍不通。他是个神学院学生——候受圣职的人，半个神父已经在了身上了，你们提的这种较量，他是被禁止的。」

===Original===

“All that he should have remembered before he struck a blow,” said M. de Chabrillane, politely.
“The blow was deliberately provoked,” raged André-Louis. Then he recovered himself, though the other’s haughty stare had no part in that recovery. “O my God, I talk in vain! How is one to argue against a purpose formed! Come away, Philippe. Don’t you see the trap﻿ ﻿…”
M. de Vilmorin cut him short, and flung him off. “Be quiet, André. M. le Marquis is entirely in the right.”
“M. le Marquis is in the right?” André-Louis let his arms fall helplessly. This man he loved above all other living men was caught in the snare of the world’s insanity. He was baring his breast to the knife for the sake of a vague, distorted sense of the honour due to himself. It was not that he did not see the trap. It was that his honour compelled him to disdain consideration of it. To André-Louis in that moment he seemed a singularly tragic figure. Noble, perhaps, but very pitiful.

===Chinese===

「这一切，他动手之前早该想起来。」夏布里扬先生客气地说。
「那记耳光是存心激出来的！」安德烈-路易怒不可遏。随后他定住了神——尽管对方的傲视对此并无功劳。「哦，我的上帝，我白费口舌！人家打定了主意，还怎么理论得动！走吧，菲利普。你难道看不见这圈套……」
维尔莫兰先生打断他，一把推开他。「别说了，安德烈。侯爵先生完全在理。」
「侯爵先生在理？」安德烈-路易的两条胳膊无可奈何地垂了下去。这个他爱得胜过世上一切活人的人，落进了这世间疯癫的罗网。他是在袒开胸膛迎着刀锋，为的是一种对他自己该得荣誉的含混而扭曲的观念。不是他看不见那圈套。是他的荣誉不许他屑于计较它。在那一刻的安德烈-路易眼里，他是一个奇特的悲剧人物。高贵，也许，然而可怜极了。

## XI The Fracas at the Théâtre Feydau / 十一 费多剧院的风波
===Original===

Leaving his host to act as his plenipotentiary with Mademoiselle de Kercadiou, and to explain to her that it was his profound contrition that compelled him to depart without taking formal leave of her, the Marquis rolled away from Sautron in a cloud of gloom. Twenty-four hours with La Binet had been more than enough for a man of his fastidious and discerning taste. He looked back upon the episode with nausea﻿—the inevitable psychological reaction﻿—marvelling at himself that until yesterday he should have found her so desirable, and cursing himself that for the sake of that ephemeral and worthless gratification he should seriously have imperilled his chances of winning Mademoiselle de Kercadiou to wife. There is, after all, nothing very extraordinary in his frame of mind, so that I need not elaborate it further. It resulted from the conflict between the beast and the angel that go to make up the composition of every man.
The Chevalier de Chabrillane﻿—who in reality occupied towards the Marquis a position akin to that of gentleman-in-waiting﻿—sat opposite to him in the enormous travelling berline. A small folding table had been erected between them, and the Chevalier suggested piquet. But M. le Marquis was in no humour for cards. His thoughts absorbed him. As they were rattling over the cobbles of Nantes’ streets, he remembered a promise to La Binet to witness her performance that night in *The Faithless Lover*. And now he was running away from her. The thought was repugnant to him on two scores. He was breaking his pledged word, and he was acting like a coward. And there was more than that. He had led the mercenary little strumpet﻿—it was thus he thought of her at present, and with some justice﻿—to expect favours from him in addition to the lavish awards which already he had made her. The baggage had almost sought to drive a bargain with him as to her future. He was to take her to Paris, put her into her own furniture﻿—as the expression ran, and still runs﻿—and under the shadow of his powerful protection see that the doors of the great theatres of the capital should be opened to her talents. He had not﻿—he was thankful to reflect﻿—exactly committed himself. But neither had he definitely refused her.

===Chinese===

侯爵把主人留下来，全权代他向德·克尔卡迪乌小姐致意，向她解释说是深切的悔恨使他来不及正式辞行便不得不离去，随即他坐着车离开索特龙，一路阴云满面。同比内那婆娘厮混了二十四小时，对他这样挑剔而精于鉴赏的口味来说早已足够过头。他回想起这段孽缘便觉恶心——这是必然的心理反应——一面惊异自己直到昨天竟还觉得她那样可爱，一面咒骂自己竟为了一朝转眼即逝、一钱不值的满足，认真危及了娶德·克尔卡迪乌小姐为妻的机会。他这种心境其实毫无特别之处，我也不必多加铺陈。这是每个人身上兽性与天使相争的结果。
夏布里扬骑士——实际上他在侯爵跟前占的位置近于贴身侍从——坐在那辆硕大的旅行马车的对面。两人之间支起一张小折桌，骑士提议玩皮克牌。可侯爵先生没玩牌的心思。他心事重重。车轮碾过南特街面的石子、辚辚作响时，他想起对比内婆娘许过的话：今晚要看她演《负心情郎》。可如今他正在躲着她。这个念头从两方面叫他厌恶：一是他失信毁约，二是他行事像个懦夫。还有更多。他曾让这个贪财的小婊子——这是他如今对她的想法，也不无道理——指望他再给她些恩惠，加在他已经慷慨给过的那些之外。那货色几乎要同他讲价钱，谈她今后的着落：要他带她去巴黎，给她置办「自家的家具」——俗话说如此，如今也还这么说——在他强大势力的庇荫之下，让京城大戏院的大门向她的才艺敞开。他没有——他庆幸地想——确切地应承下来。可他也没有明白回绝。

===Original===

It became necessary now to come to an understanding, since he was compelled to choose between his trivial passion for her﻿—a passion quenched already﻿—and his deep, almost spiritual devotion to Mademoiselle de Kercadiou.
His honour, he considered, demanded of him that he should at once deliver himself from a false position. La Binet would make a scene, of course; but he knew the proper specific to apply to hysteria of that nature. Money, after all, has its uses.
He pulled the cord. The carriage rolled to a standstill; a footman appeared at the door.
“To the Théâtre Feydau,” said he.
The footman vanished and the berline rolled on. M. de Chabrillane laughed cynically.
“I’ll trouble you not to be amused,” snapped the Marquis. “You don’t understand.” Thereafter he explained himself. It was a rare condescension in him. But, then, he could not bear to be misunderstood in such a matter. Chabrillane grew serious in reflection of the Marquis’ extreme seriousness.
“Why not write?” he suggested. “Myself, I confess that I should find it easier.”
Nothing could better have revealed M. le Marquis’ state of mind than his answer.
“Letters are liable both to miscarriage and to misconstruction. Two risks I will not run. If she did not answer, I should never know which had been incurred. And I shall have no peace of mind until I know that I have set a term to this affair. The berline can wait while we are at the theatre. We will go on afterwards. We will travel all night if necessary.”
“*Peste!*” said M. de Chabrillane with a grimace. But that was all.
The great travelling carriage drew up at the lighted portals of the Feydau, and M. le Marquis stepped out. He entered the theatre with Chabrillane, all unconsciously to deliver himself into the hands of André-Louis.
André-Louis was in a state of exasperation produced by Climène’s long absence from Nantes in the company of M. le Marquis, and fed by the unspeakable complacency with which M. Binet regarded that event of quite unmistakable import.
However much he might affect the frame of mind of the stoics, and seek to judge with a complete detachment, in the heart and soul of him André-Louis was tormented and revolted. It was not Climène he blamed. He had been mistaken in her. She was just a poor weak vessel driven helplessly by the first breath, however foul, that promised her advancement.

===Chinese===

现在必须作个了断，因为他不得不在两样之间挑选：他对她那点轻浮的欲情——这情已经熄了——和他对德·克尔卡迪乌小姐那深沉得近乎圣洁的爱慕。
他认定，他的体面要求他立刻从这个尴尬的处境里脱身。比内婆娘当然会大闹一场；可他知道对付这种性质的歇斯底里的特效药。金钱毕竟有金钱的用处。
他拉动铃绳。车停了下来；跟班出现在车门口。
“去费多剧院。”他说。
跟班退下，大车继续前行。德·夏布里扬先生冷冷一笑。
“请你免开尊笑。”侯爵厉声道，“你不懂。”接着他把原委说了。这在他是一种难得的屈尊。可是，他受不了在这类事情上叫人误会。夏布里扬见侯爵这样郑重，也郑重起来。
“何不写信？”他提议。“要是我，说实话，觉着写信容易。”
没有比侯爵先生的回答更能显出他心境的了。
“信容易误投，也容易叫人曲解。这两个险我都不冒。她若不回信，我永远不知道是哪一样出了岔子。不把这件事了结干净，我心不得安。我们在戏院里的时候车等着。出来再走。必要时连夜赶路。”
“见鬼！”德·夏布里扬先生做了个鬼脸说。可也就到此为止。
那辆豪华的大旅行马车停在灯火通明的费多剧院门前，侯爵先生下了车。他同夏布里扬走进剧场，浑然不知自己是把身子送进了安德烈-路易的手心。
安德烈-路易正处在狂怒之中：克莱门随侯爵先生久久不回南特，比内先生对这件意味再明白不过的事又摆出那副说不出的洋洋自得，火上浇油。
任凭他怎样装出斯多噶派的心境、力求以全然的超然来判断，在内心深处，安德烈-路易只觉得备受煎熬，而且愤慨。他怪的不是克莱门。他是看错了她。她不过是一只可怜的弱舟，不论风多脏，只要许她高升，头一阵风就把她身不由己地吹走了。

===Original===

She suffered from the plague of greed; and he congratulated himself upon having discovered it before making her his wife. He felt for her now nothing but a deal of pity and some contempt. The pity was begotten of the love she had lately inspired in him. It might be likened to the dregs of love, all that remained after the potent wine of it had been drained off. His anger he reserved for her father and her seducer.
The thoughts that were stirring in him on that Monday morning, when it was discovered that Climène had not yet returned from her excursion of the previous day in the coach of M. le Marquis, were already wicked enough without the spurring they received from the distraught Leandre.
Hitherto the attitude of each of these men towards the other had been one of mutual contempt. The phenomenon has frequently been observed in like cases. Now, what appeared to be a common misfortune brought them into a sort of alliance. So, at least, it seemed to Leandre when he went in quest of André-Louis, who with apparent unconcern was smoking a pipe upon the quay immediately facing the inn.
“Name of a pig!” said Leandre. “How can you take your ease and smoke at such a time?”
Scaramouche surveyed the sky. “I do not find it too cold,” said he. “The sun is shining. I am very well here.”
“Do I talk of the weather?” Leandre was very excited.
“Of what, then?”
“Of Climène, of course.”
“Oh! The lady has ceased to interest me,” he lied.
Leandre stood squarely in front of him, a handsome figure handsomely dressed in these days, his hair well powdered, his stockings of silk. His face was pale, his large eyes looked larger than usual.
“Ceased to interest you? Are you not to marry her?”
André-Louis expelled a cloud of smoke. “You cannot wish to be offensive. Yet you almost suggest that I live on other men’s leavings.”
“My God!” said Leandre, overcome, and he stared awhile. Then he burst out afresh. “Are you quite heartless? Are you always Scaramouche?”
“What do you expect me to do?” asked André-Louis, evincing surprise in his own turn, but faintly.
“I do not expect you to let her go without a struggle.”
“But she has gone already.” André-Louis pulled at his pipe a moment, what time Leandre clenched and unclenched his hands in impotent rage. “And to what purpose struggle against the inevitable? Did you struggle when I took her from you?”
“She was not mine to be taken from me.

===Chinese===

她害的是贪心的病；他庆幸自己在娶她之前发现了这一点。他如今对她只剩许多怜悯和几分鄙夷。怜悯是她新近在他心里激起的爱情所生下的，不妨比作爱情的酒底——烈酒饮尽之后剩下的那点残滓。他的怒火，他留给她的父亲和她的勾引者。
那个礼拜一的早晨，众人发觉克莱门坐侯爵先生的车出游一天之后仍未归来，他心里翻腾的念头本已够歹毒的了，偏偏又挨了方寸大乱的莱昂德一顿猛扇。
这两个人素来彼此相轻。同类情势下常见这种现象。如今，一场看似共同的厄运把他们结成了某种同盟。至少，莱昂德去找安德烈-路易时是这样想的；后者正没事人似的在客栈正对的码头上抽烟斗。
“猪猡的名字！”莱昂德说，“这种时候，你怎么还能安坐在此抽烟？”
斯卡拉穆恰望了望天。“我觉得不算太冷，”他说，“太阳照着呢。我在这儿挺好。”
“我同你谈天气么？”莱昂德激动得很。
“那谈什么？”
“自然是谈克莱门。”
“哦！这位小姐已经引不起我的兴趣了。”他撒了个谎。
莱昂德叉开双腿站到他面前，这些天他衣著讲究，人又俊俏，头发扑得匀净，长袜是真丝的。他脸色发白，大眼睛显得比平常更大。
“引不起兴趣？你不是要娶她么？”
安德烈-路易吐出一团烟。“你总不至于有意冒犯我。可你这话说得，几乎是在指我捡别的男人吃剩的。”
“我的上帝！”莱昂德被他打倒了，怔了半晌。随后他又爆发了。“你的心是石头做的？你永远只是斯卡拉穆恰么？”
“你要我怎么样？”安德烈-路易问，轮到他表示惊讶了，虽然淡淡地。
“我不指望你眼睁睁让她走了连争也不争。”
“可她已经走了。”安德烈-路易抽了一口烟，莱昂德在一旁攥紧又松开拳头，徒然发狠。“再说，同无可挽回的事争，争个什么？当初我从你手里把她夺来的时候，你争过么？”
“她本不是我的人，谈不上从我手里夺。我不过心存想望，是你赢了这场赛跑。可就算当初换一个样，这两件事怎么好比？那一件光明正大；这一件——这一件是地狱。”

===Original===

I but aspired, and you won the race. But even had it been otherwise where is the comparison? That was a thing in honour; this﻿—this is hell.”
His emotion moved André-Louis. He took Leandre’s arm. “You’re a good fellow, Leandre. I am glad I intervened to save you from your fate.”
“Oh, you don’t love her!” cried the other, passionately. “You never did. You don’t know what it means to love, or you’d not talk like this. My God! if she had been my affianced wife and this had happened, I should have killed the man﻿—killed him! Do you hear me? But you﻿ ﻿… Oh, you, you come out here and smoke, and take the air, and talk of her as another man’s leavings. I wonder I didn’t strike you for the word.”
He tore his arm from the other’s grip, and looked almost as if he would strike him now.
“You should have done it,” said André-Louis. “It’s in your part.”
With an imprecation Leandre turned on his heel to go. André-Louis arrested his departure.
“A moment, my friend. Test me by yourself. Would you marry her now?”
“Would I?” The young man’s eyes blazed with passion. “Would I? Let her say that she will marry me, and I am her slave.”
“Slave is the right word﻿—a slave in hell.”
“It would never be hell to me where she was, whatever she had done. I love her, man, I am not like you. I love her, do you hear me?”
“I have known it for some time,” said André-Louis. “Though I didn’t suspect your attack of the disease to be quite so violent. Well, God knows I loved her, too, quite enough to share your thirst for killing. For myself, the blue blood of La Tour d’Azyr would hardly quench this thirst. I should like to add to it the dirty fluid that flows in the veins of the unspeakable Binet.”
For a second his emotion had been out of hand, and he revealed to Leandre in the mordant tone of those last words something of the fires that burned under his icy exterior. The young man caught him by the hand.
“I knew you were acting,” said he. “You feel﻿—you feel as I do.”
“Behold us, fellows in viciousness. I have betrayed myself, it seems. Well, and what now? Do you want to see this pretty Marquis torn limb from limb? I might afford you the spectacle.”
“What?” Leandre stared, wondering was this another of Scaramouche’s cynicisms.
“It isn’t really difficult provided I have aid. I require only a little. Will you lend it me?”
“Anything you ask,” Leandre exploded. “My life if you require it.”

===Chinese===

他的激动打动了安德烈-路易。他挽住莱昂德的胳膊。“你是个好人，莱昂德。我当初插手把你从你的命运里救出来，救对了。”
“哦，你不爱她！”对方激愤地喊，“你从来就不爱。你不懂爱是什么滋味，不然说不出这样的话。我的上帝！她若是我的未婚妻，出了这种事，我会杀了那男人——杀了他！你听见没有？可你……哦，你，你跑到这儿来抽烟、透气，把她叫作别的男人吃剩的。我真后悔方才没为这句话给你一记耳光。”
他把胳膊从对方手里挣出来，那样子几乎像现在就要动手。
“你真该打的。”安德烈-路易说，“这本来就在你的戏份里。”
莱昂德咒骂一声，转身要走。安德烈-路易叫住了他。
“慢着，我的朋友。拿你自己来掂量我吧。换了你，如今还肯娶她么？”
“我肯不肯？”年轻人的眼睛燃着情火。“我肯不肯？只要她说一句肯嫁我，我就是她的奴仆。”
“奴仆这字眼用得对——地狱里的奴仆。”
“只要有她在，任她做过什么，那地方对我来说就绝不是地狱。我爱她，老兄，我不像你。我爱她，你听见没有？”
“这我早知道一些时候了，”安德烈-路易说，“只是没想到你这场病害得这样凶。好吧，上帝知道，我也曾够爱她，爱得足以同你分饮这份杀人的渴望。要依我，拉图尔·达齐尔那身蓝血还浇不灭我这口渴。我还想再添一味：那个说不出口的比内血管里流的脏水。”
他的情绪有一瞬间失了控，末尾几句话刻毒的腔调，让莱昂德窥见了那副冰壳底下烧着的火。年轻人一把抓住他的手。
“我早知道你是在做戏，”他说，“你同我一样——你同我一样感受。”
“瞧咱们，难兄难弟，一肚子坏水。看来我露了底。好，接下来呢？你想不想看这位漂亮侯爵叫人撕成几片？这场戏我可以给你办到。”
“什么？”莱昂德瞪着眼，疑心这又是斯卡拉穆恰哪一路的冷嘲。
“其实不难，只要有人帮我。我要的不多。你肯借我么？”
“你要什么都行，”莱昂德炸开了，“要我的命也行。”

===Original===

André-Louis took his arm again. “Let us walk,” he said. “I will instruct you.”
When they came back the company was already at dinner. Mademoiselle had not yet returned. Sullenness presided at the table. Columbine and Madame wore anxious expressions. The fact was that relations between Binet and his troupe were daily growing more strained.
André-Louis and Leandre went each to his accustomed place. Binet’s little eyes followed them with a malicious gleam, his thick lips pouted into a crooked smile.
“You two are grown very friendly of a sudden,” he mocked.
“You are a man of discernment, Binet,” said Scaramouche, the cold loathing of his voice itself an insult. “Perhaps you discern the reason?”
“It is readily discerned.”
“Regale the company with it!” he begged; and waited. “What? You hesitate? Is it possible that there are limits to your shamelessness?”
Binet reared his great head. “Do you want to quarrel with me, Scaramouche?” Thunder was rumbling in his deep voice.
“Quarrel? You want to laugh. A man doesn’t quarrel with creatures like you. We all know the place held in the public esteem by complacent husbands. But, in God’s name, what place is there at all for complacent fathers?”
Binet heaved himself up, a great towering mass of manhood. Violently he shook off the restraining hand of Pierrot who sat on his left.
“A thousand devils!” he roared; “if you take that tone with me, I’ll break every bone in your filthy body.”
“If you were to lay a finger on me, Binet, you would give me the only provocation I still need to kill you.” André-Louis was as calm as ever, and therefore the more menacing. Alarm stirred the company. He protruded from his pocket the butt of a pistol﻿—newly purchased. “I go armed, Binet. It is only fair to give you warning. Provoke me as you have suggested, and I’ll kill you with no more compunction than I should kill a slug, which after all is the thing you most resemble﻿—a slug, Binet; a fat, slimy body; foulness without soul and without intelligence. When I come to think of it I can’t suffer to sit at table with you. It turns my stomach.”
He pushed away his platter and got up. “I’ll go and eat at the ordinary below stairs.”
Thereupon up jumped Columbine.
“And I’ll come with you, Scaramouche!” cried she.
It acted like a signal. Had the thing been concerted it couldn’t have fallen out more uniformly.

安德烈-路易重又挽起他的胳膊。“咱们走走，”他说，“我教你怎么办。”
他们回来时全团正在用晚饭。小姐还没回来。一股阴沉气压罩着饭桌。科隆比内和太太满面忧色。事实是，比内同他的戏班之间的关系一天紧似一天。
安德烈-路易和莱昂德各归各座。比内的小眼睛带着恶意的光跟着他们转动，厚嘴唇撇出一丝歪笑。
“你们两个忽然亲热起来了。”他讥讽道。
“你是个有眼力的人，比内，”斯卡拉穆恰说，声音里那股冷冷的憎恶本身就是侮辱。“也许你把缘故也看出来了？”
“一看便知。”
“那就说给诸位听听！”他请求道，等着。“怎么？你犹豫了？莫非你的无耻也有个尽头？”
比内昂起他的大脑袋。“你是要同我吵架，斯卡拉穆恰？”他的粗嗓门里滚着雷。
“吵架？你真会说笑。谁也不同你这样的东西吵架。戴绿帽而心安理得的丈夫在世人眼里占个什么位置，咱们都知道。可是凭上帝的名义，心安理得的父亲又占个什么位置？”
比内撑着桌子站起来，一大坨人山。他猛地甩开坐在他左手边的皮埃罗那只拉劝的手。
“一千个魔鬼！”他咆哮道，“你再这个腔调同我说话，我把你一身贱骨头一根根拆了。”
“你敢碰我一根指头，比内，你就给了我杀你所需要的最后一样理由。”安德烈-路易同先前一样平静，因此更加吓人。全团都惊动了。他从口袋里露出一支手枪的枪柄——新买的。“我带着家伙，比内。先打个招呼，才算公平。照你方才说的那样招我，我毙你不会比踩死一个鼻涕虫多一分犹豫——说起来你最像的也就是这东西：一条鼻涕虫，比内；一身肥腻黏滑；又脏又蠢，没有灵魂。细想起来，同你同桌吃饭我都受不了。叫我恶心。”
他推开盘子站起身。“我下楼去大众饭堂吃。”
话音未落科隆比内跳了起来。
“我跟你一起去，斯卡拉穆恰！”她喊道。
这就像一个信号。若说这是串通好的，也串通得不能再齐了。

===Original===

Binet, in fact, was persuaded of a conspiracy. For in the wake of Columbine went Leandre, in the wake of Leandre, Polichinelle and then all the rest together, until Binet found himself sitting alone at the head of an empty table in an empty room﻿—a badly shaken man whose rage could afford him no support against the dread by which he was suddenly invaded.
He sat down to think things out, and he was still at that melancholy occupation when perhaps a half-hour later his daughter entered the room, returned at last from her excursion.
She looked pale, even a little scared﻿—in reality excessively self-conscious now that the ordeal of facing all the company awaited her.
Seeing no one but her father in the room, she checked on the threshold.
“Where is everybody?” she asked, in a voice rendered natural by effort.
M. Binet reared his great head and turned upon her eyes that were blood-injected. He scowled, blew out his thick lips and made harsh noises in his throat. Yet he took stock of her, so graceful and comely and looking so completely the lady of fashion in her long fur-trimmed travelling coat of bottle green, her muff and her broad hat adorned by a sparkling Rhinestone buckle above her adorably coiffed brown hair. No need to fear the future whilst he owned such a daughter, let Scaramouche play what tricks he would.
He expressed, however, none of these comforting reflections.
“So you’re back at last, little fool,” he growled in greeting. “I was beginning to ask myself if we should perform this evening. It wouldn’t greatly have surprised me if you had not returned in time. Indeed, since you have chosen to play the fine hand you held in your own way and scorning my advice, nothing can surprise me.”
She crossed the room to the table, and leaning against it, looked down upon him almost disdainfully.
“I have nothing to regret,” she said.
“So every fool says at first. Nor would you admit it if you had. You are like that. You go your own way in spite of advice from older heads. Death of my life, girl, what do you know of men?”
“I am not complaining,” she reminded him.
“No, but you may be presently, when you discover that you would have done better to have been guided by your old father. So long as your Marquis languished for you, there was nothing you could not have done with the fool. So long as you let him have no more than your fingertips to kiss﻿ ﻿…

比内当真认定这里头有阴谋。因为科隆比内身后跟着莱昂德，莱昂德身后跟着波利希奈勒，随后其余人一拥而去，直到比内发现自己独自坐在空屋子里一张空桌子的上首——一个心胆俱裂的人，怒气再也撑不住那阵突然袭来的恐惧。
他坐下来细细盘算，这桩凄凉的营生他干了约莫半个钟头，他女儿才走进屋来，游历归来。
她脸色发白，甚至有点惊惶——其实是一想到马上要当着全团的面，窘得慌了神。
看见屋里只有父亲一人，她在门槛上收住了脚。
“人都哪儿去了？”她问，声音是硬装出来的自然。
比内先生昂起他的大脑袋，冲她转过一双充血的眼睛。他沉着脸，鼓着厚嘴唇，喉咙里咯咯作响。但他仍把她上下打量了一番：这样苗条标致，一身瓶绿色的皮镶边旅行长外套，暖手筒，宽檐帽，梳得可爱的棕发上方缀着亮闪闪的莱茵石饰扣，一副彻头彻尾的时髦女郎打扮。只要有这样一个女儿，任斯卡拉穆恰耍什么花招，前途何足惧哉。
可这些宽心话他一句也没说出口。
“你到底回来了，小傻瓜，”他吼着算作招呼，“我正在问自己，今晚的戏还演不演得成。你要是赶不回来，我才一点不吃惊呢。说实在的，你既然打定主意，手里攣着一副好牌偏要自己乱打、不听我的劝，如今什么事都吓不着我了。”
她穿过屋子走到桌边，倚着桌子，几乎是鄙夷地俯视他。
“我没什么可后悔的。”她说。
“每个傻瓜起头都这么说。你就是后悔也不会认。你就是这号人。老成人的话你一句听不进，偏要走自己的路。我的命根子哟，丫头，你懂得男人什么？”
“我又没抱怨。”她提醒他。
“是没抱怨，可你早晚会，等你发觉听了你老爹的话原是上策。你的侯爵为你害相思的时候，那个蠢货你要他怎样他都依。只要你只让他亲亲你的指尖……”

===Original===

ah, name of a name! that was the time to build your future. If you live to be a thousand you’ll never have such a chance again, and you’ve squandered it, for what?”
Mademoiselle sat down.﻿—“You’re sordid,” she said, with disgust.
“Sordid, am I?” His thick lips curled again. “I have had enough of the dregs of life, and so I should have thought have you. You held a hand on which to have won a fortune if you had played it as I bade you. Well, you’ve played it, and where’s the fortune? We can whistle for that as a sailor whistles for wind. And, by Heaven, we’ll need to whistle presently if the weather in the troupe continues as it’s set in. That scoundrel Scaramouche has been at his ape’s tricks with them. They’ve suddenly turned moral. They won’t sit at table with me any more.” He was spluttering between anger and sardonic mirth. “It was your friend Scaramouche set them the example of that. He threatened my life actually. Threatened my life! Called me﻿ ﻿… Oh, but what does that matter? What matters is that the next thing to happen to us will be that the Binet Troupe will discover it can manage without M. Binet and his daughter. This scoundrelly bastard I’ve befriended has little by little robbed me of everything. It’s in his power today to rob me of my troupe, and the knave’s ungrateful enough and vile enough to make use of his power.”
“Let him,” said mademoiselle contemptuously.
“Let him?” He was aghast. “And what’s to become of us?”
“In no case will the Binet Troupe interest me much longer,” said she. “I shall be going to Paris soon. There are better theatres there than the Feydau. There’s Mlle. Montansier’s theatre in the Palais Royal; there’s the Ambigu Comique; there’s the Comédie Française; there’s even a possibility I may have a theatre of my own.”
His eyes grew big for once. He stretched out a fat hand, and placed it on one of hers. She noticed that it trembled.
“Has he promised that? Has he promised?”
She looked at him with her head on one side, eyes sly and a queer little smile on her perfect lips.
“He did not refuse me when I asked it,” she answered, with conviction that all was as she desired it.
“Bah!” He withdrew his hand, and heaved himself up. There was disgust on his face. “He did not refuse!”

===Chinese===

啊，名字的名字！那才是替你的前程打桩的时辰。你活到一千岁也再碰不上那样的机会了，你却把它精蹋了，换来了什么？
小姐坐了下来。“你一身铜臭。”她嫌恶地说。
“铜臭，是么？”他的厚嘴唇又撇了撇。“生活的渣底我尝够了，我本想你也是。你手里那副牌，照我教你的打法本可以赢来一份家业。好，你打了，家业在哪儿？咱们只好望天吹口哨求风，水手那样。而且，天晓得，要是戏班里这股邪气照这样刮下去，咱们马上就得吹口哨了。那个恶棍斯卡拉穆恰又对他们耍他的猴把戏了。他们忽然讲起道德来了。再不肯同我同桌吃饭。”他又气又笑，唾沫横飞。“给他们开这个头的，正是你那位朋友斯卡拉穆恰。他竟威胁我的性命。威胁我的性命！骂我是……哦，那有什么要紧？要紧的是，下一件落到我们头上的事，就是比内剧团发现没有比内先生和他女儿也照样过日子。我这个好心收留的杂种，一点一点把我什么都偷光了。如今他手里就有力量把我的戏班偷走，这无赖又忘恩负义又下作，真使得出这力量。”
“让他偷好了。”小姐轻蔑地说。
“让他偷？”他吓呆了。“那我们怎么办？”
“反正比内剧团也留不住我多久了，”她说，“我不久就要去巴黎。那里有的是比费多好的戏院子。有王宫市场蒙唐西耶小姐（Mlle. Montansier）的戏院；有安比居喜剧院；有法兰西喜剧院；没准我还能有一座自己的戏院。”
他的眼睛头一回睁大了。他伸出一只胖手，按在她的一只手上。她察觉那只手在抖。
“他应承了？他应承了？”
她歪着头看他，眼睛狡黠，端正的唇上挂着一丝古怪的浅笑。
“我开口要的时候，他没有回绝。”她答道，笃定事事都如了她的愿。
“呸！”他缩回手，撑起身来，一脸的嫌恶。“他没有回绝！”

===Original===

he mocked her; and then with passion: “Had you acted as I advised you, he would have consented to anything that you asked, and what is more he would have provided anything that you asked﻿—anything that lay within his means, and they are inexhaustible. You have changed a certainty into a possibility, and I hate possibilities﻿—God of God! I have lived on possibilities, and infernally near starved on them.”
Had she known of the interview taking place at that moment at the Château de Sautron she would have laughed less confidently at her father’s gloomy forebodings. But she was destined never to know, which indeed was the cruellest punishment of all. She was to attribute all the evil that of a sudden overwhelmed her, the shattering of all the future hopes she had founded upon the Marquis and the sudden disintegration of the Binet Troupe, to the wicked interference of that villain Scaramouche.
She had this much justification that possibly, without the warning from M. de Sautron, the Marquis would have found in the events of that evening at the Théâtre Feydau a sufficient reason for ending an entanglement that was fraught with too much unpleasant excitement, whilst the breaking-up of the Binet Troupe was most certainly the result of André-Louis’ work. But it was not a result that he intended or even foresaw.
So much was this the case that in the interval after the second act, he sought the dressing-room shared by Polichinelle and Rhodomont. Polichinelle was in the act of changing.
“I shouldn’t trouble to change,” he said. “The piece isn’t likely to go beyond my opening scene of the next act with Leandre.”
“What do you mean?”
“You’ll see.” He put a paper on Polichinelle’s table amid the grease-paints. “Cast your eye over that. It’s a sort of last will and testament in favour of the troupe. I was a lawyer once; the document is in order. I relinquish to all of you the share produced by my partnership in the company.”
“But you don’t mean that you are leaving us?” cried Polichinelle in alarm, whilst Rhodomont’s sudden stare asked the same question.
Scaramouche’s shrug was eloquent. Polichinelle ran on gloomily: “Of course it was to have been foreseen. But why should you be the one to go? It is you who have made us; and it is you who are the real head and brains of the troupe; it is you who have raised it into a real theatrical company.

===Chinese===

他学了她一句；随即动了火气：“你若照我教的办，你要什么他都会应承，而且给什么他都肯给——只要他力所能及，而他的家私是没底的。你把一个定局换成了一点指望，我恨指望——上帝的上帝！我这辈子就活在指望上，饿得只剩一层皮。”
她若是知道此时索特龙城堡里正进行着怎样一场谈话，对她父亲这些阴郁的预言就不会笑得那样有把握了。可她命中注定永远不会知道——这原是一切惩罚里最残酷的一种。骤然间压到她头上的一切灾祸，她建立在侯爵身上的全部前程希望的破灭，以及比内剧团的猝然解体，她都要归罪于那个恶棍斯卡拉穆恰的歹毒搅局。
她也不是全无理由：倘若没有德·索特龙先生那一番告诫，侯爵多半也能在费多剧院那一晚的事件里找到充足的理由，了结这桩火气太盛、太不痛快的孽缘；而比内剧团的散伙，则确凿无疑是安德烈-路易干出来的结果。只是这结果既非他的本意，甚至也非他所能预见。
正因如此，第二幕之后的幕间休息里，他去了波利希奈勒同罗多蒙合用的化妆间。波利希奈勒正在换装。
“不必费事换了，”他说，“这出戏多半演不到下一幕我同莱昂德的开场。”
“你什么意思？”
“你看着吧。”他把一张纸放在波利希奈勒桌上油彩中间。“过目一下。这是留给剧团的一份遗嘱之类的东西。我从前做过律师；文书的手续齐全。我在剧团里的合伙人股份生出的那一份，我让给你们全体。”
“可你不是要离开我们吧？”波利希奈勒惊慌地喊，罗多蒙猛然一瞪眼，问的是同一句话。
斯卡拉穆恰一耸肩，胜过千言。波利希奈勒郁郁地接着说：“当然，这也是料得到的。可为什么走的是你？成全我们的是你；戏班真正的头脑和主心骨是你；把这个班拔擢成真正戏班的也是你。

===Original===

If anyone must go, let it be Binet﻿—Binet and his infernal daughter. Or if you go, name of a name! we all go with you!”
“Aye,” added Rhodomont, “we’ve had enough of that fat scoundrel.”
“I had thought of it, of course,” said André-Louis. “It was not vanity, for once; it was trust in your friendship. After tonight we may consider it again, if I survive.”
“If you survive?” both cried.
Polichinelle got up. “Now, what madness have you in mind?” he asked.
“For one thing I think I am indulging Leandre; for another I am pursuing an old quarrel.”
The three knocks sounded as he spoke.
“There, I must go. Keep that paper, Polichinelle. After all, it may not be necessary.”
He was gone. Rhodomont stared at Polichinelle. Polichinelle stared at Rhodomont.
“What the devil is he thinking of?” quoth the latter.
“That is most readily ascertained by going to see,” replied Polichinelle. He completed changing in haste, and despite what Scaramouche had said; and then followed with Rhodomont.
As they approached the wings a roar of applause met them coming from the audience. It was applause and something else; applause on an unusual note. As it faded away they heard the voice of Scaramouche ringing clear as a bell:
“And so you see, my dear M. Leandre, that when you speak of the Third Estate, it is necessary to be more explicit. What precisely is the Third Estate?”
“Nothing,” said Leandre.
There was a gasp from the audience, audible in the wings, and then swiftly followed Scaramouche’s next question:
“True. Alas! But what should it be?”
“Everything,” said Leandre.
The audience roared its acclamations, the more violent because of the unexpectedness of that reply.
“True again,” said Scaramouche. “And what is more, that is what it will be; that is what it already is. Do you doubt it?”
“I hope it,” said the schooled Leandre.
“You may believe it,” said Scaramouche, and again the acclamations rolled into thunder.
Polichinelle and Rhodomont exchanged glances: indeed, the former winked, not without mirth.
“Sacred name!” growled a voice behind them. “Is the scoundrel at his political tricks again?”
They turned to confront M. Binet. Moving with that noiseless tread of his, he had come up unheard behind them, and there he stood now in his scarlet suit of Pantaloon under a trailing bedgown, his little eyes glaring from either side of his false nose.

要有人非走不可，让比内走——比内和他那个地狱里的女儿。再不然，你要走，名字的名字！我们全跟你走！”
“是啊，”罗多蒙附和，“那个肥恶棍我们受够了。”
“这个我自然想到过，”安德烈-路易说。“这一回不是自负；是信得过你们的交情。过了今晚，只要我还活着，咱们可以再议。”
“只要你还活着？”两人齐声喊。
波利希奈勒站起身。“这回你心里打的是什么疯主意？”他问。
“一半是我想成全莱昂德，一半是我要清算一桩旧账。”
说话间台上敲响了三声。
“我得走了。那张纸你收着，波利希奈勒。说不定到底用不上。”
他走了。罗多蒙瞪着波利希奈勒，波利希奈勒瞪着罗多蒙。
“他到底打的什么鬼主意？”后者说。
“要看个明白，最省事的法子就是去看。”波利希奈勒答。他不顾斯卡拉穆恰方才的话，赶紧换完装，同罗多蒙一起跟了出去。
他们走近侧幕时，观众席上一阵喝彩的声浪迎面扑来。是喝彩，又不止是喝彩；调门异乎寻常。声浪落下去时，他们听见斯卡拉穆恰的声音，清越如钟：
“所以您瞧，亲爱的莱昂德先生，谈起第三等级，话就得说得更明白些。第三等级究竟是什么？”
“什么也不是。”莱昂德说。
观众席里倒抽一口凉气，侧幕都听得见；紧跟着是斯卡拉穆恰的下一问：
“对。可惜！可它应该是什么？”
“一切。”莱昂德说。
观众轰然喝彩，这一答出于意外，彩声愈发猛烈。
“又对了，”斯卡拉穆恰说，“而且不止应该——它正要成为一切，它已经是一切了。诸位不信么？”
“我但愿如此。”调教好的莱昂德说。
“您可以信。”斯卡拉穆恰说，喝彩声又滚成一片雷鸣。
波利希奈勒同罗多蒙交换了个眼色：前者还挤了挤眼，不无得意。
“神圣的名字！”他们背后有人低吼。“这恶棍又耍他的政治把戏了？”
他们转身，正对比内先生。他迈着那双悄无声息的步子，跟上来竟没人听见；此刻他站在那里，潘塔隆的一身猩红戏装配着一件扫地长睡袍，假鼻子两边小眼睛怒瞪着。

===Original===

But their attention was held by the voice of Scaramouche. He had stepped to the front of the stage.
“He doubts it,” he was telling the audience. “But then this M. Leandre is himself akin to those who worship the worm-eaten idol of Privilege, and so he is a little afraid to believe a truth that is becoming apparent to all the world. Shall I convince him? Shall I tell him how a company of noblemen backed by their servants under arms﻿—six hundred men in all﻿—sought to dictate to the Third Estate of Rennes a few short weeks ago? Must I remind him of the martial front shown on that occasion by the Third Estate, and how they swept the streets clean of that rabble of nobles﻿—*cette canaille noble?*﻿ ﻿…”
Applause interrupted him. The phrase had struck home and caught. Those who had writhed under that infamous designation from their betters leapt at this turning of it against the nobles themselves.
“But let me tell you of their leader﻿—*le pins noble de cette canaille, ou bien le plus canaille de ces nobles!* You know him﻿—that one. He fears many things, but the voice of truth he fears most. With such as him the eloquent truth eloquently spoken is a thing instantly to be silenced. So he marshalled his peers and their *valetailles*, and led them out to slaughter these miserable bourgeois who dared to raise a voice. But these same miserable bourgeois did not choose to be slaughtered in the streets of Rennes. It occurred to them that since the nobles decreed that blood should flow, it might as well be the blood of the nobles. They marshalled themselves too﻿—this noble rabble against the rabble of nobles﻿—and they marshalled themselves so well that they drove M. de La Tour d’Azyr and his warlike following from the field with broken heads and shattered delusions. They sought shelter at the hands of the Cordeliers; and the shavelings gave them sanctuary in their convent﻿—those who survived, among whom was their proud leader, M. de La Tour d’Azyr. You have heard of this valiant Marquis, this great lord of life and death?”
The pit was in an uproar a moment. It quieted again as Scaramouche continued:
“Oh, it was a fine spectacle to see this mighty hunter scuttling to cover like a hare, going to earth in the Cordelier Convent. Rennes has not seen him since. Rennes would like to see him again. But if he is valorous, he is also discreet.

===Chinese===

可他们的注意力被斯卡拉穆恰的声音抓住了。他已跨到台口。
“他不信，”他正对观众说。“可这位莱昂德先生自己，就同那些膜拜特权这尊蛀空偶像的人沾亲带故，所以一条正为举世所共见的真理，他有点不敢信。要我说服他么？要不要我告诉他，几个礼拜之前，一伙贵族带着他们持械的仆从——总共六百人——怎样想对雷恩的第三等级发号施令？要不要我提醒他，第三等级那天摆出的战斗阵容，他们怎样把街上那伙贵族渣滓清扫干净——这‘贵族渣滓’（cette canaille noble）？……”
喝彩打断了他。这个字眼打中了要害，叫人一下抓住。那些曾被老爷们用这句臭名打在身上的人，见它反过来砸向贵族自己，踊跃不已。
“可让我给你们讲讲他们的头儿——这伙渣滓里最高贵的，或者说这伙贵族里最下作的！你们认识他——那一位。他怕的东西多，可他最怕真理的声音。对付他这种人，雄辩的真理一说出口，就得立刻叫它噤声。于是他纠合他的同辈和他们的仆役大军，开出去屠杀这些胆敢出声的可怜资产者。可这些可怜资产者偏不打算在雷恩的街上挨屠。他们转念一想：既然贵族老爷定要流血，那就不如流贵族的血。他们也结了阵——以民之渣滓对贵之渣滓——而且结得那样好，把德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生和他那支尚武的队伍打得脑袋开花、迷梦破碎，逐出了战场。他们躲到科尔德利埃修士（Cordeliers）门下求庇；那些剃度僧把他们收进了修道院——活下来的那些人，其中就有他们那位高贵的头领德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生。诸位听说过这位英勇的侯爵，这位操人生死的大老爷么？”
池座里一时大乱。斯卡拉穆恰继续说下去，乱声又平息了：
“哦，那场面多壮观：这位了不起的大猎手抱头鼠窜，活像只野兔，一头钻进科尔德利埃修道院的地洞。雷恩从此没再见到他。雷恩倒很想再见见他。可他虽然骁勇，却也谨慎。

===Original===

And where do you think he has taken refuge, this great nobleman who wanted to see the streets of Rennes washed in the blood of its citizens, this man who would have butchered old and young of the contemptible canaille to silence the voice of reason and of liberty that presumes to ring through France today? Where do you think he hides himself? Why, here in Nantes.”
Again there was uproar.
“What do you say? Impossible? Why, my friends, at this moment he is here in this theatre﻿—skulking up there in that box. He is too shy to show himself﻿—oh, a very modest gentleman. But there he is behind the curtains. Will you not show yourself to your friends, M. de La Tour d’Azyr, Monsieur le Marquis who considers eloquence so very dangerous a gift? See, they would like a word with you; they do not believe me when I tell them that you are here.”
Now, whatever he may have been, and whatever the views held on the subject by André-Louis, M. de La Tour d’Azyr was certainly not a coward. To say that he was hiding in Nantes was not true. He came and went there openly and unabashed. It happened, however, that the Nantais were ignorant until this moment of his presence among them. But then he would have disdained to have informed them of it just as he would have disdained to have concealed it from them.
Challenged thus, however, and despite the ominous manner in which the bourgeois element in the audience had responded to Scaramouche’s appeal to its passions, despite the attempts made by Chabrillane to restrain him, the Marquis swept aside the curtain at the side of the box, and suddenly showed himself, pale but self-contained and scornful as he surveyed first the daring Scaramouche and then those others who at sight of him had given tongue to their hostility.
Hoots and yells assailed him, fists were shaken at him, canes were brandished menacingly.
“Assassin! Scoundrel! Coward! Traitor!”
But he braved the storm, smiling upon them his ineffable contempt. He was waiting for the noise to cease; waiting to address them in his turn. But he waited in vain, as he very soon perceived.
The contempt he did not trouble to dissemble served but to goad them on.
In the pit pandemonium was already raging. Blows were being freely exchanged; there were scuffling groups, and here and there swords were being drawn, but fortunately the press was too dense to permit of their being used effectively.

===Chinese===

诸位猜猜，这位巴不得见雷恩的街道拿市民的血来洗一洗的大贵人——这个人，为了叫如今敢于响彻法兰西的理性和自由之声噤口，原肯把那可鄙的贱民老幼杀个精光——他躲到哪儿去了？诸位猜猜他藏身何处？咳，就在这儿，南特。”
又是一阵大乱。
“您说什么？不可能？哎呀，我的朋友们，此时此刻他就在这座戏园子里——就缩在上头那个包厢里。他脸皮薄，不好意思露面——哦，一位非常谦逊的绅士。可他就在那帘子后头。您不肯赏脸见见您的朋友们么，德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生，这位认为雄辩是非常危险的才能的侯爵先生？瞧，他们都想同您说句话；我告诉他们您在这儿，他们还不信呢。”
然而，不论此人到底如何，也不论安德烈-路易对他持何等看法，德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生断然不是懦夫。说他藏匿在南特并不确实。他在城里来来去去，公开坦然。只是碰巧，南特人直到此刻才知道他在他们中间。而他本不屑于知会他们，正如也不屑于瞒着他们。
可是受到这样的挑衅，尽管观众里的市民分子对斯卡拉穆恰煽起的激愤已有凶险的反应，尽管夏布里扬竭力拦他，侯爵还是一把掠开包厢侧面的帷幔，蕦地现出身来：脸色发白，却镇定而轻蔑，先扫一眼那个胆大包天的斯卡拉穆恰，再扫一眼那些一见他便鼓躁出敌意的旁人。
嘘声和吼叫劈头盖脸砸向他，拳头朝他挥舞，手杖威胁地扬起。
“凶手！恶棍！懦夫！叛徒！”
可他昂然顶住这场风暴，向他们报以那份说不出的轻蔑的微笑。他等着喧嚣平息；等着轮到他向他们讲话。但他白等了，这一点他很快就看明白了。
他那懒得掩饰的轻蔑，只是给他们火上浇油。
池座里早已经闹成一片混战。拳头你来我往，到处是扭作一团的人堆，这里那里已有剑拔出鞘，所幸人挤得太密，剑倒使得不利落。

===Original===

Those who had women with them and the timid by nature were making haste to leave a house that looked like becoming a cockpit, where chairs were being smashed to provide weapons, and parts of chandeliers were already being used as missiles.
One of these hurled by the hand of a gentleman in one of the boxes narrowly missed Scaramouche where he stood, looking down in a sort of grim triumph upon the havoc which his words had wrought. Knowing of what inflammable material the audience was composed, he had deliberately flung down amongst them the lighted torch of discord, to produce this conflagration.
He saw men falling quickly into groups representative of one side or the other of this great quarrel that already was beginning to agitate the whole of France. Their rallying cries were ringing through the theatre.
“Down with the canaille!” from some.
“Down with the privileged!” from others.
And then above the general din one cry rang out sharply and insistently:
“To the box! Death to the butcher of Rennes! Death to La Tour d’Azyr who makes war upon the people!”
There was a rush for one of the doors of the pit that opened upon the staircase leading to the boxes.
And now, whilst battle and confusion spread with the speed of fire, overflowing from the theatre into the street itself, La Tour d’Azyr’s box, which had become the main object of the attack of the bourgeoisie, had also become the rallying ground for such gentlemen as were present in the theatre and for those who, without being men of birth themselves, were nevertheless attached to the party of the nobles.
La Tour d’Azyr had quitted the front of the box to meet those who came to join him. And now in the pit one group of infuriated gentlemen, in attempting to reach the stage across the empty orchestra, so that they might deal with the audacious comedian who was responsible for this explosion, found themselves opposed and held back by another group composed of men to whose feelings André-Louis had given expression.
Perceiving this, and remembering the chandelier, he turned to Leandre, who had remained beside him.
“I think it is time to be going,” said he.
Leandre, looking ghastly under his paint, appalled by the storm which exceeded by far anything that his unimaginative brain could have conjectured, gurgled an inarticulate agreement.

===Chinese===

带着女眷的和天生胆小的，赶紧逃离这座眼看要变成斗鸡场的园子——椅子正在被砸成武器，吊灯上的零件已经开始当飞弹使。
一件这样的飞弹从某个包厢里一位先生手中掷出，擦着斯卡拉穆恰的头顶飞过，险些命中。他站在那里，带着一种冷酷的得意俯瞰自己一番言辞酿成的浩劫。他深知这批观众是什么易燃材料构成的，故意把点着的火把丢进他们中间，好烧起这场大火。
他眼看着人们迅速站队，分成这场已经开始搅动全法兰西的大论战的两边。口号声在剧场里回荡。
“打倒贱民！”有人喊。
“打倒特权！”有人应。
随后，一片鼎沸之上，一声喊拔地而起，又尖又急：
“冲包厢！处死雷恩的屠夫！处死同人民为敌的拉图尔·达齐尔！”
池座里一扇通往包厢楼梯的门，被人潮冲开。
此刻，战火和混乱以火势般的速度蔓延，从剧场漫到街上；拉图尔·达齐尔的包厢，既已成了资产者攻击的主要目标，也成了在场的绅士们——连同那些虽非世家出身、却依附贵族一党的人——的集结地。
拉图尔·达齐尔已离开包厢前口，去迎那些来同他会合的人。这时池座里有一伙怒不可遏的绅士，想穿过空荡荡的乐池扑上台去，收拾这个闯了大祸的狂妄戏子，却被另一伙人拦住顶回——后一伙，正是安德烈-路易替他们出了那口气的人。
见此情景，又想起那盏吊灯，他转向一直守在旁边的莱昂德。
“我看该走了。”他说。
莱昂德油彩下面死灰着脸，被这场远非他贫瘠的想象力所能梦想的风暴吓破了胆，喉咙里咕噜出一句含混的赞同。

===Original===

But it looked as if already they were too late, for in that moment they were assailed from behind.
M. Binet had succeeded at last in breaking past Polichinelle and Rhodomont, who in view of his murderous rage had been endeavouring to restrain him. Half a dozen gentlemen, habitués of the greenroom, had come round to the stage to disembowel the knave who had created this riot, and it was they who had flung aside those two comedians who hung upon Binet. After him they came now, their swords out; but after them again came Polichinelle, Rhodomont, Harlequin, Pierrot, Pasquariel, and Basque the artist, armed with such implements as they could hastily snatch up, and intent upon saving the man with whom they sympathized in spite of all, and in whom now all their hopes were centred.
Well ahead rolled Binet, moving faster than any had ever seen him move, and swinging the long cane from which Pantaloon is inseparable.
“Infamous scoundrel!” he roared. “You have ruined me! But, name of a name, you shall pay!”
André-Louis turned to face him. “You confuse cause with effect,” said he. But he got no farther﻿ ﻿… Binet’s cane, viciously driven, descended and broke upon his shoulder. Had he not moved swiftly aside as the blow fell it must have taken him across the head, and possibly stunned him. As he moved, he dropped his hand to his pocket, and swift upon the cracking of Binet’s breaking cane came the crack of the pistol with which André-Louis replied.
“You had your warning, you filthy pander!” he cried. And on the word he shot him through the body.
Binet went down screaming, whilst the fierce Polichinelle, fiercer than ever in that moment of fierce reality, spoke quickly into André-Louis’ ear:
“Fool! So much was not necessary! Away with you now, or you’ll leave your skin here! Away with you!”
André-Louis thought it good advice, and took it. The gentlemen who had followed Binet in that punitive rush upon the stage, partly held in check by the improvised weapons of the players, partly intimidated by the second pistol that Scaramouche presented, let him go. He gained the wings, and here found himself faced by a couple of sergeants of the watch, part of the police that was already invading the theatre with a view to restoring order.

===Chinese===

可是看样子已经迟了，就在这一刻，他们背后遭到袭击。
比内先生终于冲破了波利希奈勒和罗多蒙的拦阻——两人见他杀气冲天，一直死死劝阻。六七位常进后台的绅士绕到台上，要挖出这个肇事的奸贼的肚肠，正是他们摔开了缠住比内的那两个艺人。他们此刻跟在他身后追来，剑已出鞘；可他们后面又跟上了波利希奈勒、罗多蒙、哈勒昆、皮埃罗、帕斯夸里埃尔，还有艺人巴斯克，操着各自顺手抢到的家伙，一心要救那个他们到底还是同情的、如今承载着他们全部指望的人。
比内一马当先地滚在前面，动作快得谁都没见过，抡着潘塔隆须臾不离身的那根长手杖。
“无耻的恶棍！”他吼道，“你毁了我！可是，名字的名字，你要拿命来偿！”
安德烈-路易转身面对他。“你把因和果搞混了。”他说。可他的话没能说下去……比内的手杖狠狠抡下，断在他肩上。若不是落杖时他疾快闪身，那一下准砍在他头上，多半要打昏过去。闪身之际，他垂手探进衣袋，比内的手杖折断之声未落，安德烈-路易回敬的手枪已经炸响。
“警告过你了，你这脏拉皮条的！”他喊道。话音未落，一枪打穿了他的身体。
比内惨叫着倒下；凶悍的波利希奈勒——在这个凶悍得真实的时刻比任何时候都凶悍——凑到安德烈-路易耳边急促地说：
“糊涂！哪用得着这样！快走，不然你要把皮留在这儿！快走！”
安德烈-路易觉得这话在理，听了劝。跟着比内冲上台来行私刑的那伙绅士，一半被艺人们操起的临时武器挡住，一半被斯卡拉穆恰亮出的第二支手枪吓住，放他去了。他退进侧幕，却在这里撞上两名巡防军士——警察已经开进剧场来恢复秩序了。

===Original===

The sight of them reminded him unpleasantly of how he must stand towards the law for this night’s work, and more particularly for that bullet lodged somewhere in Binet’s obese body. He flourished his pistol.
“Make way, or I’ll burn your brains!” he threatened them, and intimidated, themselves without firearms, they fell back and let him pass. He slipped by the door of the greenroom, where the ladies of the company had shut themselves in until the storm should be over, and so gained the street behind the theatre. It was deserted. Down this he went at a run, intent on reaching the inn for clothes and money, since it was impossible that he should take the road in the garb of Scaramouche.

===Chinese===

一见他们，他就不痛快地想起今晚这场买卖在法律面前会是什么罪名，尤其是射进比内那身肥肉里某个地方的那颗子弹。他挥着手枪。
“让开，不然轰了你们的脑袋！”他威胁道；两人没有火器，被吓退了，放他过去。他侧身闪过后台化妆间的门——戏班的女眷们把自己反锁在里面躲风头——从那里绕到剧场后面的街上。街上空无一人。他顺着街一路奔逃，一心要赶回客栈取衣裳和钱：穿着斯卡拉穆恰这身行头，是上不了路的。

## IV The Heritage / 四 遗产
===Original===

It was M. de Vilmorin’s desire that the matter should be settled out of hand. In this he was at once objective and subjective. A prey to emotions sadly at conflict with his priestly vocation, he was above all in haste to have done, so that he might resume a frame of mind more proper to it. Also he feared himself a little; by which I mean that his honour feared his nature. The circumstances of his education, and the goal that for some years now he had kept in view, had robbed him of much of that spirited brutality that is the birthright of the male. He had grown timid and gentle as a woman. Aware of it, he feared that once the heat of his passion was spent he might betray a dishonouring weakness in the ordeal.
M. le Marquis, on his side, was no less eager for an immediate settlement; and since they had M. de Chabrillane to act for his cousin, and André-Louis to serve as witness for M. de Vilmorin, there was nothing to delay them.
And so, within a few minutes, all arrangements were concluded, and you behold that sinisterly intentioned little group of four assembled in the afternoon sunshine on the bowling-green behind the inn. They were entirely private, screened more or less from the windows of the house by a ramage of trees, which, if leafless now, was at least dense enough to provide an effective lattice.
There were no formalities over measurements of blades or selection of ground. M. le Marquis removed his sword-belt and scabbard, but declined﻿—not considering it worth while for the sake of so negligible an opponent﻿—to divest himself either of his shoes or his coat. Tall, lithe, and athletic, he stood to face the no less tall, but very delicate and frail, M. de Vilmorin. The latter also disdained to make any of the usual preparations. Since he recognized that it could avail him nothing to strip, he came on guard fully dressed, two hectic spots above the cheekbones burning on his otherwise grey face.
M. de Chabrillane, leaning upon a cane﻿—for he had relinquished his sword to M. de Vilmorin﻿—looked on with quiet interest. Facing him on the other side of the combatants stood André-Louis, the palest of the four, staring from fevered eyes, twisting and untwisting clammy hands.
His every instinct was to fling himself between the antagonists, to protest against and frustrate this meeting.

===Chinese===

维尔莫兰先生巴不得这事当场了断。这一点上，他既客观又主观。他受着同神职激烈冲突的情绪的煎熬，最要紧的是快点了结，好恢复一种更合乎神职的心境。他也有一点怕自己；我是说，他的荣誉害怕他的天性。他所受的教育，以及他几年来盯住不放的目标，剥去了他许多男子汉与生俱来的血气。他长得像女人一样怯懦温柔。他自知如此，生怕激情的热劲一过，他会在那场考验里露出丢人的软弱。
侯爵先生那边，同样急盼当场了结；既然有夏布里扬先生替他表亲充当助手，又有安德烈-路易给维尔莫兰先生做见证，便再没有什么可耽搁的了。
就这样，几分钟工夫，一切安排停当，你便看见那四个心怀凶意的人，在午后的阳光里，聚到客栈后面的滚木球草坪上。四下无人，一丛树木多少遮住了宅子的窗户——树虽光秃，枝干却密得像一道有效的格栅。
量剑长、选场地，一应虚文全免。侯爵先生解下佩剑的腰带和剑鞘，却不肯——为这样不足挂齿的对手，犯不着——再脱掉鞋子和外衣。他顾长、柔韧、矫健，站着迎对面同样顾长、却十分单薄孱弱的维尔莫兰先生。后者也不屑于作任何惯常的准备。他明白脱了衣服也无济于事，便穿戴整齐地上前交剑，灰败的脸上，颧骨上方烧着两团热病似的红晕。
夏布里扬先生拄着手杖——他的剑已经让给维尔莫兰先生了——神色安静地观看着，饶有兴味。在交手两人的另一侧，同他相对站着安德烈-路易，四个人里数他脸色最白，一双眼睛烧着，两汪冷汗的手绞了又绞，松了又松。
他的每一分本能都叫喊着要扑到两个对手中间去，抗议这场决斗，搅散它。

===Original===

That sane impulse was curbed, however, by the consciousness of its futility. To calm him, he clung to the conviction that the issue could not really be very serious. If the obligations of Philippe’s honour compelled him to cross swords with the man he had struck, M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s birth compelled him no less to do no serious hurt to the unfledged lad he had so grievously provoked. M. le Marquis, after all, was a man of honour. He could intend no more than to administer a lesson; sharp, perhaps, but one by which his opponent must live to profit. André-Louis clung obstinately to that for comfort.
Steel beat on steel, and the men engaged. The Marquis presented to his opponent the narrow edge of his upright body, his knees slightly flexed and converted into living springs, whilst M. de Vilmorin stood squarely, a full target, his knees wooden. Honour and the spirit of fair play alike cried out against such a match.
The encounter was very short, of course. In youth, Philippe had received the tutoring in swordplay that was given to every boy born into his station of life. And so he knew at least the rudiments of what was now expected of him. But what could rudiments avail him here? Three disengages completed the exchanges, and then without any haste the Marquis slid his right foot along the moist turf, his long, graceful body extending itself in a lunge that went under M. de Vilmorin’s clumsy guard, and with the utmost deliberation he drove his blade through the young man’s vitals.
André-Louis sprang forward just in time to catch his friend’s body under the armpits as it sank. Then, his own legs bending beneath the weight of it, he went down with his burden until he was kneeling on the damp turf. Philippe’s limp head lay against André-Louis’ left shoulder; Philippe’s relaxed arms trailed at his sides; the blood welled and bubbled from the ghastly wound to saturate the poor lad’s garments.
With white face and twitching lips, André-Louis looked up at M. de La Tour d’Azyr, who stood surveying his work with a countenance of grave but remorseless interest.
“You have killed him!” cried André-Louis.
“Of course.”
The Marquis ran a lace handkerchief along his blade to wipe it. As he let the dainty fabric fall, he explained himself. “He had, as I told him, a too dangerous gift of eloquence.”
And he turned away, leaving completest understanding with André-Louis.

===Chinese===

可这股清醒的冲动，被「白费劲」三个字压住了。为着定住心神，他死抱住一个信念：结局断不会当真十分严重。如果说菲利普的荣誉责成他同他打了耳光的人交剑，那么拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵的出身，也同样责成他不得给他如此狠心激怒的这没长成的少年任何重创。侯爵先生到底是个讲荣誉的人。他至多存心给一顿教训；厉害些罢了，却必是对手能活着受用的一顿。安德烈-路易死死抱着这一点，给自己宽心。
剑刃相击，两人交上了手。侯爵侧身向着对手，竖直的身躯只给他窄窄一线，双膝微微一屈，化作两股活的弹簧；维尔莫兰先生却端端正正站着，一副整个儿的靶子，两条腿木得笔直。荣誉的精神、公平竞技的精神，都在呐喊着反对这样一场对局。
交锋自然极短。菲利普少年时受过剑术的点拨——凡是生在他那个阶层的孩子都受过——所以他至少懂得眼下这场面的入门功夫。可入门功夫顶什么用？三个虚晃变换便走完了交锋，随后侯爵不慌不忙，右脚顺着湿草滑出，顾长优雅的身躯舒展开一记弓步，剑锋从维尔莫兰先生笨拙的剑防底下穿入，他以最大的从容，把剑刃送进了年轻人的要害。
安德烈-路易抢上前去，恰来得及从他朋友瘫软下去的腋下把身体接住。跟着他自己的双腿被那重量压弯，他同怀里的负担一起沉下去，直到跪在湿草上。菲利普瘫软的头靠在安德烈-路易的左肩上；菲利普松开的两臂垂在身侧；血从可怕的伤口里涌出来，冒着泡，浸透了这可怜少年的衣衫。
安德烈-路易脸色惨白，嘴唇抽搐着，抬头望拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵——侯爵正站在那里，端详自己的手笔，神情凝重，却无一丝悔意。
「你杀了他！」安德烈-路易喊道。
「当然。」
侯爵拿一方花边手帕顺着剑刃抹了过去。他松手让那片精致织物飘落，一面替自己解说：「他那张嘴，我告诉过他，口才太危险。」
说罢他转身走了，把最透彻的醒悟留给了安德烈-路易。

===Original===

Still supporting the limp, draining body, the young man called to him.
“Come back, you cowardly murderer, and make yourself quite safe by killing me too!”
The Marquis half turned, his face dark with anger. Then M. de Chabrillane set a restraining hand upon his arm. Although a party throughout to the deed, the Chevalier was a little appalled now that it was done. He had not the high stomach of M. de La Tour d’Azyr, and he was a good deal younger.
“Come away,” he said. “The lad is raving. They were friends.”
“You heard what he said?” quoth the Marquis.
“Nor can he, or you, or any man deny it,” flung back André-Louis. “Yourself, monsieur, you made confession when you gave me now the reason why you killed him. You did it because you feared him.”
“If that were true﻿—what, then?” asked the great gentleman.
“Do you ask? Do you understand of life and humanity nothing but how to wear a coat and dress your hair﻿—oh, yes, and to handle weapons against boys and priests? Have you no mind to think, no soul into which you can turn its vision? Must you be told that it is a coward’s part to kill the thing he fears, and doubly a coward’s part to kill in this way? Had you stabbed him in the back with a knife, you would have shown the courage of your vileness. It would have been a vileness undisguised. But you feared the consequences of that, powerful as you are; and so you shelter your cowardice under the pretext of a duel.”
The Marquis shook off his cousin’s hand, and took a step forward, holding now his sword like a whip. But again the Chevalier caught and held him.
“No, no, Gervais! Let be, in God’s name!”
“Let him come, monsieur,” raved André-Louis, his voice thick and concentrated. “Let him complete his coward’s work on me, and thus make himself safe from a coward’s wages.”
M. de Chabrillane let his cousin go. He came white to the lips, his eyes glaring at the lad who so recklessly insulted him. And then he checked. It may be that he remembered suddenly the relationship in which this young man was popularly believed to stand to the Seigneur de Gavrillac, and the well-known affection in which the Seigneur held him. And so he may have realized that if he pushed this matter further, he might find himself upon the horns of a dilemma.

===Chinese===

那年轻人仍旧架着这具瘫软的、血往外渗的躯体，冲他喊：
「回来，你这懦夫凶手！把我也杀了，好叫你万无一失！」
侯爵半转过身，脸气得发黑。夏布里扬先生把一只手按住他的手臂。这桩事他从头到尾都是同谋，如今事已做下，他也有些胆寒。他没有拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵那副高傲的胆量，何况他年轻得多。
「走吧，」他说，「这孩子说胡话呢。他们原是朋友。」
「你听见他说的话了？」侯爵道。
「他不能否认，你也不能，谁也不能！」安德烈-路易回敬。「您自己，先生，方才您说出杀他的缘由时，就已经招认了。您杀他，是因为您怕他。」
「就算是真的——那又怎样？」这位大贵人问。
「还要问？难不成人生和人道，您懂的只剩下怎么穿衣裳、怎么梳理头发——哦，是，还有怎么拿兵器对付孩子和教士？您就没有一颗会想事的心，没有一个灵魂，容您往里看上一眼？还用得着人来告诉您，杀掉他所怕的东西，是懦夫的行径；用这样的法子杀，更是加倍的懦夫么？您若用刀子从背后捅了他，倒还显出您卑鄙的胆量，卑鄙得明明白白。可那样的事您怕后果，凭您何等有权势；于是您拿决斗做幌子，把您的怯懦藏在底下。」
侯爵甩开他表亲的手，抢上一步，手里的剑像条鞭子似的提着。可骑士又拉住了他。
「别，别，热尔维（Gervais）！看在上帝分上，随他去！」
「让他来，先生，」安德烈-路易声音发闷发狠，语声急促。「让他在我身上做完他懦夫的活计，好把懦夫的工钱稳稳挣到手。」
夏布里扬先生松开了他的表亲。侯爵走上前来，嘴唇发白，双眼瞪着这个豁出去辱骂他的少年。随后他收住了。也许他猛然想起，众人风传这年轻人同加夫里雅克领主的亲戚关系，想起领主对他众所周知的疼爱。于是他也许明白过来，这事再往前逼，自己就要被架上两难的地步。

===Original===

He would be confronted with the alternatives of shedding more blood, and so embroiling himself with the Lord of Gavrillac at a time when that gentleman’s friendship was of the first importance to him, or else of withdrawing with such hurt to his dignity as must impair his authority in the countryside hereafter.
Be it so or otherwise, the fact remains that he stopped short; then, with an incoherent ejaculation, between anger and contempt, he tossed his arms, turned on his heel and strode off quickly with his cousin.
When the landlord and his people came, they found André-Louis, his arms about the body of his dead friend, murmuring passionately into the deaf ear that rested almost against his lips:
“Philippe! Speak to me, Philippe! Philippe﻿ ﻿… Don’t you hear me? O God of Heaven! Philippe!”
At a glance they saw that here neither priest nor doctor could avail. The cheek that lay against André-Louis’s was leaden-hued, the half-open eyes were glazed, and there was a little froth of blood upon the vacuously parted lips.
Half blinded by tears André-Louis stumbled after them when they bore the body into the inn. Upstairs in the little room to which they conveyed it, he knelt by the bed, and holding the dead man’s hand in both his own, he swore to him out of his impotent rage that M. de La Tour d’Azyr should pay a bitter price for this.
“It was your eloquence he feared, Philippe,” he said. “Then if I can get no justice for this deed, at least it shall be fruitless to him. The thing he feared in you, he shall fear in me. He feared that men might be swayed by your eloquence to the undoing of such things as himself. Men shall be swayed by it still. For your eloquence and your arguments shall be my heritage from you. I will make them my own. It matters nothing that I do not believe in your gospel of freedom. I know it﻿—every word of it; that is all that matters to our purpose, yours and mine. If all else fails, your thoughts shall find expression in my living tongue. Thus at least we shall have frustrated his vile aim to still the voice he feared. It shall profit him nothing to have your blood upon his soul. That voice in you would never half so relentlessly have hounded him and his as it shall in me﻿—if all else fails.”
It was an exulting thought. It calmed him; it soothed his grief, and he began very softly to pray.

===Chinese===

要么再流更多的血，在这位贵人的交情于他头等重要的时候，同加夫里雅克的领主结下怨仇；要么就此撤退，尊严受损，从此削弱他在这片乡间的威势。
不管是不是这个缘故，事实是他停住了脚步；随后，他含混地喊了一声，半是怒半是鄙夷，双臂一甩，转身同他表亲一起疾步而去。
店主人和他的伙计们赶到时，只见安德烈-路易双臂搂着他亡友的身体，对着那只几乎贴着他嘴唇的、再听不见的耳朵，激动地喃喃着：
「菲利普！同我说话，菲利普！菲利普……你听不见我么？哦，上帝啊！菲利普！」
他们一眼看出，这里教士也好、医生也好，都不中用了。贴着安德烈-路易的那半边脸颊是铅色的，半睁的眼睛已经失了神，痴痴张开的嘴唇上有一点血沫。
他们把尸体抬进客栈，安德烈-路易泪水迷了眼，跌跌撞撞跟在后面。在楼上安置尸体的小房间里，他跪在床边，把亡人的手合握在自己两只手里，凭着满腔无处施展的悲愤，向他起誓：拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵必为此付出惨痛的代价。
「他怕的是你的口才，菲利普，」他说。「那么，这件事我若讨不到公道，至少要叫它于他一无所得。他怕你身上的那样东西，他要在我身上怕个够。他怕的是世人的心被你的口才拨动，起来倾覆他这一类人——世人的心还要被它拨动。因为你的口才、你的道理，将是你留给我的遗产。我要把它们变作我自己的。我不信你那套自由的福音，这无关紧要。我懂得它——一字一句都懂；这于我们的目的，你的和我的，才是唯一紧要的。倘若万般不成，你的思想要从我活着的舌头里发出声来。这样，他掐灭他所怕的那个声音的卑污图谋，至少要落空。叫你的血担在他灵魂上，于他一无好处。那声音在你身上，断不会像在我身上这样，穷追他不舍，追他和他那一类——倘若万般不成。」
这是一个叫人振奋的念头。它叫他平静了；它抚慰了他的悲伤，他开始轻声地祈祷起来。

===Original===

And then his heart trembled as he considered that Philippe, a man of peace, almost a priest, an apostle of Christianity, had gone to his Maker with the sin of anger on his soul. It was horrible. Yet God would see the righteousness of that anger. And in no case﻿—be man’s interpretation of Divinity what it might﻿—could that one sin outweigh the loving good that Philippe had ever practised, the noble purity of his great heart. God after all, reflected André-Louis, was not a grand-seigneur.

===Chinese===

随后他的心又颤了：他想到菲利普，一个爱好和平的人，半个神父，一个基督的信徒，竟带着愤怒的罪去了上帝面前。这太可怕了。可上帝会看见那愤怒的正义。而且无论如何——任凭人对神意作何解说——那一桩罪，断然压不过菲利普一生躬行的仁善，压不过他那颗伟大的心高贵而纯洁的善。归根到底，安德烈-路易想道，上帝并不是一位大领主。

## V The Lord of Gavrillac / 五 加夫里雅克的领主
===Original===

For the second time that day André-Louis set out for the château, walking briskly, and heeding not at all the curious eyes that followed him through the village, and the whisperings that marked his passage through the people, all agog by now with that day’s event in which he had been an actor.
He was ushered by Bénoît, the elderly body-servant, rather grandiloquently called the seneschal, into the ground-floor room known traditionally as the library. It still contained several shelves of neglected volumes, from which it derived its title, but implements of the chase﻿—fowling-pieces, powder-horns, hunting-bags, sheath-knives﻿—obtruded far more prominently than those of study. The furniture was massive, of oak richly carved, and belonging to another age. Great massive oak beams crossed the rather lofty whitewashed ceiling.
Here the squat Seigneur de Gavrillac was restlessly pacing when André-Louis was introduced. He was already informed, as he announced at once, of what had taken place at the Breton Armé. M. de Chabrillane had just left him, and he confessed himself deeply grieved and deeply perplexed.
“The pity of it!” he said. “The pity of it!” He bowed his enormous head. “So estimable a young man, and so full of promise. Ah, this La Tour d’Azyr is a hard man, and he feels very strongly in these matters. He may be right. I don’t know. I have never killed a man for holding different views from mine. In fact, I have never killed a man at all. It isn’t in my nature. I shouldn’t sleep of nights if I did. But men are differently made.”
“The question, monsieur my godfather,” said André-Louis, “is what is to be done.” He was quite calm and self-possessed, but very white.
M. de Kercadiou stared at him blankly out of his pale eyes.
“Why, what the devil is there to do? From what I am told, Vilmorin went so far as to strike M. le Marquis.”
“Under the very grossest provocation.”
“Which he himself provoked by his revolutionary language. The poor lad’s head was full of this encyclopaedist trash. It comes of too much reading. I have never set much store by books, André; and I have never known anything but trouble to come out of learning. It unsettles a man. It complicates his views of life, destroys the simplicity which makes for peace of mind and happiness. Let this miserable affair be a warning to you, André.

===Chinese===

那天里这是第二回，安德烈-路易动身往庄院去，脚程急急，全不理会村人好奇的眼睛一路跟着他，不理会他打人丛中穿行时四下的窃语——那桩他亲身参与的事，如今已叫全村人心痒难熬了。
引他进去的是贝努瓦，上了年纪的贴身老仆，人给他说得响亮些，叫做总管。进的是楼下那间祖上传下来唤作书房的屋子。屋里确实还摆着几架无人过问的书，屋子也正由此得名；可打猎的家什——鸟枪、药角、猎袋、带鞘猎刀——抢眼的程度远胜书本。家具厚重，橡木雕花繁复，属于另一个时代。粗大的橡木梁横过粉刷得雪白、颇见高敞的天花板。
安德烈-路易被领进去时，矮壮的加夫里雅克领主正在屋里焦躁地踱步。布雷东·阿梅出的事，他已经得了信——他当场就这样宣布；夏布里扬先生才离开他。他坦言自己悲恸，也着实困惑。
「可惜了！可惜了！」他说，硕大的脑袋点了点。「这么一个可敬的年轻人，前程那样远大。唉，这位拉图尔·达齐尔是个狠人，这类事上他的感情极重。兴许他对。我说不好。我从来没有为着别人同我见解不同就杀人的。说真的，我压根儿没杀过人。这不在我的天性里。杀了人我夜里要睡不着觉的。可人是各色各样生的。」
「问题，教父先生，」安德烈-路易说，「在于眼下怎么办。」他十分平静镇定，只是脸色惨白。
凯尔卡迪乌先生一双淡色的眼睛茫然瞪着他。
「嗐，还能怎么办，见鬼？照我听说的，维尔莫兰竟动手打了侯爵先生。」
「那是在最横暴的挑逗之下。」
「那挑逗也是他自己那套造反话招来的。这可怜的小伙子，一脑袋百科全书派的乌烟瘴气。都是书读多了的毛病。我从来不怎么看重书本，安德烈；学问带来的，我就没见过别样，只有祸事。它叫人心神不定。它把人对人生的看法搅复杂了，毁了那份让人心境平和、日子安稳的单纯。让这桩倒霉事给你个教训吧，安德烈。

===Original===

You are, yourself, too prone to these new-fashioned speculations upon a different constitution of the social order. You see what comes of it. A fine, estimable young man, the only prop of his widowed mother too, forgets himself, his position, his duty to that mother﻿—everything; and goes and gets himself killed like this. It is infernally sad. On my soul it is sad.” He produced a handkerchief, and blew his nose with vehemence.
André-Louis felt a tightening of his heart, a lessening of the hopes, never too sanguine, which he had founded upon his godfather.
“Your criticisms,” he said, “are all for the conduct of the dead, and none for that of the murderer. It does not seem possible that you should be in sympathy with such a crime.”
“Crime?” shrilled M. de Kercadiou. “My God, boy, you are speaking of M. de La Tour d’Azyr.”
“I am, and of the abominable murder he has committed﻿ ﻿…”
“Stop!” M. de Kercadiou was very emphatic. “I cannot permit that you apply such terms to him. I cannot permit it. M. le Marquis is my friend, and is likely very soon to stand in a still closer relationship.”
“Notwithstanding this?” asked André-Louis.
M. de Kercadiou was frankly impatient.
“Why, what has this to do with it? I may deplore it. But I have no right to condemn it. It is a common way of adjusting differences between gentlemen.”
“You really believe that?”
“What the devil do you imply, André? Should I say a thing that I don’t believe? You begin to make me angry.”
“ ‘Thou shalt not kill,’ is the King’s law as well as God’s.”
“You are determined to quarrel with me, I think. It was a duel﻿ ﻿…”
André-Louis interrupted him. “It is no more a duel than if it had been fought with pistols of which only M. le Marquis’s was loaded. He invited Philippe to discuss the matter further, with the deliberate intent of forcing a quarrel upon him and killing him. Be patient with me, monsieur my godfather. I am not telling you of what I imagine but what M. le Marquis himself admitted to me.”
Dominated a little by the young man’s earnestness, M. de Kercadiou’s pale eyes fell away. He turned with a shrug, and sauntered over to the window.
“It would need a court of honour to decide such an issue. And we have no courts of honour,” he said.
“But we have courts of justice.”
With returning testiness the seigneur swung round to face him again.

===Chinese===

你自己就太沾染这些时新花样，动辄议论什么改换社会秩序。你看看这落得什么下场。一个好好的、可敬的年轻人，寡母唯一的依靠，忘了自家的身份、名分、对母亲的责任——什么都忘了；就这样跑去把自己送了命。糟透了，糟透了。凭我的灵魂说，真叫人痛心。」他掏出手帕，狠狠掉了一把鼻子。
安德烈-路易觉得心口一紧，他把寄在教父身上的指望——本就不甚热切——又往下落了几分。
「您的责备，」他说，「全冲着死者的行事，凶手那边倒一字不提。您居然会同情这样一桩罪，这不像话。」
「罪？」凯尔卡迪乌先生尖声叫起来。「我的上帝，孩子，你说的是拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵。」
「说的就是他，还有他犯下的这桩可憎的谋杀……」
「住口！」凯尔卡迪乌先生斩钉截铁。「我不能容你拿这种字眼加在他身上。我不能容。侯爵先生是我的朋友，而且眼看着就要结成更近的亲戚。」
「出了这样的事，还结？」安德烈-路易问。
凯尔卡迪乌先生干脆不耐烦起来。
「嗐，这跟那有什么相干？我可以痛惜。可我没有权利问罪。绅士之间化解嫌隙，这是寻常的路数。」
「您当真信这个？」
「你这话什么意思，安德烈？难道我还会说自己不信的话？你要惹我动气了。」
「『不可杀人』，这是上帝的诚命，也是王法。」
「我看你是存心同我吵架。那是一场决斗……」
安德烈-路易打断他。「这哪里算决斗，就好比两人用手枪对射，只有侯爵先生的枪里上了子弹。他请菲利普去把那件事再谈谈，存的就是这个心：硬把一场争端栽到他头上，好杀了他。您耐心听我一句，教父先生。我说的不是我的臆测，是侯爵先生亲口向我认下的。」
叫年轻人的这股恳切压住了几分，凯尔卡迪乌先生一双淡色的眼睛避开了。他耸耸肩，踱到窗前。
「这种事，得有个荣誉法庭来裁断才是。可我们没有荣誉法庭。」他说。
「可我们有司法法庭。」
领主又挂上烦躁，猛地转身对着他。

===Original===

“And what court of justice, do you think, would listen to such a plea as you appear to have in mind?”
“There is the court of the King’s Lieutenant at Rennes.”
“And do you think the King’s Lieutenant would listen to you?”
“Not to me, perhaps, Monsieur. But if you were to bring the plaint﻿ ﻿…”
“I bring the plaint?” M. de Kercadiou’s pale eyes were wide with horror of the suggestion.
“The thing happened here on your domain.”
“I bring a plaint against M. de La Tour d’Azyr! You are out of your senses, I think. Oh, you are mad; as mad as that poor friend of yours who has come to this end through meddling in what did not concern him. The language he used here to M. le Marquis on the score of Mabey was of the most offensive. Perhaps you didn’t know that. It does not at all surprise me that the Marquis should have desired satisfaction.”
“I see,” said André-Louis, on a note of hopelessness.
“You see? What the devil do you see?”
“That I shall have to depend upon myself alone.”
“And what the devil do you propose to do, if you please?”
“I shall go to Rennes, and lay the facts before the King’s Lieutenant.”
“He’ll be too busy to see you.” And M. de Kercadiou’s mind swung a trifle inconsequently, as weak minds will. “There is trouble enough in Rennes already on the score of these crazy States General, with which the wonderful M. Necker is to repair the finances of the kingdom. As if a peddling Swiss bank-clerk, who is also a damned Protestant, could succeed where such men as Calonne and Brienne have failed.”
“Good afternoon, monsieur my godfather,” said André-Louis.
“Where are you going?” was the querulous demand.
“Home at present. To Rennes in the morning.”
“Wait, boy, wait!” The squat little man rolled forward, affectionate concern on his great ugly face, and he set one of his podgy hands on his godson’s shoulder. “Now listen to me, André,” he reasoned. “This is sheer knight-errantry﻿—moonshine, lunacy. You’ll come to no good by it if you persist. You’ve read *Don Quixote*, and what happened to him when he went tilting against windmills. It’s what will happen to you, neither more nor less. Leave things as they are, my boy. I wouldn’t have a mischief happen to you.”
André-Louis looked at him, smiling wanly.
“I swore an oath today which it would damn my soul to break.”
“You mean that you’ll go in spite of anything that I may say?” Impetuous as he was inconsequent, M.

「你想哪个司法法庭，肯听你心里打算的这种状子？」
