「还真有，安德烈。鲁加尼不是告诉你，还有别人……」
「他提过普卢加斯特尔夫人和她的仆人。」
「那为什么……？」凯尔卡迪乌先生顿住了，眼睛把话问了出来。
安德烈-路易极郑重地摇了摇头。
「那个办不到。」他说。
凯尔卡迪乌先生惊得张开了嘴。「办不到！」他重复道，「为什么？」
「先生，我为阿琳做的这一切，可以不亏良心。再说，为阿琳我就肯亏一亏良心。可普卢加斯特尔夫人的情形全然不同。阿琳和她的家里人都没有沾过反革命的勾当——那才是眼下这场大祸的真正根子。我能问心无愧地把她迁出巴黎，深信自己做的没有一件可以受人指摘、没有一件会招来查问。可普卢加斯特尔夫人是普卢加斯特尔伯爵的妻子，而普卢加斯特尔伯爵是宫廷同流亡贵族之间的搭线人，这是天下皆知的。」
「那不是她的过错。」凯尔卡迪乌先生大惊失色地喊。
「我认。可她随时可能被叫去证明自己不曾同那些阴谋沾边。人家知道她今天在巴黎。倘若明天找她不着，查出她已经走了，追查必定蜂起，追到头来，必是我背弃所托、滥用权力以营私。我希望，先生，您能明白：为一个外人担这样的风险，太大了。」
「外人？」领主责备地说。
「对我来说，实在算是个外人。」安德烈-路易说。
「可她于我不是外人，安德烈。她是我表妹，是我极亲极看重的朋友。而且，我的上帝！你这番话，只说明把她弄出巴黎更急了。必须救她，安德烈，不惜一切——必须救她！

===Original===

Why, her case is infinitely more urgent than Aline’s!”
He stood a suppliant before his godson, very different now from the stern man who had greeted him on his arrival. His face was pale, his hands shook, and there were beads of perspiration on his brow.
“Monsieur my godfather, I would do anything in reason. But I cannot do this. To rescue her might mean ruin for Aline and yourself as well as for me.”
“We must take the risk.”
“You have a right to speak for yourself, of course.”
“Oh, and for you, believe me, André, for you!” He came close to the young man. “André, I implore you to take my word for that, and to obtain this permit for Mme. de Plougastel.”
André looked at him mystified. “This is fantastic,” he said. “I have grateful memories of the lady’s interest in me for a few days once when I was a child, and again more recently in Paris when she sought to convert me to what she accounts the true political religion. But I do not risk my neck for her﻿—no, nor yours, nor Aline’s.”
“Ah! But, André﻿ ﻿…”
“That is my last word, monsieur. It is growing late, and I desire to sleep in Paris.”
“No, no! Wait!” The Lord of Gavrillac was displaying signs of unspeakable distress. “André, you must!”
There was in this insistence and, still more, in the frenzied manner of it, something so unreasonable that André could not fail to assume that some dark and mysterious motive lay behind it.
“I must?” he echoed. “Why must I? Your reasons, monsieur?”
“André, my reasons are overwhelming.”
“Pray allow me to be the judge of that.” André-Louis’ manner was almost peremptory.
The demand seemed to reduce M. de Kercadiou to despair. He paced the room, his hands tight-clasped behind him, his brow wrinkled. At last he came to stand before his godson.
“Can’t you take my word for it that these reasons exist?” he cried in anguish.
“In such a matter as this﻿—a matter that may involve my neck? Oh, monsieur, is that reasonable?”
“I violate my word of honour, my oath, if I tell you.” M. de Kercadiou turned away, wringing his hands, his condition visibly piteous; then turned again to André. “But in this extremity, in this desperate extremity, and since you so ungenerously insist, I shall have to tell you. God help me, I have no choice. She will realize that when she knows. André, my boy﻿ ﻿…” He paused again, a man afraid.

===Chinese===

哎呀，她的情形比阿琳的急切何止百倍！」
他在教子面前站着，一副哀求的样子，同先前迎接教子时那个严厉的人判若两人。他脸色发白，双手发抖，额头上挂着汗珠。
「教父先生，情理之内我什么都肯做。可这件事我做不得。救她，可能把阿琳、把您、也把我自己一并断送。」
「风险只好担了。」
「您有权替您自己说话，自然。」
「哦，也替你，信我，安德烈，也替你！」他凑近年轻人。「安德烈，我求你信我这一句，替普卢加斯特尔夫人把那张通行证办下来。」
安德烈莫名其妙地望着他。「这也太玄了。」他说。「我做孩子时，那位夫人对我有过几天照拂，我铭记在心；前些时候在巴黎，她又想把我说转到她认作正统的政治信仰上去。可我不为她拿脖子冒险——不，也不为您，也不为阿琳。」
「啊！可是，安德烈……」
「我话说到头了，先生。夜深了，我要赶回巴黎睡觉去。」
「别，别，等等！」加夫里雅克的领主显出难以言表的焦灼。「安德烈，你必须！」
这番执拗，尤其是这副近乎发狂的模样里，有一样东西这样不合情理，安德烈不能不认定，底下藏着某个阴暗的秘密动机。
「我必须？」他应道，「为什么必须？说说您的理由，先生？」
「安德烈，我的理由是压倒一切的。」
「请容我自己来判断。」安德烈-路易的口气近乎命令。
这要求像是把凯尔卡迪乌先生逼到了绝境。他在屋里踱步，双手紧背在身后，眉头拧成一团。末了他过来立在教子面前。
「你就不能信我这理由确凿存在么？」他痛苦地喊。
「在这样的事上——一件可能搭上我脖子的事上？哦，先生，这讲道理么？」
「我说出来，就是背弃我的信誉、我的誓言。」凯尔卡迪乌先生转过身去，绞着双手，那副样子着实可怜；跟着又转回来面对安德烈。「可到了这步田地，这走投无路的田地，你又一味相逼，我只好说了。上帝帮帮我，我没有别的路。她知道了会明白的。安德烈，我的孩子……」他又顿住了，是个吓怕了的人。

===Original===

He set a hand on his godson’s shoulder, and to his increasing amazement André-Louis perceived that over those pale, shortsighted eyes there was a film of tears. “Mme. de Plougastel is your mother.”
Followed, for a long moment, utter silence. This thing that he was told was not immediately understood. When understanding came at last André-Louis’ first impulse was to cry out. But he possessed himself, and played the Stoic. He must ever be playing something. That was in his nature. And he was true to his nature even in this supreme moment. He continued silent until, obeying that queer histrionic instinct, he could trust himself to speak without emotion. “I see,” he said, at last, quite coolly.
His mind was sweeping back over the past. Swiftly he reviewed his memories of Mme. de Plougastel, her singular if sporadic interest in him, the curious blend of affection and wistfulness which her manner towards him had always presented, and at last he understood so much that hitherto had intrigued him.
“I see,” he said again; and added now, “Of course, any but a fool would have guessed it long ago.”
It was M. de Kercadiou who cried out, M. de Kercadiou who recoiled as from a blow.
“My God, André, of what are you made? You can take such an announcement in this fashion?”
“And how would you have me take it? Should it surprise me to discover that I had a mother? After all, a mother is an indispensable necessity to getting one’s self born.”
He sat down abruptly, to conceal the too-revealing fact that his limbs were shaking. He pulled a handkerchief from his pocket to mop his brow, which had grown damp. And then, quite suddenly, he found himself weeping.
At the sight of those tears streaming silently down that face that had turned so pale, M. de Kercadiou came quickly across to him. He sat down beside him and threw an arm affectionately over his shoulder.
“André, my poor lad,” he murmured. “I﻿ ﻿… I was fool enough to think you had no heart. You deceived me with your infernal pretence, and now I see﻿ ﻿… I see﻿ ﻿…” He was not sure what it was that he saw, or else he hesitated to express it.
“It is nothing, monsieur. I am tired out, and﻿ ﻿… and I have a cold in the head.” And then, finding the part beyond his power, he abruptly threw it up, utterly abandoned all pretence. “Why﻿ ﻿… why has there been all this mystery?” he asked. “Was it intended that I should never know?”

===Chinese===

他一只手搭上教子的肩头；安德烈-路易愈发惊诧地看见，那双淡色的近视眼上蒙了一层泪。「普卢加斯特尔夫人是你的母亲。」
长长的一刻，四下无声。他听到的这件事，没有立刻被明白过来。等终于明白，安德烈-路易头一个冲动是喊出来。可他按住了，演起了斯多噶。他就得永远演点什么。这是他的天性。在这至高的关头，他也忠于他的天性。他一直沉默着，直到——顺从那股改不掉的戏子本能——他能开口而不带感情了。「我明白了。」他末了说，冷冷的。
他的思绪扫回过去。他飞快地把记忆里的普卢加斯特尔夫人过了一遍：她那份时断时续、异样的关切，她待他的态度里那种疼爱同企盼的古怪掺和——他终于懂了那么多先前叫他纳罕的东西。
「我明白了。」他又说了一遍；随后添了一句：「当然，除了傻子，谁都该早就猜着了。」
喊出声的是凯尔卡迪乌先生，像挨了一击而向后踉跄的也是凯尔卡迪乌先生。
「我的上帝，安德烈，你是甚么造的？这样的消息，你就这样受着？」
「那要我怎样受着？发现自己有个母亲，很叫人惊奇么？说到底，一个人要把自己生下来，母亲是缺不得的必需品。」
他猛地坐下去，好遮掩两条腿在发抖这个泄露太多的事实。他从口袋里扯出手帕，抹了抹湿了的额头。跟着，毫无来由地，他发觉自己在流泪。
看见泪水无声地淌下那张变得惨白的脸，凯尔卡迪乌先生快步走到他跟前，在他身边坐下，一条胳膊慈爱地搭上他的肩头。
「安德烈，我可怜的孩子。」他喃喃道。「我……我蠢到以为你没有心。你那该死的做作骗了我，如今我看见……我看见……」他自己也不清楚看见了什么，或者是说不出口。
「没什么，先生。我累坏了，而且……而且我伤风了。」随后，这个角色实在撑不下去了，他干脆攞了挑子，把一切做作丢了个干净。「为什么……为什么这许多年瞒得这样死？是打定主意永远不叫我知道的么？」

===Original===

“It was, André. It﻿ ﻿… it had to be, for prudence’ sake.”
“But why? Complete your confidence, sir. Surely you cannot leave it there. Having told me so much, you must tell me all.”
“The reason, my boy, is that you were born some three years after your mother’s marriage with M. de Plougastel, some eighteen months after M. de Plougastel had been away with the army, and some four months before his return to his wife. It is a matter that M. de Plougastel has never suspected, and for gravest family reasons must never suspect. That is why the utmost secrecy has been preserved. That is why none was ever allowed to know. Your mother came betimes into Brittany, and under an assumed name spent some months in the village of Moreau. It was while she was there that you were born.”
André-Louis turned it over in his mind. He had dried his tears. And sat now rigid and collected.
“When you say that none was ever allowed to know, you are telling me, of course, that you, monsieur﻿ ﻿…”
“Oh, *mon Dieu*, no!” The denial came in a violent outburst. M. de Kercadiou sprang to his feet propelled from André’s side by the violence of his emotions. It was as if the very suggestion filled him with horror. “I was the only other one who knew. But it is not as you think, André. You cannot imagine that I should lie to you, that *I* should deny you if you were my son?”
“If you say that I am not, monsieur, that is sufficient.”
“You are not. I was Thérèse’s cousin and also, as she well knew, her truest friend. She knew that she could trust me; and it was to me she came for help in her extremity. Once, years before, I would have married her. But, of course, I am not the sort of man a woman could love. She trusted, however, to my love for her, and I have kept her trust.”
“Then, who was my father?”
“I don’t know. She never told me. It was her secret, and I did not pry. It is not in my nature, André.”
André-Louis got up, and stood silently facing M. de Kercadiou.
“You believe me, André.”
“Naturally, monsieur; and I am sorry, I am sorry that I am not your son.”
M. de Kercadiou gripped his godson’s hand convulsively, and held it a moment with no word spoken. Then as they fell away from each other again:
“And now, what will you do, André?” he asked. “Now that you know?”
André-Louis stood awhile, considering, then broke into laughter. The situation had its humours. He explained them.

===Chinese===

「正是，安德烈。这……这不得不如此，为谨慎起见。」
「可为什么？把话说完，先生。总不能说到这里就停。既然告诉我这么多，就得全告诉我。」
「缘故是，我的孩子：你生在你母亲同普卢加斯特尔先生成婚后约三年，那时普卢加斯特尔先生随军离开已有十八个月，离他回到妻子身边还有四个月。这件事普卢加斯特尔先生从不曾起疑，为了最要紧的家门体面，也永不可叫他起疑。所以才守得这样密不透风。所以才从来不许人知道。你母亲早早到了布列塔尼，用一个化名在莫罗村住了几个月。你就是她在那里的时候生的。」
安德烈-路易在心里翻来覆去地掂量。他擦干了泪，端坐着，神色收敛。
「您说从来不许人知道，那自然是说，您，先生……」
「哦，我的上帝，不！」这否认激烈得炸了出来。凯尔卡迪乌先生被自己情绪的猛劲弹起来，离开了安德烈身边。单是这么一猜，就仿佛叫他充满惊骇。「我是仅有的另一个知情的人。可不是你想的那样，安德烈。你想不到我会骗你，想不到你若是我儿子，我会不认你？」
「您说我不是，先生，这就够了。」
「你不是。我是泰蕾茠的表兄，也是——她清楚得很——她最忠实的朋友。她知道信得过我；正是到我这儿，她在绝境里来求助。多年前，我本可以娶她。可我这种人，自然不是女人爱得了的。不过她信托我对她的爱，我也守住了这份信托。」
「那么，我父亲是谁？」
「我不知道。她从没告诉我。那是她的秘密，我没有探问。这不在我的天性里，安德烈。」
安德烈-路易站起来，默默面对着凯尔卡迪乌先生。
「你信我，安德烈。」
「自然，先生；我也遺憾——遺憾我不是您的儿子。」
凯尔卡迪乌先生痉挛般攥住教子的手，一言不发地握了片刻。随后两人重又分开，他问：
「如今你打算怎么办，安德烈？如今你知道了？」
安德烈-路易立了半晦，寻思着，忽然笑出声来。这局面自有它的滑稽处。他把它们说了。

===Original===

“What difference should the knowledge make? Is filial piety to be called into existence by the mere announcement of relationship? Am I to risk my neck through lack of circumspection on behalf of a mother so very circumspect that she had no intention of ever revealing herself? The discovery rests upon the merest chance, upon a fall of the dice of Fate. Is that to weigh with me?”
“The decision is with you, André.”
“Nay, it is beyond me. Decide it who can, I cannot.”
“You mean that you refuse even now?”
“I mean that I consent. Since I cannot decide what it is that I should do, it only remains for me to do what a son should. It is grotesque; but all life is grotesque.”
“You will never, never regret it.”
“I hope not,” said André. “Yet I think it very likely that I shall. And now I had better see Rougane again at once, and obtain from him the other two permits required. Then perhaps it will be best that I take them to Paris myself, in the morning. If you will give me a bed, monsieur, I shall be grateful. I﻿ ﻿… I confess that I am hardly in case to do more tonight.”

===Chinese===

「知道了又能怎样？难道母子之情，凭一句宣布亲戚关系，就能无中生有？难道我该为一个瞒得这样严实——严实到根本不打算露相的母亲，去莽撞地拿脖子冒险？这个发现，全凭最偶然的偶然，凭命运骰子的一掷。这也要左右我么？」
「主意你自己拿，安德烈。」
「不，这超出了我。谁能裁决谁裁决去，我裁决不了。」
「你是说，事到如今你还是不肯？」
「我是说我肯。既然我裁不出我该做什么，那就只剩一条：儿子该做什么，我做什么。荒唐；可人生处处荒唐。」
「你永远、永远不会后悔的。」
「但愿如此，」安德烈说。「可我看多半要后悔。现在我最好立刻再去见鲁加尼，把他需要的另外两张通行证领出来。然后，明早多半还是我自己送进巴黎去最好。您若肯给我一张床，先生，我感激不尽。我……我承认，今夜再干什么，我是干不动了。」

## IX The Paladin of the Third / 九 第三等级的游侠骑士
===Original===

M. Le Chevalier de Chabrillane had been closely connected, you will remember, with the iniquitous affair in which Philippe de Vilmorin had lost his life. We know enough to justify a surmise that he had not merely been La Tour d’Azyr’s second in the encounter, but actually an instigator of the business. André-Louis may therefore have felt a justifiable satisfaction in offering up the Chevalier’s life to the Manes of his murdered friend. He may have viewed it as an act of common justice not to be procured by any other means. Also it is to be remembered that Chabrillane had gone confidently to the meeting, conceiving that he, a practised *ferailleur*, had to deal with a bourgeois utterly unskilled in swordsmanship. Morally, then, he was little better than a murderer, and that he should have tumbled into the pit he conceived that he dug for André-Louis was a poetic retribution. Yet, notwithstanding all this, I should find the cynical note on which André-Louis announced the issue to the Assembly utterly detestable did I believe it sincere. It would justify Aline of the expressed opinion, which she held in common with so many others who had come into close contact with him, that André-Louis was quite heartless.
You have seen something of the same heartlessness in his conduct when he discovered the faithlessness of La Binet, although that is belied by the measures he took to avenge himself. His subsequent contempt of the woman I account to be born of the affection in which for a time he held her. That this affection was as deep as he first imagined, I do not believe; but that it was as shallow as he would almost be at pains to make it appear by the completeness with which he affects to have put her from his mind when he discovered her worthlessness, I do not believe; nor, as I have said, do his actions encourage that belief. Then, again, his callous cynicism in hoping that he had killed Binet is also an affectation. Knowing that such things as Binet are better out of the world, he can have suffered no compunction; he had, you must remember, that rarely level vision which sees things in their just proportions, and never either magnifies or reduces them by sentimental considerations. At the same time, that he should contemplate the taking of life with such complete and cynical equanimity, whatever the justification, is quite incredible.

===Chinese===

你当记得，夏布里扬骑士同那桩害掉菲利普·德·维尔莫兰性命的罪恶勾当，牵连极深。我们有足够的根据可以猜测：他在那场决斗里不光做了拉图尔·达齐尔的副手，实际上还是整件事的撺掇者。因此，安德烈-路易把骑士的性命祭给他亡友的英灵，或许可以感到一种无可指摘的满足。他未尝不把它看作一桩公义的执行，舍此别无他途可得。还应记得：夏布里扬当初赴会时信心十足，满以为自己这个老练的击剑手（ferailleur），对付的是一个剑术全然不精的资产者。所以从道义上说，他比杀人凶手强不了多少；他自以为给安德烈-路易挖坑，结果自己跌了进去，也算得一种诗意的报应。然而尽管如此，倘若我相信安德烈-路易向议会宣布结果时那副冷嘲的腔调出自真心，我也会觉得它可憎透顶。那样的话，阿琳的看法——她同许多接近过他的人所共有的看法——说安德烈-路易全无心肝，便算是说中了。
你已经在发现拉比内那婆娘负心时他的做派里，见识过同样的无心肝，尽管他随后采取的报复手段又否定了这个评语。他后来对这女人的鄙夷，我把它算作他一度对她怀过的情意所生。这情意是否有他起初想象的深，我不信；但它是否又浅到他几乎刻意装出的那个地步——发现她一文不值之后便把她从心里一笔勾销得那样干净——我也不信；而且如我所说，他的行事也不支持这种看法。再者，他希望自己已经杀死了比内时那副冷酷的犬儒腔，也是一种做作。他知道比内这种东西还是离开人世的好，所以不会有什么悔恨；但你须记得，他生就一双罕见的平正眼光，看事物总看它恰当的比例，从不容感伤的考虑把它们放大或缩小。可同时，无论理由多充分，他竟能以那样全然冷酷的平静来掂量杀人这件事，又全然叫人难以置信。

===Original===

Similarly now, it is not to be believed that in coming straight from the Bois de Boulogne, straight from the killing of a man, he should be sincerely expressing his nature in alluding to the fact in terms of such outrageous flippancy. Not quite to such an extent was he the incarnation of Scaramouche. But sufficiently was he so ever to mask his true feelings by an arresting gesture, his true thoughts by an effective phrase. He was the actor always, a man ever calculating the effect he would produce, ever avoiding self-revelation, ever concerned to overlay his real character by an assumed and quite fictitious one. There was in this something of impishness, and something of other things.
Nobody laughed now at his flippancy. He did not intend that anybody should. He intended to be terrible; and he knew that the more flippant and casual his tone, the more terrible would be its effect. He produced exactly the effect he desired.
What followed in a place where feelings and practices had become what they had become is not difficult to surmise. When the session rose, there were a dozen *spadassins* awaiting him in the vestibule, and this time the men of his own party were less concerned to guard him. He seemed so entirely capable of guarding himself; he appeared, for all his circumspection, to have so completely carried the war into the enemy’s camp, so completely to have adopted their own methods, that his fellows scarcely felt the need to protect him as yesterday.
As he emerged, he scanned that hostile file, whose air and garments marked them so clearly for what they were. He paused, seeking the man he expected, the man he was most anxious to oblige. But M. de La Tour d’Azyr was absent from those eager ranks. This seemed to him odd. La Tour d’Azyr was Chabrillane’s cousin and closest friend. Surely he should have been among the first today. The fact was that La Tour d’Azyr was too deeply overcome by amazement and grief at the utterly unexpected event. Also his vindictiveness was held curiously in leash. Perhaps he, too, remembered the part played by Chabrillane in the affair at Gavrillac, and saw in this obscure André-Louis Moreau, who had so persistently persecuted him ever since, an ordained avenger. The repugnance he felt to come to the point, with him, particularly after this culminating provocation, was puzzling even to himself.

===Chinese===

同样，此刻也不可相信：他刚从布洛涅林园（Bois de Boulogne）来，刚杀了一个人，转眼就用那样轻慢得离谱的口吻提起这件事，会是他性情的真流露。他还不至于就此完全变成了斯卡拉穆恰的化身。可他毕竟够得上是了——永远惯于用一个惊人的手势遮掩真情绪，用一句漂亮话遮掩真心思。他永远是演员，一个时刻算计自己将造成什么效果的人，时刻避免自我袒露，时刻用心用一层假造的、全然虚构的性格罩住真性格。这里面有几分淘气，也有几分别的名堂。
这会儿没人笑他的轻慢。他也不打算叫任何人笑。他要的是叫人害怕；他知道，自己的腔调越是轻慢、越是随便，效果便越可怕。他收到的不多不少，正是他要的效果。
在一个情感与行事都已落到这般田地的地方，接下来发生什么不难猜到。散会时，门厅里有十几个带剑的打手（spadassins）候着他，而这一回，他自己一党的人不那么急着护卫他了。他看上去那样完全能够自卫；尽管处处谨慎，他却又那样彻底地把战火烧进了敌人的营地、那样彻底地用起了他们自己的手段，以致他的同僚几乎觉得不像昨天那样有护他的必要了。
他一现身，便扫了一眼那列杀气腾腾的队伍——那副神气、那身装束，一望便知是什么东西。他停了步，寻找他预期的人、他最巴望效劳的人。可德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生竟不在那群踊跃的行伍之中。他觉着蹊跷。拉图尔·达齐尔是夏布里扬的表兄弟兼至交。今天他理该头一个到场。事实是，这个全然意外的变故让拉图尔·达齐尔惊得、痛得过深。再者，他的报复心也被一种古怪的东西勒着。或许他也记得夏布里扬在加夫里雅克那件事里扮演的角色，并且在这个始终追着他不放的、来历不明的安德烈-路易·莫罗身上，看出了一位天数派来的复仇者。他不愿同此人见真章——尤其在这接二连三的挑衅之后——这份抵触连他自己也解不透。

===Original===

But it existed, and it curbed him now.
To André-Louis, since La Tour was not one of that waiting pack, it mattered little on that Tuesday morning who should be the next. The next, as it happened, was the young Vicomte de La Motte-Royau, one of the deadliest blades in the group.
On the Wednesday morning, coming again an hour or so late to the Assembly, André-Louis announced﻿—in much the same terms as he had announced the death of Chabrillane﻿—that M. de La Motte-Royau would probably not disturb the harmony of the Assembly for some weeks to come, assuming that he were so fortunate as to recover ultimately from the effects of an unpleasant accident with which he had quite unexpectedly had the misfortune to meet that morning.
On Thursday he made an identical announcement with regard to the Vidame de Blavon. On Friday he told them that he had been delayed by M. de Troiscantins, and then turning to the members of the Côté Droit, and lengthening his face to a sympathetic gravity:
“I am glad to inform you, messieurs, that M. des Troiscantins is in the hands of a very competent surgeon who hopes with care to restore him to your councils in a few weeks’ time.”
It was paralyzing, fantastic, unreal; and friend and foe in that assembly sat alike stupefied under those bland daily announcements. Four of the most redoubtable spadassinicides put away for a time, one of them dead﻿—and all this performed with such an air of indifference and announced in such casual terms by a wretched little provincial lawyer!
He began to assume in their eyes a romantic aspect. Even that group of philosophers of the Côté Gauche, who refused to worship any force but the force of reason, began to look upon him with a respect and consideration which no oratorical triumphs could ever have procured him.
And from the Assembly the fame of him oozed out gradually over Paris. Desmoulins wrote a panegyric upon him in his paper *Les Revolutions*, wherein he dubbed him the “Paladin of the Third Estate,” a name that caught the fancy of the people, and clung to him for some time. Disdainfully was he mentioned in the *Actes des Apôtres*, the mocking organ of the Privileged party, so light-heartedly and provocatively edited by a group of gentlemen afflicted by a singular mental myopy.

===Chinese===

可它确实存在，此刻它正勒着他。
至于安德烈-路易，既然拉图尔不在这拨候着的人里，那个礼拜二的早晨下一个是谁，对他都无所谓。下一个，碰巧是年轻的拉莫特-罗亚奥子爵（La Motte-Royau），那一伙里数得着的辣手。
礼拜三早晨，他又迟了个把钟头到议会，用了宣布夏布里扬之死时大同小异的措辞宣布：德·拉莫特-罗亚奥先生未来几个星期大概不会来扰乱议会的和谐——倘若他有幸终究能从今早十分意外遭遇的那桩不愉快的变故中康复的话。
礼拜四，他就维达姆·德·布拉冯（Vidame de Blavon）作了同样的宣布。礼拜五，他告诉他们，他叫德·特鲁瓦坎坦先生耽搁了；随后转向右派的议员们，把脸拉成一副同情的严正：
“诸位先生，我高兴地通知各位，德·特鲁瓦坎坦先生已在一位非常高明的外科医生手里；这位医生指望精加调治，几星期后把他修复如初，送还诸位的会议。”
这真叫人瘫倒：荒唐、离奇、不像真的；友敌两造坐在这些不动声色的每日通告之下，一样地目瞪口呆。四个最凶名昭著的剑客杀手（spadassinicides）一时全给收拾了，其中一个已经死了——而这一切，由一个贱不足道的外省小律师办出来，摆出一副满不在乎的神气，用这样随口的措辞宣布！
他在他们眼里开始带上了一层传奇色彩。连左派那群哲学家——那群只肯崇拜理性一种力量的人——也开始对他另眼相看、以礼相待，这是任何口才的胜利都决计挣不来的。
从议会，他的名声又渐渐渗通到巴黎全城。德穆兰在他的《革命报》（Les Révolutions）上写文章捧他，封他做“第三等级的游侠骑士”，这名字投了小民的脾气，粘在他身上好一阵子。而《使徒行传》（Actes des Apôtres）——特权党的讪笑机关报，由一群害着奇特精神近视的绅士们轻狂而挑衅地编辑着——则对他加以轻蔑的提及。

===Original===

The Friday of that very busy week in the life of this young man who even thereafter is to persist in reminding us that he is not in any sense a man of action, found the vestibule of the Manège empty of swordsmen when he made his leisurely and expectant egress between Le Chapelier and Kersain.
So surprised was he that he checked in his stride.
“Have they had enough?” he wondered, addressing the question to Le Chapelier.
“They have had enough of you, I should think,” was the answer. “They will prefer to turn their attention to someone less able to take care of himself.”
Now this was disappointing. André-Louis had lent himself to this business with a very definite object in view. The slaying of Chabrillane had, as far as it went, been satisfactory. He had regarded that as a sort of acceptable hors d’oeuvre. But the three who had followed were no affair of his at all. He had met them with a certain amount of repugnance, and dealt with each as lightly as consideration of his own safety permitted. Was the baiting of him now to cease whilst the man at whom he aimed had not presented himself? In that case it would be necessary to force the pace!
Out there under the awning a group of gentlemen stood in earnest talk. Scanning the group in a rapid glance, André-Louis perceived M. de La Tour d’Azyr amongst them. He tightened his lips. He must afford no provocation. It must be for them to fasten their quarrels upon him. Already the *Actes des Apôtres* that morning had torn the mask from his face, and proclaimed him the fencing-master of the Rue du Hasard, successor to Bertrand des Amis. Hazardous as it had been hitherto for a man of his condition to engage in single combat it was rendered doubly so by this exposure, offered to the public as an aristocratic apologia.
Still, matters could not be left where they were, or he should have had all his pains for nothing. Carefully looking away from that group of gentlemen, he raised his voice so that his words must carry to their ears.
“It begins to look as if my fears of having to spend the remainder of my days in the Bois were idle.”
Out of the corner of his eye he caught the stir his words created in that group. Its members had turned to look at him; but for the moment that was all. A little more was necessary.

===Chinese===

这个对这位年轻人而言万分忙碌的礼拜，其礼拜五——他此后还一直执意提醒我们他决不是一个行动家——当他由勒·夏普利耶和凯尔桑（Kersain）陪同，从容而有所待地走出骑术馆大厅（Manège）时，发现门厅里竟没有一个带剑的。
他惊奇之下竟收住了脚步。
“他们受够了？”他问勒·夏普利耶。
“我看是受够你了，”对方答，“他们宁可把注意力转到一个自卫能力差些的人身上去。”
这可叫人失望。安德烈-路易投身这场买卖，是抱着一个十分明确的目标的。杀夏布里扬，就其本身而言，还差强人意；他把那个看作一道过得去的前菜。可后面跟上来的那三位，同他毫无恩怨。他带几分嫌恶应了他们的战，又只求自保所容许的限度内轻轻打发。如今他瞄准的那个人尚未露面，对他的挑衅就要收场了么？若果如此，那就得加火催一催了！
外头凉棚底下，一群绅士正站在那里剧谈。安德烈-路易目光迅速一扫，认出了其中的德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生。他抿紧了嘴。他决不可先动衅。须得由他们把争吵强加给他。《使徒行传》那天早晨已经搞下了他的面具，宣布他就是机运街的剑术教师、贝特朗·德扎米的继任者。像他这种身份的人私斗，本已够凶险；经这一番以贵族辩调文呈于公众的揭露，更是险上加险。
然而事情不能搁在原地不管，否则他这番辛苦就全白吃了。他小心地把眼光从那群绅士身上移开，提高嗓门，务必叫话传进他们耳朵：
“看来我担心要在布洛涅林园里度过余生的顾虑，是多余的。”
眼角里他瞥见这话在那群人里掠起的骚动。他们转过身来看他；可眼下也仅止于此。还差一点火候。

===Original===

Pacing slowly along between his friends he resumed:
“But is it not remarkable that the assassin of Lagron should make no move against Lagron’s successor? Or perhaps it is not remarkable. Perhaps there are good reasons. Perhaps the gentleman is prudent.”
He had passed the group by now, and he left that last sentence of his to trail behind him, and after it sent laughter, insolent and provoking.
He had not long to wait. Came a quick step behind him, and a hand falling upon his shoulder, spun him violently round. He was brought face to face with M. de La Tour d’Azyr, whose handsome countenance was calm and composed, but whose eyes reflected something of the sudden blaze of passion stirring in him. Behind him several members of the group were approaching more slowly. The others﻿—like André-Louis’ two companions﻿—remained at gaze.
“You spoke of me, I think,” said the Marquis quietly.
“I spoke of an assassin﻿—yes. But to these my friends.” André-Louis’ manner was no less quiet, indeed the quieter of the two, for he was the more experienced actor.
“You spoke loudly enough to be overheard,” said the Marquis, answering the insinuation that he had been eavesdropping.
“Those who wish to overhear frequently contrive to do so.”
“I perceive that it is your aim to be offensive.”
“Oh, but you are mistaken, M. le Marquis. I have no wish to be offensive. But I resent having hands violently laid upon me, especially when they are hands that I cannot consider clean. In the circumstances I can hardly be expected to be polite.”
The elder man’s eyelids flickered. Almost he caught himself admiring André-Louis’ bearing. Rather, he feared that his own must suffer by comparison. Because of this, he enraged altogether, and lost control of himself.
“You spoke of me as the assassin of Lagron. I do not affect to misunderstand you. You expounded your views to me once before, and I remember.”
“But what flattery, monsieur!”
“You called me an assassin then, because I used my skill to dispose of a turbulent hothead who made the world unsafe for me. But how much better are you, M. the fencing-master, when you oppose yourself to men whose skill is as naturally inferior to your own!”
M. de La Tour d’Azyr’s friends looked grave, perturbed. It was really incredible to find this great gentleman so far forgetting himself as to descend to argument with a canaille of a lawyer-swordsman.

===Chinese===

他在两位朋友中间缓步前行，接着说：
“可是，杀害拉格龙的凶手竟不对拉格龙的继任者动手，岂不奇怪？哦，也许并不奇怪。也许自有充分的理由。也许那位先生谨慎。”
他此时已走过那群人，把末尾那句话留在身后，随着话音又抛过去一串笑声，傲慢而挑衅。
他没有等多久。身后响起一记急促的脚步，一只手落在他肩上，猛地把他掼得转身。他面对面撞上德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生：那张俊脸平静而自持，一双眼睛却映出胸中骤然腾起的怒火。他身后，那群人中的几位正缓缓逼来。其余的——同安德烈-路易的两个同伴一样——都站在原地观望。
“我想，您方才说的是我。”侯爵安静地说。
“我说的是一个凶手——是的。不过是对我这几位朋友说的。”安德烈-路易的态度同样安静，实在比对方更安静，因为他是更老练的演员。
“您说得够响，存心叫人听见。”侯爵说，回答的是那句暗指他偷听的话。
“存心想偷听的人，向来有法子听得着。”
“我明白了，您的目的就是要冒犯我。”
“哦，您可想错了，侯爵先生。我并无意冒犯谁。只是我不喜欢被人粗暴地动手动肩，尤其这双手在我看来不见得干净。在这种情形之下，要我还讲客气，未免强人所难。”
年长的那位眼皮一跳。他几乎要忍不住欣赏安德烈-路易的气度了。更确切说，他是怕自己的气度相形见绌。正因如此，他勃然大怒，失了自持。
“您管我叫杀害拉格龙的凶手。我不屑装作没听懂。您从前向我阐发过一套见解，我还记得。”
“何等的抬举，先生！”
“那时您叫我凶手，是因为我凭本领除掉了一个搅得我世上不安分的莽撞狂徒。可您又高明多少呢，剑术教师先生？您专门同那些本领天生不如您的人过不去！”
德·拉图尔·达齐尔的朋友们脸色沉重、惶惶不安。这位大贵人竟然失态到这个地步，同一个律师出身的老剑客的贱民条理起是非来，实在叫人难以置信。

===Original===

And what was worse, it was an argument in which he was being made ridiculous.
“I oppose myself to them!” said André-Louis on a tone of amused protest. “Ah, pardon, M. le Marquis; it is they who chose to oppose themselves to me﻿—and so stupidly. They push me, they slap my face, they tread on my toes, they call me by unpleasant names. What if I am a fencing-master? Must I on that account submit to every manner of ill-treatment from your bad-mannered friends? Perhaps had they found out sooner that I am a fencing-master their manners would have been better. But to blame me for that! What injustice!”
“Comedian!” the Marquis contemptuously apostrophized him. “Does it alter the case? Are these men who have opposed you men who live by the sword like yourself?”
“On the contrary, M. le Marquis, I have found them men who died by the sword with astonishing ease. I cannot suppose that you desire to add yourself to their number.”
“And why, if you please?” La Tour d’Azyr’s face had flamed scarlet before that sneer.
“Oh,” André-Louis raised his eyebrows and pursed his lips, a man considering. He delivered himself slowly. “Because, monsieur, you prefer the easy victim﻿—the Lagrons and Vilmorins of this world, mere sheep for your butchering. That is why.”
And then the Marquis struck him.
André-Louis stepped back. His eyes gleamed a moment; the next they were smiling up into the face of his tall enemy.
“No better than the others, after all! Well, well! Remark, I beg you, how history repeats itself﻿—with certain differences. Because poor Vilmorin could not bear a vile lie with which you goaded him, he struck you. Because you cannot bear an equally vile truth which I have uttered, you strike me. But always is the vileness yours. And now as then for the striker there is﻿ ﻿…” He broke off. “But why name it? You will remember what there is. Yourself you wrote it that day with the point of your too-ready sword. But there. I will meet you if you desire it, monsieur.”
“What else do you suppose that I desire? To talk?”
André-Louis turned to his friends and sighed. “So that I am to go another jaunt to the Bois. Isaac, perhaps you will kindly have a word with one of these friends of M. le Marquis’, and arrange for nine o’clock tomorrow, as usual.”
“Not tomorrow,” said the Marquis shortly to Le Chapelier. “I have an engagement in the country, which I cannot postpone.”

===Chinese===

更糟的是，在这场辩论里，他自己正被弄得滑稽可笑。
“我同他们过不去！”安德烈-路易用一种被逗乐了的抗议腔说。“啊，对不起，侯爵先生；是他们存心同我过不去——而且过得那样蠢。他们撞我，扇我的脸，踩我的脚尖，用难听的名字骂我。我是个剑术教师又怎样？难道就该因此忍受您那帮没教养的朋友百般的欺凌？也许他们早些发现我是剑术教师，教养本来会好些。可拿这个来怪我！多么不公！”
“戏子！”侯爵轻蔑地冲他直呼。“这改变得了案情么？同您作对的这些人，可是像您一样靠剑吃饭的？”
“恰恰相反，侯爵先生，我发现他们是些死在剑下死得出奇容易的人。我想您总不见得打算把自己也算进他们那个数里去。”
“凭什么，恕我请教？”这句讥诮之下，拉图尔·达齐尔的脸腾地烧成了猩红。
“哦，”安德烈-路易扬起眉毛，抿起嘴唇，一副盘算的样子。他慢慢说出来。“凭的是，先生，您偏爱不费力的猎物——这世上的拉格龙们和维尔莫兰们，生来给您宰杀的羔羊。凭的就是这个。”
于是侯爵掴了他一掌。
安德烈-路易退后一步。他的眼睛闪了一下；随即便笑吟吟地仰望着这位高个仇人的脸。
“到底同旁人一样，也不过如此！好，好！请您注意，历史怎样重演——只换了些细节。可怜的维尔莫兰受不了您用来刺他的那句下流谎言，掴了您。您受不了我说出的这句同样下流的真话，掴了我。可下流的始终是您。如今同当年一样，掴人的那位该得……”他顿住。“何必说破？您记得该得什么。那是您自己那天用您那把出鞘太快的剑尖写下的。就这样吧。您若愿意，我奉陪，先生。”
“不然您以为我要什么？聊天么？”
安德烈-路易转向朋友们，叹了口气。“这么说，我又得去一趟布洛涅。伊萨克，有劳你同侯爵先生的哪位朋友讲一声，照老规矩，明天早上九点。”
“不明天。”侯爵冲勒·夏普利耶短短地说。“我在乡间有个约会，推不掉。”

===Original===

Le Chapelier looked at André-Louis.
“Then for M. le Marquis’ convenience, we will say Sunday at the same hour.”
“I do not fight on Sunday. I am not a pagan to break the holy day.”
“But surely the good God would not have the presumption to damn a gentleman of M. le Marquis’ quality on that account? Ah, well, Isaac, please arrange for Monday, if it is not a feast-day or monsieur has not some other pressing engagement. I leave it in your hands.”
He bowed with the air of a man wearied by these details, and threading his arm through Kersain’s withdrew.
“Ah, *Dieu de Dieu!* But what a trick of it you have,” said the Breton deputy, entirely unsophisticated in these matters.
“To be sure I have. I have taken lessons at their hands.” He laughed. He was in excellent good-humour. And Kersain was enrolled in the ranks of those who accounted André-Louis a man without heart or conscience.
But in his *Confessions* he tells us﻿—and this is one of the glimpses that reveal the true man under all that make-believe﻿—that on that night he went down on his knees to commune with his dead friend Philippe, and to call his spirit to witness that he was about to take the last step in the fulfilment of the oath sworn upon his body at Gavrillac two years ago.

===Chinese===

勒·夏普利耶看了看安德烈-路易。
“那就迁就侯爵先生，定礼拜天同一个钟点吧。”
“我礼拜天不打架。我不是异教徒，不肯干犯圣日。”
“可仁慈的上帝总不至于放肆到为这点事，就诅咒掉侯爵先生这样体面的一位绅士吧？唉，好吧，伊萨克，那就请你安排礼拜一，倘若那天不是节日、这位先生也没有别的要紧约会的话。这事我交给你了。”
他带着被这些细节弄倦了的架势鞠了一躬，挎起凯尔桑的胳膊走了。
“啊，我的上帝！”这位在这些事情上全然不入门的布列塔尼议员说，“您这一手玩得真绝。”
“那自然。我是从他们手里学来的功课。”他笑了。他的兴致好得很。于是凯尔桑也入了册，排进那些断定安德烈-路易无心肝、无良知的人的队伍里。
可是他在《忏悔录》里告诉我们——这是层层假面底下照见真人的惊鸿一瞥之一——那天夜里，他跪下来同亡友菲利普默祷，请他的在天之灵作证：他即将走完最后一步，兑现在两年前加夫里雅克、在亡友遗体上立下的誓言。

## I The Republican / 一 共和党人
===Original===

He was born with a gift of laughter and a sense that the world was mad. And that was all his patrimony. His very paternity was obscure, although the village of Gavrillac had long since dispelled the cloud of mystery that hung about it. Those simple Brittany folk were not so simple as to be deceived by a pretended relationship which did not even possess the virtue of originality. When a nobleman, for no apparent reason, announces himself the godfather of an infant fetched no man knew whence, and thereafter cares for the lad’s rearing and education, the most unsophisticated of country folk perfectly understand the situation. And so the good people of Gavrillac permitted themselves no illusions on the score of the real relationship between André-Louis Moreau﻿—as the lad had been named﻿—and Quintin de Kercadiou, Lord of Gavrillac, who dwelt in the big grey house that dominated from its eminence the village clustering below.
André-Louis had learnt his letters at the village school, lodged the while with old Rabouillet, the attorney, who in the capacity of fiscal intendant, looked after the affairs of M. de Kercadiou. Thereafter, at the age of fifteen, he had been packed off to Paris, to the Lycée of Louis Le Grand, to study the law which he was now returned to practise in conjunction with Rabouillet. All this at the charges of his godfather, M. de Kercadiou, who by placing him once more under the tutelage of Rabouillet would seem thereby quite clearly to be making provision for his future.
André-Louis, on his side, had made the most of his opportunities. You behold him at the age of four-and-twenty stuffed with learning enough to produce an intellectual indigestion in an ordinary mind. Out of his zestful study of Man, from Thucydides to the Encyclopaedists, from Seneca to Rousseau, he had confirmed into an unassailable conviction his earliest conscious impressions of the general insanity of his own species. Nor can I discover that anything in his eventful life ever afterwards caused him to waver in that opinion.
In body he was a slight wisp of a fellow, scarcely above middle height, with a lean, astute countenance, prominent of nose and cheekbones, and with lank, black hair that reached almost to his shoulders. His mouth was long, thin-lipped, and humorous.

===Chinese===

他生来带着笑的天赋，还带着一种感觉：这世界是疯的。这就是他的全部家产。连他的生身父亲都是个谜，虽说加夫里雅克村早就拨开了悬在这上面的迷雾。布列塔尼的这些乡下人，并不像表面那样简单，一个装出来的亲戚关系——连点新鲜劲儿都没有——骗不了他们。一位贵人，无缘无故，自认一个不知从哪儿抱来的婴儿的教父，此后又张罗这孩子的养育和教养，最不谙世事的乡下人也完全懂得这是怎么一回事。于是，安德烈-路易·莫罗——这是孩子的名字——同昆坦·德·凯尔卡迪乌、加夫里雅克的领主之间的真实关系，加夫里雅克的善良百姓是毫不存幻想的；领主住在大宅子里，灰扑扑的，从高处俯瞰着脚下簇拥成堆的村庄。
安德烈-路易在村里的学堂开了蒙，寄住在老公证人拉布耶家；拉布耶以财务总管的身份，照管着凯尔卡迪乌先生的事务。此后，十五岁上，他被送去巴黎，进路易大帝中学攻读法律——如今他回来了，同拉布耶合伙执业。这一切都由他教父凯尔卡迪乌先生出钱；教父把他重新安放在拉布耶的羽翼之下，清楚不过地是在为他的前程打算。
安德烈-路易自己呢，把机会用到十二分。你看他二十四岁上，满腹的学问，塞得寻常脑袋要犯积食。他兴致勃勃地研究人，从修昔底德到百科全书派，从塞内加到卢梭，把最早有知觉时对同类的总体疯狂的印象，锤炼成一条不可撼动的定见。我也找不出，往后他那多事的一生里，有什么曾叫他在这个见解上动摇过。
论身体，他是个瘦条条的人，刚过中等身量，一张清瘦精明的脸，鼻梁颧骨都高，乌黑的头发稀稀拉拉直披到肩头。嘴长，唇薄，带笑意。

===Original===

He was only just redeemed from ugliness by the splendour of a pair of ever-questing, luminous eyes, so dark as to be almost black. Of the whimsical quality of his mind and his rare gift of graceful expression, his writings﻿—unfortunately but too scanty﻿—and particularly his *Confessions*, afford us very ample evidence. Of his gift of oratory he was hardly conscious yet, although he had already achieved a certain fame for it in the Literary Chamber of Rennes﻿—one of those clubs by now ubiquitous in the land, in which the intellectual youth of France foregathered to study and discuss the new philosophies that were permeating social life. But the fame he had acquired there was hardly enviable. He was too impish, too caustic, too much disposed﻿—so thought his colleagues﻿—to ridicule their sublime theories for the regeneration of mankind. Himself he protested that he merely held them up to the mirror of truth, and that it was not his fault if when reflected there they looked ridiculous.
All that he achieved by this was to exasperate; and his expulsion from a society grown mistrustful of him must already have followed but for his friend, Philippe de Vilmorin, a divinity student of Rennes, who, himself, was one of the most popular members of the Literary Chamber.
Coming to Gavrillac on a November morning, laden with news of the political storms which were then gathering over France, Philippe found in that sleepy Breton village matter to quicken his already lively indignation. A peasant of Gavrillac, named Mabey, had been shot dead that morning in the woods of Meupont, across the river, by a gamekeeper of the Marquis de La Tour d’Azyr. The unfortunate fellow had been caught in the act of taking a pheasant from a snare, and the gamekeeper had acted under explicit orders from his master.
Infuriated by an act of tyranny so absolute and merciless, M. de Vilmorin proposed to lay the matter before M. de Kercadiou. Mabey was a vassal of Gavrillac, and Vilmorin hoped to move the Lord of Gavrillac to demand at least some measure of reparation for the widow and the three orphans which that brutal deed had made.
But because André-Louis was Philippe’s dearest friend﻿—indeed, his almost brother﻿—the young seminarist sought him out in the first instance.

===Chinese===

把他从丑里赎出来的，是一双永远在探问、亮闪闪的眼睛，黑得几乎纯黑。至于他心思的古怪、谈吐的漂亮——那副难得的天赋——他的著作（可惜太少了），尤其是他的《忏悔录》，给我们留下极充实的证据。演说的天赋他自己还不甚知觉，虽说他在雷恩的文学会里已经因此小有名声——文学会如今遍布全国，法兰西的智识青年聚在那里研习讨论渗透社会生活的新哲学。可他在那里挣下的名声并不叫人羡慕。他太鬼，太刻薄，太爱（他的会友们这样想）嘲弄他们那些再造人类的崇高理论。他自己分辩说，他不过把他们举到真理的镜子跟前，镜子里照出来他们显得可笑，可不能怪他。
他这一套的成就，只是叫人冒火；要不是他的朋友菲利普·德·维尔莫兰（Philippe de Vilmorin）——雷恩的一名神学生，文学会里最受欢迎的成员之一——他早被那个起了疑心的会社除名了。
十一月的一个早晨，菲利普来到加夫里雅克，满载着当时正往法兰西头顶上聚拢的政治风暴的消息，在这个昏昏欲睡的布列塔尼村子里，找到了叫他那本就活跃的义愤更火上浇油的材料。加夫里雅克的一个庄稼人，叫马贝的，当天早晨在河对岸默蓬（Meupont）的林子里，被拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵的一名猎场看守开枪打死了。这个不幸的家伙被抓了现行——正从套索里取一只野鸡——而那看守，是奉了主人明明白白的号令行事。
维尔莫兰先生被这桩如此绝对、如此无情的暴行激怒了，提议把事情摆到凯尔卡迪乌先生面前。马贝是加夫里雅克的佃户，维尔莫兰盼着打动加夫里雅克的领主，为那桩暴行留下的寡妇和三个孤儿，至少讨几分赔偿。
可安德烈-路易是菲利普最亲爱的朋友（简直是他兄弟），这年轻的神学生头一件便是来找他。

===Original===

He found him at breakfast alone in the long, low-ceilinged, white-panelled dining-room at Rabouillet’s﻿—the only home that André-Louis had ever known﻿—and after embracing him, deafened him with his denunciation of M. de La Tour d’Azyr.
“I have heard of it already,” said André-Louis.
“You speak as if the thing had not surprised you,” his friend reproached him.
“Nothing beastly can surprise me when done by a beast. And La Tour d’Azyr is a beast, as all the world knows. The more fool Mabey for stealing his pheasants. He should have stolen somebody else’s.”
“Is that all you have to say about it?”
“What more is there to say? I’ve a practical mind, I hope.”
“What more there is to say I propose to say to your godfather, M. de Kercadiou. I shall appeal to him for justice.”
“Against M. de La Tour d’Azyr?” André-Louis raised his eyebrows.
“Why not?”
“My dear ingenuous Philippe, dog doesn’t eat dog.”
“You are unjust to your godfather. He is a humane man.”
“Oh, as humane as you please. But this isn’t a question of humanity. It’s a question of game-laws.”
M. de Vilmorin tossed his long arms to Heaven in disgust. He was a tall, slender young gentleman, a year or two younger than André-Louis. He was very soberly dressed in black, as became a seminarist, with white bands at wrists and throat and silver buckles to his shoes. His neatly clubbed brown hair was innocent of powder.
“You talk like a lawyer,” he exploded.
“Naturally. But don’t waste anger on me on that account. Tell me what you want me to do.”
“I want you to come to M. de Kercadiou with me, and to use your influence to obtain justice. I suppose I am asking too much.”
“My dear Philippe, I exist to serve you. I warn you that it is a futile quest; but give me leave to finish my breakfast, and I am at your orders.”
M. de Vilmorin dropped into a winged armchair by the well-swept hearth, on which a piled-up fire of pine logs was burning cheerily. And whilst he waited now he gave his friend the latest news of the events in Rennes. Young, ardent, enthusiastic, and inspired by Utopian ideals, he passionately denounced the rebellious attitude of the privileged.
André-Louis, already fully aware of the trend of feeling in the ranks of an order in whose deliberations he took part as the representative of a nobleman, was not at all surprised by what he heard. M.

===Chinese===

他在拉布耶家找到了他——那是安德烈-路易有生以来知道的唯一的家——独自在那间长而低矮、镶白壁板的饭厅里用早饭。拥抱过朋友，他便拿对拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵的声讨劈头盖脸浇下来。
「我已经听说了。」安德烈-路易说。
「你说得好像一点不吃惊似的。」朋友责备他。
「畜生干出畜生的事，没有什么能叫我吃惊。拉图尔·达齐尔是头畜生，天下皆知。马贝偷他的野鸡，愈发是傻瓜。该偷别家的去。」
「你要说的就是这些？」
「还有什么可说的？我自信有颗讲实际的头脑。」
「还有什么可说的，我打算去对你教父凯尔卡迪乌先生说。我要向他求一个公道。」
「告拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵？」安德烈-路易扬起眉毛。
「为什么不能？」
「我亲爱的天真的菲利普，狗不吃狗。」
「你冤枉你教父了。他是个厚道人。」
「哦，要多厚道有多厚道。可这不是人道的问题。这是猎场法的问题。」
维尔莫兰先生嫌恶地把两条长胳膊甩向天。他是个高挑纤瘦的青年绅士，比安德烈-路易小一两岁，穿一身合乎神学生身份的素黑衣裳，手腕和领口缀着白饰边，鞋上钉银扣。棕色头发齐整地束着，不扑粉。
「你说话像个律师。」他炸了。
「自然。可别为这个白朝我动气。说吧，要我做什么。」
「我要你陪我一起去见凯尔卡迪乌先生，用你的影响讨个公道。我算要求过分了吧。」
「我亲爱的菲利普，我生来就是为你效劳的。我警告你，这趟是白跑；可容我把早饭吃完，悉听尊命。」
维尔莫兰先生跌坐在扫得干干净净的壁炉边一张高背扶手椅里，炉膛里松柴堆得高高的，烧得正欢。等待的当口，他把雷恩的最新事态对朋友讲了一遍。他年轻、热烈、满腔热忱，又受着乌托邦理想的鼓舞，把特权阶级的跋扈姿态痛骂了个够。
安德烈-路易呢，他身为一位贵族的代表、参与着那个等级的议事，对那边的风向早一清二楚，听见这些毫不惊讶。维尔莫兰先生却恼火了，朋友居然不肯同他共此义愤。

===Original===

de Vilmorin found it exasperating that his friend should apparently decline to share his own indignation.
“Don’t you see what it means?” he cried. “The nobles, by disobeying the King, are striking at the very foundations of the throne. Don’t they perceive that their very existence depends upon it; that if the throne falls over, it is they who stand nearest to it who will be crushed? Don’t they see that?”
“Evidently not. They are just governing classes, and I never heard of governing classes that had eyes for anything but their own profit.”
“That is our grievance. That is what we are going to change.”
“You are going to abolish governing classes? An interesting experiment. I believe it was the original plan of creation, and it might have succeeded but for Cain.”
“What we are going to do,” said M. de Vilmorin, curbing his exasperation, “is to transfer the government to other hands.”
“And you think that will make a difference?”
“I know it will.”
“Ah! I take it that being now in minor orders, you already possess the confidence of the Almighty. He will have confided to you His intention of changing the pattern of mankind.”
M. de Vilmorin’s fine ascetic face grew overcast. “You are profane, André,” he reproved his friend.
“I assure you that I am quite serious. To do what you imply would require nothing short of divine intervention. You must change man, not systems. Can you and our vapouring friends of the Literary Chamber of Rennes, or any other learned society of France, devise a system of government that has never yet been tried? Surely not. And can they say of any system tried that it proved other than a failure in the end? My dear Philippe, the future is to be read with certainty only in the past. *Ab actu ad posse valet consecutio.* Man never changes. He is always greedy, always acquisitive, always vile. I am speaking of Man in the bulk.”
“Do you pretend that it is impossible to ameliorate the lot of the people?” M. de Vilmorin challenged him.
“When you say the people you mean, of course, the populace. Will you abolish it? That is the only way to ameliorate its lot, for as long as it remains populace its lot will be damnation.”
“You argue, of course, for the side that employs you. That is natural, I suppose.” M. de Vilmorin spoke between sorrow and indignation.
“On the contrary, I seek to argue with absolute detachment. Let us test these ideas of yours.

===Chinese===

「你看不出这意味着什么吗？」他喊道，「贵族们抗命国王，是在砍王座的根脚！他们看不见自己的存亡全系在这上头么？看不见王座一倒，砸得最扁的，正是站得最近的他们么？他们看不见这个么？」
「显然看不见。统治阶级就是统治阶级，我还没听说哪个统治阶级的眼睛长在自家好处以外的地方。」
「这正是我们的冤屈。这正是我们要改的。」
「你们要废除统治阶级？一个有趣的实验。我记得这是创世的最初方案，若不是出了该隐，本来有望成功。」
「我们要做的，」维尔莫兰先生按住性子说，「是把政府换到别人手里。」
「你以为换了手就换了样？」
「我知道会换样。」
「啊！我明白了，你既受了低品神职，想必已经得了全能上帝的信任。他老人家准把更换人类样式的心意，单独透露给你了。」
维尔莫兰先生那张清苦的俊脸阴了下来。「你亵渎了，安德烈。」他责备朋友。
「我向你保证我是认真的。要做你暗示的那种事，非得神明亲自插手不可。要改的是人，不是制度。你，加上咱们雷恩文学会那些夸夸其谈的朋友，或者法兰西随便哪家学术会社，能设计出一套从未试过的政体么？当然不能。试过的制度，又有哪一套到头来不是以失败告终？我亲爱的菲利普，未来只有在过去里才读得准。从已然推未然（Ab actu ad posse valet consecutio）。人从不改变。他永远贪婪，永远攄取，永远卑劣。我说的是总体的人。」
「你是想说，百姓的命就改善不得了？」维尔莫兰先生挑战他。
「你说百姓，当然指的是贱民。你要废除贱民么？那是改善贱民命运的唯一法子；因为贱民存在一日，其命运便是沉沦。」
「你辩护的，自然是雇你的那一方。这也怪不得你，我想。」维尔莫兰先生半是悲恸半是慚懑地说。
「恰恰相反，我力求绝对超然地论辩。来，咱们验一验你这些主意。

===Original===

To what form of government do you aspire? A republic, it is to be inferred from what you have said. Well, you have it already. France in reality is a republic today.”
Philippe stared at him. “You are being paradoxical, I think. What of the King?”
“The King? All the world knows there has been no king in France since Louis XIV. There is an obese gentleman at Versailles who wears the crown, but the very news you bring shows for how little he really counts. It is the nobles and clergy who sit in the high places, with the people of France harnessed under their feet, who are the real rulers. That is why I say that France is a republic; she is a republic built on the best pattern﻿—the Roman pattern. Then, as now, there were great patrician families in luxury, preserving for themselves power and wealth, and what else is accounted worth possessing; and there was the populace crushed and groaning, sweating, bleeding, starving, and perishing in the Roman kennels. That was a republic; the mightiest we have seen.”
Philippe strove with his impatience. “At least you will admit﻿—you have, in fact, admitted it﻿—that we could not be worse governed than we are?”
“That is not the point. The point is should we be better governed if we replaced the present ruling class by another? Without some guarantee of that I should be the last to lift a finger to effect a change. And what guarantees can you give? What is the class that aims at government? I will tell you. The bourgeoisie.”
“What?”
“That startles you, eh? Truth is so often disconcerting. You hadn’t thought of it? Well, think of it now. Look well into this Nantes manifesto. Who are the authors of it?”
“I can tell you who it was constrained the municipality of Nantes to send it to the King. Some ten thousand workmen﻿—shipwrights, weavers, labourers, and artisans of every kind.”
“Stimulated to it, driven to it, by their employers, the wealthy traders and shipowners of that city,” André-Louis replied. “I have a habit of observing things at close quarters, which is why our colleagues of the Literary Chamber dislike me so cordially in debate. Where I delve they but skim. Behind those labourers and artisans of Nantes, counselling them, urging on these poor, stupid, ignorant toilers to shed their blood in pursuit of the will o’ the wisp of freedom, are the sail-makers, the spinners, the shipowners and the slave-traders.

===Chinese===

你们向往哪种政体？共和国——从你的话里推得出来。好，你们已经有了。法兰西今日实际上就是个共和国。」
菲利普瞪着他。「我看你在说反话。那国王算什么？」
「国王？天下皆知，自打路易十四起，法兰西就没有国王了。凡尔赛有个胖子先生头上顶着王冠，可你带来的消息本身就表明，他实际算得了什么。真正掌权的，是坐在高位的僧俗贵人，法兰西的百姓套在轭下，垫着他们的脚。正因如此，我说法兰西是个共和国；一个按最佳样板（罗马样板）建立的共和国。当年同今日一样，有豪奢的世家大族，为自己保住权力、财富以及一切算得上值得占有的东西；也有被压碎的贱民，在罗马的沟渠里呻吟、流汗、流血、挨饿、死亡。那就是共和国，我们见过的最强大的共和国。」
菲利普按捺着不耐。「至少你肯承认（其实你已经承认了）：我们的治，糟得不能再糟了？」
「那不是要点。要点是：把现今的统治阶级换成另一拨，我们会治得更好么？没有这方面的保证，我一根手指也不会动。你能给什么保证？瞄准政权的，是哪个阶级？我告诉你：资产阶级。」
「什么？」
「吓了你一跳，嗯？真理常常叫人坐立不安。没想到吧？那就现在想想。好好看看那份南特请愿书。作者是谁？」
「谁逼着南特市政府把它递给国王的，我倒能告诉你：约莫一万名工人——造船工、织工、苦力，各行各业的匠人。」
「是被人攝掇到这一步、逼到这一步的，主使的是他们的东家，南特的阔商人和船主。」安德烈-路易答道。「我有个习惯，凡事贴着近处看，所以文学会的同僚们在辩论里那样切齿地不喜欢我。他们掠过水面，我潜到水底。在南特那些苦力匠人背后出主意、攝着这帮可怜、愚昧、无知的苦人去为自由那团鬼火流血的，是制帆匠、纺纱商、船主和贩奴商。

===Original===

The slave-traders! The men who live and grow rich by a traffic in human flesh and blood in the colonies, are conducting at home a campaign in the sacred name of liberty! Don’t you see that the whole movement is a movement of hucksters and traders and peddling vassals swollen by wealth into envy of the power that lies in birth alone? The money-changers in Paris who hold the bonds in the national debt, seeing the parlous financial condition of the State, tremble at the thought that it may lie in the power of a single man to cancel the debt by bankruptcy. To secure themselves they are burrowing underground to overthrow a state and build upon its ruins a new one in which they shall be the masters. And to accomplish this they inflame the people. Already in Dauphiny we have seen blood run like water﻿—the blood of the populace, always the blood of the populace. Now in Brittany we may see the like. And if in the end the new ideas prevail? If the seigneurial rule is overthrown, what then? You will have exchanged an aristocracy for a plutocracy. Is that worth while? Do you think that under money-changers and slave-traders and men who have waxed rich in other ways by the ignoble arts of buying and selling, the lot of the people will be any better than under their priests and nobles? Has it ever occurred to you, Philippe, what it is that makes the rule of the nobles so intolerable? Acquisitiveness. Acquisitiveness is the curse of mankind. And shall you expect less acquisitiveness in men who have built themselves up by acquisitiveness? Oh, I am ready to admit that the present government is execrable, unjust, tyrannical﻿—what you will; but I beg you to look ahead, and to see that the government for which it is aimed at exchanging it may be infinitely worse.”
Philippe sat thoughtful a moment. Then he returned to the attack.
“You do not speak of the abuses, the horrible, intolerable abuses of power under which we labour at present.”
“Where there is power there will always be the abuse of it.”
“Not if the tenure of power is dependent upon its equitable administration.”
“The tenure of power is power. We cannot dictate to those who hold it.”
“The people can﻿—the people in its might.”
“Again I ask you, when you say the people do you mean the populace? You do. What power can the populace wield? It can run wild. It can burn and slay for a time.

===Chinese===

贩奴商！靠着殖民地人口血肉的买卖活着、发着财的人，正在本土，打着自由的圣洁旗号，搞一场运动！你看不出来么，这整个运动，是一群小店主、生意人和让钱财撑肥了、眼红起纯凭血统的权柄的暴发户们搞的运动？巴黎那些握着国债债券的兑换商们，看着国家财政风雨飘摇，心惊胆战，生怕哪个大权独揽的人一举宣布破产、把国债勾销。为保住自己，他们正在地底下打洞，要拱翻一个国家，在废墟上另建一个自己做主人的新国家。为办成这一桩，他们煽动百姓。多菲内已经血流成河——流的是贱民的血，流的永远是贱民的血。如今布列塔尼怕也要见着同样的光景。倘若到头来新思想赢了呢？倘若领主统治被掀翻了呢？那又怎样？你们不过拿一个财主统治换掉一个贵族统治。这值得么？你以为在兑换商、贩奴商以及靠买卖这门下贱行当发起来的诸色人等手下，百姓的日子会比在他们的神父和贵族手下好过些？你可曾想过，菲利普，是什么叫贵族的统治这般难忍？是贪得无厌。贪得无厌是人类的诅咒。指望靠贪得无厌发起来的那拨人，贪得无厌会轻些么？哦，我随时承认，如今的政府恶劣、不公、暴虐（你说什么都行）；可我求你往前看：拿它去换的那个政府，可能坏到没有边。」
菲利普坐着出了一会儿神。随后他又攻上来。
「你不提那些弊端呢？眼下压得我们喘不过气的、骇人听闻、忍无可忍的滥权。」
「哪里有权，哪里就有滥权。」
「若权力的保有系于权力的公正行使，就不会。」
「权力的保有，靠的就是权力。我们指挥不动掌权的人。」
「百姓能（百姓以其力量。」
「我再问你一遍：你说百姓，指的可是贱民？正是。贱民能使得出什么力量？它能撒野。它能烧，能杀，逞凶一时。

===Original===

But enduring power it cannot wield, because power demands qualities which the populace does not possess, or it would not be populace. The inevitable, tragic corollary of civilization is populace. For the rest, abuses can be corrected by equity; and equity, if it is not found in the enlightened, is not to be found at all. M. Necker is to set about correcting abuses, and limiting privileges. That is decided. To that end the States General are to assemble.”
“And a promising beginning we have made in Brittany, as Heaven hears me!” cried Philippe.
“Pooh! That is nothing. Naturally the nobles will not yield without a struggle. It is a futile and ridiculous struggle﻿—but then﻿ ﻿… it is human nature, I suppose, to be futile and ridiculous.”
M. de Vilmorin became witheringly sarcastic. “Probably you will also qualify the shooting of Mabey as futile and ridiculous. I should even be prepared to hear you argue in defence of the Marquis de La Tour d’Azyr that his gamekeeper was merciful in shooting Mabey, since the alternative would have been a life-sentence to the galleys.”
André-Louis drank the remainder of his chocolate; set down his cup, and pushed back his chair, his breakfast done.
“I confess that I have not your big charity, my dear Philippe. I am touched by Mabey’s fate. But, having conquered the shock of this news to my emotions, I do not forget that, after all, Mabey was thieving when he met his death.”
M. de Vilmorin heaved himself up in his indignation.
“That is the point of view to be expected in one who is the assistant fiscal intendant of a nobleman, and the delegate of a nobleman to the States of Brittany.”
“Philippe, is that just? You are angry with me!” he cried, in real solicitude.
“I am hurt,” Vilmorin admitted. “I am deeply hurt by your attitude. And I am not alone in resenting your reactionary tendencies. Do you know that the Literary Chamber is seriously considering your expulsion?”
André-Louis shrugged. “That neither surprises nor troubles me.”
M. de Vilmorin swept on, passionately: “Sometimes I think that you have no heart. With you it is always the law, never equity. It occurs to me, André, that I was mistaken in coming to you. You are not likely to be of assistance to me in my interview with M. de Kercadiou.” He took up his hat, clearly with the intention of departing.
André-Louis sprang up and caught him by the arm.

===Chinese===

可持久的权力它握不住，因为权力要求的品质，贱民恰好没有——不然它也不成其为贱民了。文明的必然的、悲剧性的推论，就是贱民。至于其余，弊端可以拿公正来纠正；而公正，若不在开明人身上，便哪里都没有。内克尔先生就要着手纠正弊端、限制特权了。这是定了的。为着这个目的，三级会议就要召开。」
「咱们布列塔尼这个开头可真叫人乐观，老天作证！」菲利普喊道。
「呸！那算不得什么。贵族们自然不肯不作一番挣扎就交权。这是一场徒劳可笑的挣扎（不过话说回来……人之为人，大概就免不了徒劳可笑。」
维尔莫兰先生的讥诣刮起了白霜。「想必你还要把枪杀马贝也归作徒劳可笑。我简直准备听你替拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵辩护，说他的看守枪杀马贝还是慈悲呢，不然马贝的下场便是终身划桨的大牢。」
安德烈-路易喝完杯里剩下的巧克力，放下杯子，推回椅子，早饭用完了。
「我承认我没有你那副大慈悲，我亲爱的菲利普。马贝的命是动了我。可激动过去，我忘不了：马贝挨枪子儿的时候，到底是在偷东西。」
维尔莫兰先生带着一腔义惯直起身来。
「这也难怪，一位贵族的财务总管助理、贵族派往布列塔尼三级会议的代表，看事情自然是这个角度。」
「菲利普，这公道么？你在生我的气！」他喊，真心着慌了。
「我是伤心，」维尔莫兰承认，「你的态度叫我很伤心。怨你这份反动倾向的，也不止我一个。你可知道，文学会正认真考虑开除你？」
安德烈-路易耸耸肩。「这既不吃惊，也不烦心。」
维尔莫兰先生只顾慷慨激昂地说下去：「有时候我想，你是没有心的。你开口闭口是法，从来不是义。我想起来了，安德烈，我这趟找你，是找错了。我去见凯尔卡迪乌先生，你帮不上什么忙。」他拿起帽子，分明是要走了。
安德烈-路易一跃而起，拽住他的胳膊。

===Original===

“I vow,” said he, “that this is the last time ever I shall consent to talk law or politics with you, Philippe. I love you too well to quarrel with you over other men’s affairs.”
“But I make them my own,” Philippe insisted vehemently.
“Of course you do, and I love you for it. It is right that you should. You are to be a priest; and everybody’s business is a priest’s business. Whereas I am a lawyer﻿—the fiscal intendant of a nobleman, as you say﻿—and a lawyer’s business is the business of his client. That is the difference between us. Nevertheless, you are not going to shake me off.”
“But I tell you frankly, now that I come to think of it, that I should prefer you did not see M. de Kercadiou with me. Your duty to your client cannot be a help to me.”
His wrath had passed; but his determination remained firm, based upon the reason he gave.
“Very well,” said André-Louis. “It shall be as you please. But nothing shall prevent me at least from walking with you as far as the château, and waiting for you while you make your appeal to M. de Kercadiou.”
And so they left the house good friends, for the sweetness of M. de Vilmorin’s nature did not admit of rancour, and together they took their way up the steep main street of Gavrillac.

===Chinese===

「我起誓，」他说，「这是最后一回，我答应同你谈法律谈政治了，菲利普。我太爱你，不能为别人的事同你吵。」
「可我把它们当作我自己的事。」菲利普激烈地坚持。
「你自然这样，我也因此爱你。你这样是对的。你要做神父；人人份内的事都是神父的事。而我呢，是个律师（如你所说，一位贵族的财务总管），律师份内的事，是当事人的事。这便是你我之间的分别。不过，你可休想甩开我。」
「可我坦白说，这会儿细想起来，我倒宁可你不陪我见凯尔卡迪乌先生。你对当事人所尽的本分，帮不上我。」
他的火气过去了，决心却还铁着，凭的正是他说的那个缘故。
「很好，」安德烈-路易说，「依你。可至少陪我走到庄院门口，这你拦不住我；你同凯尔卡迪乌先生申诉的时候，我就在外头等。」
于是两人和和气气出了门（维尔莫兰先生天性那份甜，存不下怨恨（并肩走上加夫里雅克那条陡陡的主街。

## XI The Returning Carriage / 十一 归来的马车
===Original===

M. de Kercadiou wrote a letter.
“Godson,” he began, without any softening adjective, “I have learnt with pain and indignation that you have dishonoured yourself again by breaking the pledge you gave me to abstain from politics. With still greater pain and indignation do I learn that your name has become in a few short days a byword, that you have discarded the weapon of false, insidious arguments against my class﻿—the class to which you owe everything﻿—for the sword of the assassin. It has come to my knowledge that you have an assignation tomorrow with my good friend M. de La Tour d’Azyr. A gentleman of his station is under certain obligations imposed upon him by his birth, which do not permit him to draw back from an engagement. But you labour under no such disadvantages. For a man of your class to refuse an engagement of honour, or to neglect it when made, entails no sacrifice. Your peers will probably be of the opinion that you display a commendable prudence. Therefore I beg you, indeed, did I think that I still exercise over you any such authority as the favours you have received from me should entitle me to exercise, I would command you, to allow this matter to go no farther, and to refrain from rendering yourself to your assignation tomorrow morning. Having no such authority, as your past conduct now makes clear, having no reason to hope that a proper sentiment of gratitude to me will induce to give heed to this my most earnest request, I am compelled to add that should you survive tomorrow’s encounter, I can in no circumstances ever again permit myself to be conscious of your existence. If any spark survives of the affection that once you expressed for me, or if you set any value upon the affection, which, in spite of all that you have done to forfeit it, is the chief prompter of this letter, you will not refuse to do as I am asking.”
It was not a tactful letter. M. de Kercadiou was not a tactful man. Read it as he would, André-Louis﻿—when it was delivered to him on that Sunday afternoon by the groom dispatched with it into Paris﻿—could read into it only concern for M. La Tour d’Azyr, M. de Kercadiou’s good friend, as he called him, and prospective nephew-in-law.
He kept the groom waiting a full hour while composing his answer. Brief though it was, it cost him very considerable effort and several unsuccessful attempts.

===Chinese===

德·凯尔卡迪乌先生写了一封信。
「教子」，他这样开头，不带一个缓颊的字眼。「我得知你又背弃了对我立下的不问政治的保证，自玷其名，我痛心，也愤慨。而几天工夫你的名字竟成了骂人的口头禅，你丢开那套用来对付我的阶级——你的一切都是这个阶级给的——的虚伪阴险的议论，换上了刺客的剑，这叫我更痛心、更愤慨。我已经得知，你明天同我的好朋友德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生有个约会。他那样身分的绅士，生来便负有种种义务，不容他临阵退缩。你却不受这种累赘。你们那个阶级的人，拒绝一场荣誉之约，或者约了又借故不去，算不得什么牺牲。你的同侪多半还会说你有可嘉的谨慎。因此我请求你——说真的，倘若我以为我对你还行使得了那种权威（你受了我那么多恩惠，本该让我行使的权威），我就干脆命令你——到此为止，明早不要赴那个约会。既然我并没有这种权威，你的既往已把这层挑明了；既然我没有理由指望你对我还有一点正当的感激之心，肯听我这一番最恳切的请求，那我只好再添一句：倘若你明天活了下来，无论何种情形，我都决不容自己再意识到你的存在。倘若你对我从前表示过的情谊尚存一星半点，倘若你还把这份情谊当回事——尽管你种种作为都在把它精蹋，它却正是这封信的主要出发点——你就不会拒绝照我的话做。」
这不是一封得体的信。德·凯尔卡迪乌先生本也不是个得体的人。随安德烈-路易怎么读——信是礼拜天下晌由专差送进巴黎交到他手里的——他读来读去，只读出一件东西：对德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生的关心，对德·凯尔卡迪乌先生那位，用他自己的话说，好朋友、未来的内侄女婿的关心。
他把信差足足留了一个钟头，才把回信写好。信虽短，却费了他很大的劲，写废了好几回。

===Original===

In the end this is what he wrote:

> Monsieur my godfather,
> You make refusal singularly hard for me when you appeal to me upon the ground of affection. It is a thing of which all my life I shall hail the opportunity to give you proofs, and I am therefore desolated beyond anything I could hope to express that I cannot give you the proof you ask today. There is too much between M. de La Tour d’Azyr and me. Also you do me and my class﻿—whatever it may be﻿—less than justice when you say that obligations of honour are not binding upon us. So binding do I count them, that, if I would, I could not now draw back.
> If hereafter you should persist in the harsh intention you express, I must suffer it. That I shall suffer be assured.
> Your affectionate and grateful godson
> André-Louis

===Chinese===

末了他写出来的是这样一封：

> 教父先生：
> 您拿情谊来劝我，叫我实在难以回绝。这一生但有机会向您证明这份情谊，我无不欢迎；正因如此，今天不能给您要的这个证明，我的痛苦非言语所能表达。德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生同我之间，账太多了。再者，您说荣誉的义务约束不了我们——不管「我们」算哪个阶级——这也是看轻了我和我的阶级。恰恰相反，我把这义务看得那样重，如今即便想退，也退不成了。
> 倘若您日后仍旧坚持您信里说的那个严厉的决定，我只好承受。请您相信，我一定承受。
> 您情爱而感激的教子
> 安德烈-路易

===Original===

He dispatched that letter by M. de Kercadiou’s groom, and conceived this to be the end of the matter. It cut him keenly; but he bore the wound with that outward stoicism he affected.
Next morning, at a quarter past eight, as with Le Chapelier﻿—who had come to break his fast with him﻿—he was rising from table to set out for the Bois, his housekeeper startled him by announcing Mademoiselle de Kercadiou.
He looked at his watch. Although his cabriolet was already at the door, he had a few minutes to spare. He excused himself from Le Chapelier, and went briskly out to the anteroom.
She advanced to meet him, her manner eager, almost feverish.
“I will not affect ignorance of why you have come,” he said quickly, to make short work. “But time presses, and I warn you that only the most solid of reasons can be worth stating.”
It surprised her. It amounted to a rebuff at the very outset, before she had uttered a word; and that was the last thing she had expected from André-Louis. Moreover, there was about him an air of aloofness that was unusual where she was concerned, and his voice had been singularly cold and formal.
It wounded her. She was not to guess the conclusion to which he had leapt. He made with regard to her﻿—as was but natural, after all﻿—the same mistake that he had made with regard to yesterday’s letter from his godfather. He conceived that the mainspring of action here was solely concern for M. de La Tour d’Azyr. That it might be concern for himself never entered his mind. So absolute was his own conviction of what must be the inevitable issue of that meeting that he could not conceive of anyone entertaining a fear on his behalf.
What he assumed to be anxiety on the score of the predestined victim had irritated him in M. de Kercadiou; in Aline it filled him with a cold anger; he argued from it that she had hardly been frank with him; that ambition was urging her to consider with favour the suit of M. de La Tour d’Azyr. And than this there was no spur that could have driven more relentlessly in his purpose, since to save her was in his eyes almost as momentous as to avenge the past.
She conned him searchingly, and the complete calm of him at such a time amazed her. She could not repress the mention of it.
“How calm you are, André!”
“I am not easily disturbed. It is a vanity of mine.”
“But﻿ ﻿… Oh, André, this meeting must not take place!”

他托德·凯尔卡迪乌先生的信差把回信发走，心想这件事就算了结了。信割得他心头生疼；可他把这份伤摆在一层外表的淡漠底下，那是他惯装的斯多噶气派。
第二天早晨八点一刻，勒·夏普利耶——他过来同他一起用早饭——正陪着他起身离桌、准备动身去布洛涅，女管家进来通报德·凯尔卡迪乌小姐到，吓了他一跳。
他看了看表。双轮马车虽已候在门口，还来得及腾出几分钟。他向勒·夏普利耶告了罪，快步走到前厅。
她迎上前来，神色急切，近乎狂乱。
「我不装作不知道您为什么来，」他开门见山地说，「只是时间紧，我先提醒您，只有最站得住的理由，才值得说。」
这叫她意外。这等于她一个字还没说，开口先碰了个钉子；这是她从安德烈-路易那里最想不到的。何况他待她有一种素来没有的疏远，声气也冷得反常、客气得反常。
这伤了她的心。她猜不到他一跳就跳到的那个结论。他对她的看法——到底也是自然的——同他对教父那封信的看法犯的是同一个错误。他认定这一趟的主动机，又只是对德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生的关心。至于那可能是对他的关心，他压根儿没往那上头想。他对那场约斗的结局会如何，自己确信到那样绝对，以致想不出世上还有人会替他担忧。
这种——照他的理解——对那个注定的牺牲者的焦虑，在德·凯尔卡迪乌先生身上惹他恼；在阿琳身上，则叫他心里灌满一股冷怒；他由此断定她对他不够坦白，断定野心正在撺掇她青眼看待德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生的求婚。而这层没有哪根刺能比它更狠地鞭策他去赴约：因为在他眼里，救她几乎同报仇旧恨一样重大。
她细细打量他，他在这种时候竟然这样全然的镇定，叫她惊愕。她忍不住说了出来。
「你多镇定啊，安德烈！」
「我不容易乱。这是我的一个自负。」
「可是……哦，安德烈，这场约斗万万去不得！」

She came close up to him, to set her hands upon his shoulders, and stood so, her face within a foot of his own.
“You know, of course, of some good reason why it should not?” said he.
“You may be killed,” she answered him, and her eyes dilated as she spoke.
It was so far from anything that he had expected that for a moment he could only stare at her. Then he thought he had understood. He laughed as he removed her hands from his shoulders, and stepped back. This was a shallow device, childish and unworthy in her.
“Can you really think to prevail by attempting to frighten me?” he asked, and almost sneered.
“Oh, you are surely mad! M. de La Tour d’Azyr is reputed the most dangerous sword in France.”
“Have you never noticed that most reputations are undeserved? Chabrillane was a dangerous swordsman, and Chabrillane is underground. La Motte-Royau was an even more dangerous swordsman, and he is in a surgeon’s hands. So are the other spadassinicides who dreamt of skewering a poor sheep of a provincial lawyer. And here today comes the chief, the fine flower of these bully-swordsmen. He comes, for wages long overdue. Be sure of that. So if you have no other reason to urge﻿ ﻿…”
It was the sarcasm of him that mystified her. Could he possibly be sincere in his assurance that he must prevail against M. de La Tour d’Azyr? To her in her limited knowledge, her mind filled with her uncle’s contrary conviction, it seemed that André-Louis was only acting; he would act a part to the very end.
Be that as it might, she shifted her ground to answer him.
“You had my uncle’s letter?”
“And I answered it.”
“I know. But what he said, he will fulfil. Do not dream that he will relent if you carry out this horrible purpose.”
“Come, now, that is a better reason than the other,” said he. “If there is a reason in the world that could move me it would be that. But there is too much between La Tour d’Azyr and me. There is an oath I swore on the dead hand of Philippe de Vilmorin. I could never have hoped that God would afford me so great an opportunity of keeping it.”
“You have not kept it yet,” she warned him.
He smiled at her. “True!” he said. “But nine o’clock will soon be here. Tell me,” he asked her suddenly, “why did you not carry this request of yours to M. de La Tour d’Azyr?”
“I did,” she answered him, and flushed as she remembered her yesterday’s rejection.

她走近他，把双手搭上他的肩，就那样站着，脸离他的脸不到一尺。
「您当然知道什么像样的缘故，叫它去不得？」他说。
「您会被杀死的。」她答，说话时眼睛睁得大大的。
这同他预料的差得太远，一时间他只能怔怔看她。随后他以为自己懂了。他把她的手从肩上拿下来，退后一步，笑了。这是个浅拙的伎俩，孩子气，也配不上她。
「您真以为吓一吓就能叫我让步？」他问，几乎带出讥讽。
「哦，您准是疯了！德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生可是公认的法兰西最辣的剑。」
「您从没留意到，大多数名声都名不副实么？夏布里扬是危险的剑手，夏布里扬进了土。拉莫特-罗亚奥是更危险的剑手，他躺在外科医生手里。那几个做起梦要把一个外省小律师的可怜羔羊串上烤叉的剑客杀手，也都躺在那里。而今天来的是头儿，这帮恶霸剑手里的精华。他是来讨一笔拖欠已久的工钱的。这一点您放心。您若没有别的理由要讲……」
叫她如坠云里的，正是他这份刻薄。他必胜德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生的这份笃定，可能是真心么？在她那点有限的见识里，满脑子又是伯父的反面断定，安德烈-路易这似乎只是在做戏；他要一路把这出戏做到底了。
无论怎样，她换了阵地来答他。
「伯父的信你收到了？」
「回了。」
「我知道。可他说到做到。你别做梦以为你干出这桩可怕的事，他会心软。」
「瞧，这就比刚才那条像样了，」他说，「世上若还有一个能打动我的理由，就是那个。可拉图尔·达齐尔同我之间账太多了。我在菲利普·德·维尔莫兰那只死手上发过誓。我从没敢指望上帝会给我这样一个践约的大好机会。」
「誓你还没践呢。」她提醒他。
他朝她一笑。「不错！」他说，「可九点钟马上就到。告诉我，」他忽然问她，「您这番请求，怎么不去向德·拉图尔·达齐尔先生提？」
「提了。」她答，想起昨天碰的钉子，脸红了。

He interpreted the flush quite otherwise.

“So, so. He must, of course, whatever it may have cost him. Yet in his place I should have counted the cost as nothing. But men are different, you see.” He sighed. “Also in your place, had that been so, I think I should have left the matter there. But then﻿ ﻿…”
“I don’t understand you, André.”
“I am not so very obscure. Not nearly so obscure as I can be. Turn it over in your mind. It may help to comfort you presently.” He consulted his watch again. “Pray use this house as your own. I must be going.”
Le Chapelier put his head in at the door.
“Forgive the intrusion. But we shall be late, André, unless you﻿ ﻿…”
“Coming,” André answered him. “If you will await my return, Aline, you will oblige me deeply. Particularly in view of your uncle’s resolve.”
She did not answer him. She was numbed. He took her silence for assent, and, bowing, left her. Standing there she heard his steps going down the stairs together with Le Chapelier’s. He was speaking to his friend, and his voice was calm and normal.
Oh, he was mad﻿—blinded by self-confidence and vanity. As his carriage rattled away, she sat down limply, with a sense of exhaustion and nausea. She was sick and faint with horror. André-Louis was going to his death. Conviction of it﻿—an unreasoning conviction, the result, perhaps, of all M. de Kercadiou’s rantings﻿—entered her soul. Awhile she sat thus, paralyzed by hopelessness. Then she sprang up again, wringing her hands. She must do something to avert this horror. But what could she do? To follow him to the Bois and intervene there would be to make a scandal for no purpose. The conventions of conduct were all against her, offering a barrier that was not to be overstepped. Was there no one could help her?
Standing there, half-frenzied by her helplessness, she caught again a sound of vehicles and hooves on the cobbles of the street below. A carriage was approaching. It drew up with a clatter before the fencing-academy. Could it be André-Louis returning? Passionately she snatched at that straw of hope. Knocking, loud and urgent, fell upon the door. She heard André-Louis’ housekeeper, her wooden shoes clanking upon the stairs, hurrying down to open.

She sped to the door of the anteroom, and pulling it wide, stood breathlessly to listen. But the voice that floated up to her was not the voice she so desperately hoped to hear. It was a woman’s voice asking in urgent tones for M. André-Louis﻿—a voice at first vaguely familiar, then clearly recognized, the voice of Mme. de Plougastel.
Excited, she ran to the head of the narrow staircase in time to hear Mme. de Plougastel exclaim in agitation:
“He has gone already! Oh, but how long since? Which way did he take?”
It was enough to inform Aline that Mme. de Plougastel’s errand must be akin to her own. At the moment, in the general distress and confusion of her mind, her mental vision focused entirely on the one vital point, she found in this no matter for astonishment. The singular regard conceived by Mme. de Plougastel for André-Louis seemed to her then a sufficient explanation.
Without pausing to consider, she ran down that steep staircase, calling:
“Madame! Madame!”
The portly, comely housekeeper drew aside, and the two ladies faced each other on that threshold. Mme. de Plougastel looked white and haggard, a nameless dread staring from her eyes.
“Aline! You here!” she exclaimed. And then in the urgency sweeping aside all minor considerations, “Were you also too late?” she asked.
“No, madame. I saw him. I implored him. But he would not listen.”
“Oh, this is horrible!” Mme. de Plougastel shuddered as she spoke. “I heard of it only half an hour ago, and I came at once, to prevent it at all costs.”
The two women looked blankly, despairingly, at each other. In the sunshine-flooded street one or two shabby idlers were pausing to eye the handsome equipage with its magnificent bay horses, and the two great ladies on the doorstep of the fencing-academy. From across the way came the raucous voice of an itinerant bellows-mender raised in the cry of his trade:
“*A raccommoder les vieux soufflets!*”
Madame swung to the housekeeper.
“How long is it since monsieur left?”
“Ten minutes, maybe; hardly more.” Conceiving these great ladies to be friends of her invincible master’s latest victim, the good woman preserved a decently stolid exterior.
Madame wrung her hands. “Ten minutes! Oh!” It was almost a moan. “Which way did he go?”
“The assignation is for nine o’clock in the Bois de Boulogne,” Aline informed her. “Could we follow? Could we prevail if we did?”

===Original===

The question is should we come in time? At nine o’clock! And it wants but little more than a quarter of an hour. *Mon Dieu! Mon Dieu!*” Madame clasped and unclasped her hands in anguish. “Do you know, at least, where in the Bois they are to meet?”
“No﻿—only that it is in the Bois.”
“In the Bois!” Madame was flung into a frenzy. “The Bois is nearly half as large as Paris.” But she swept breathlessly on, “Come, Aline: get in, get in!”
Then to her coachman. “To the Bois de Boulogne by way of the Cours la Reine,” she commanded, “as fast as you can drive. There are ten pistoles for you if we are in time. Whip up, man!”
She thrust Aline into the carriage, and sprang after her with the energy of a girl. The heavy vehicle﻿—too heavy by far for this race with time﻿—was moving before she had taken her seat. Rocking and lurching it went, earning the maledictions of more than one pedestrian whom it narrowly avoided crushing against a wall or trampling underfoot.
Madame sat back with closed eyes and trembling lips. Her face showed very white and drawn. Aline watched her in silence. Almost it seemed to her that Mme. de Plougastel was suffering as deeply as herself, enduring an anguish of apprehension as great as her own.
Later Aline was to wonder at this. But at the moment all the thought of which her half-numbed mind was capable was bestowed upon their desperate errand.
The carriage rolled across the Place Louis XV and out on to the Cours la Reine at last. Along that beautiful, tree-bordered avenue between the Champs Élysées and the Seine, almost empty at this hour of the day, they made better speed, leaving now a cloud of dust behind them.
But fast to danger-point as was the speed, to the women in that carriage it was too slow. As they reached the barrier at the end of the Cours, nine o’clock was striking in the city behind them, and every stroke of it seemed to sound a note of doom.
Yet here at the barrier the regulations compelled a momentary halt. Aline enquired of the sergeant-in-charge how long it was since a cabriolet such as she described had gone that way. She was answered that some twenty minutes ago a vehicle had passed the barrier containing the deputy M. le Chapelier and the Paladin of the Third Estate, M. Moreau. The sergeant was very well informed. He could make a shrewd guess, he said, with a grin, of the business that took M.

这层阿琳日后还要诧异。可此刻，她那半麻的头脑里容得下的一点念头，全扑在她们这桩孤注一掷的使命上。
马车辚辚驶过路易十五广场，终于转上王后大道。沿着香榭丽舍与塞纳河之间那条美丽的林荫道——这个钟点几乎空无一人——他们走得快了些，身后扬起一长溜尘土。
可是无论车速怎样逼到危险的程度，对车中这两位妇人来说都嫌太慢。抵达大道尽头的关卡时，城里身后的钟正敲九点，每一下都像敲了一声丧钟。
然而在关卡这里，规矩逼得她们略停一停。阿琳向把卡的军士打听：她描述的那种双轮马车，多久前过的卡。回答是约莫二十分钟前，有一辆车过卡，车里是议员勒·夏普利耶先生和第三等级的游侠骑士莫罗先生。军士消息灵通得很。他咧嘴说，他自问能猜个八九不离十，是什么营生一清早把莫

===Original===

Moreau that way so early in the day.
They left him, to speed on now through the open country, following the road that continued to hug the river. They sat back mutely despairing, staring hopelessly ahead, Aline’s hand clasped tight in madame’s. In the distance, across the meadows on their right, they could see already the long, dusky line of trees of the Bois, and presently the carriage swung aside following a branch of the road that turned to the right, away from the river and heading straight for the forest.
Mademoiselle broke at last the silence of hopelessness that had reigned between them since they had passed the barrier.
“Oh, it is impossible that we should come in time! Impossible!”
“Don’t say it! Don’t say it!” madame cried out.
“But it is long past nine, madame! André would be punctual, and these﻿ ﻿… affairs do not take long. It﻿ ﻿… it will be all over by now.”
Madame shivered, and closed her eyes. Presently, however, she opened them again, and stirred. Then she put her head from the window. “A carriage is approaching,” she announced, and her tone conveyed the thing she feared.
“Not already! Oh, not already!” Thus Aline expressed the silently communicated thought. She experienced a difficulty in breathing, felt the sudden need of air. Something in her throat was throbbing as if it would suffocate her; a mist came and went before her eyes.
In a cloud of dust an open caleche was speeding towards them, coming from the Bois. They watched it, both pale, neither venturing to speak, Aline, indeed, without breath to do so.
As it approached, it slowed down, perforce, as they did, to effect a safe passage in that narrow road. Aline was at the window with Mme. de Plougastel, and with fearful eyes both looked into this open carriage that was drawing abreast of them.
“Which of them is it, madame? Oh, which of them?” gasped Aline, scarce daring to look, her senses swimming.
On the near side sat a swarthy young gentleman unknown to either of the ladies. He was smiling as he spoke to his companion. A moment later and the man sitting beyond came into view. He was not smiling. His face was white and set, and it was the face of the Marquis de La Tour d’Azyr.
For a long moment, in speechless horror, both women stared at him, until, perceiving them, blankest surprise invaded his stern face.

罗先生引到那边去的。

小姐终于打破了过卡以来两人之间那阵绝望的沉默。
「哦，咱们赶不上了！赶不上了！」
「别说这话！别说这话！」夫人喊出声来。
「可早过九点了，夫人！安德烈是守时的人，而这种事……费不了多少工夫。这会儿……这会儿怕已经完了。」
夫人打了个寒噤，闭上了眼。不过片刻她又睁开，动了动，把头探出窗外。「有一辆马车过来了。」她说，声气里透着她怕的那个东西。
「别已经完了！哦，别已经完了！」阿琳这样喊出两人心照不宣的那个念头。她觉得气闷，忽然需要空气。喉咙里有什么东西在跳，像要憋死她；眼前一阵阵地发雾。
一溜尘土里，一辆敞篷四轮马车正对着她们驶来，从布洛涅那边来。两人望着它，都脸色发白，谁也不敢开口，阿琳更是连开口的气都没有。
两车相近，为在那窄路上安全错车，双方都慢了下来。阿琳同普卢加斯特尔夫人一起倚在窗口，两双恐惧的眼睛望着那辆渐渐并到身侧的敞篷车。
「是哪一位，夫人？哦，是哪一位？」阿琳喘着气问，眼睛几乎不敢看，神志发晕。
近侧坐着一位皮肤黝黑的年轻绅士，两位夫人都不认识。他正笑着同他的同伴说话。过了一刻，坐在那一位外侧的男人进入眼帘。他没有笑。他的脸发白、僵着，而那是拉图尔·达齐尔侯爵的脸。
两个女人一时惊得说不出话，直瞪着他；直到他望见她们，那张严峻的脸上也漫过一片空前的惊愕。
